One bit if simple.

More if hidden damage appeared.

Not a quote from me.

Not permission from Mera.

"Do you start without her?"

"No."

Even better.

"Then I need Mera."

"Or Cala can send word."

The cooper's shopkeeper said, "Mera's at egg row until ten."

I looked at him.

He shrugged.

Bread, cousins, and people in lanes.

The communications system remained undefeated.

I stood.

Right foot under.

One hand on curb.

Crutches set.

No issue.

"I can find her."

Olan said, "You don't have to."

That stopped me.

He was right.

I had delivered the basket.

My promised scope was complete.

Mera could receive word through Cala.

Olan could wait.

Lyssa could still choose to carry her own cloth if timing failed.

The city would survive without Greg becoming a message service because a basket had one loose handle.

Then the cooper's shopkeeper said, "Egg row is on your way back."

Damn geography.

"That is manipulative."

"Still true."

I considered.

Going home by egg row added almost nothing.

The message was bounded.

One bit if simple wrap and set. More if rim opens. Olan will not start without approval.

No analysis required.

No purchase required.

No magical expertise required.

"Fine," I said. "I will tell her."

Olan returned to the latch.

"Tell her I said quick means quick, not pretty."

"That sounds like a threat."

"She knows."

I left the basket with him.

Without it, my body narrowed back to its usual unreasonable dimensions.

The leather shoulder bag sat where it belonged.

Crutches moved cleanly.

Egg row was three turns north.

I knew it by smell too.

Not eggs.

Straw.

Feathers.

Warm grain.

The eggs themselves smelled like commerce, which mostly meant people arguing.

Mera turned out to be a broad woman with a red scarf and three shallow trays of eggs in front of her.

I recognized her only after she recognized me.

"Basket?"

Useful opening.

"With Olan. Handle attachment pulled. He says one bit if it is simple wrap and set. More if the rim opens when he pulls the old binding. He will not start without your approval. Quick means quick, not pretty."

Mera closed her eyes briefly.

"He said that?"

"Exactly enough that I resent remembering it."

She looked at the sun.

"One bit. If more, leave it."

"I can carry that message."

She reached under the stall cloth.

I raised a hand.

"Do not give me the bit."

She paused.

"Why?"

"Because then I become responsible for money I do not need to touch. Tell him you pay when you collect."

Mera considered this.

"Fine."

Then she picked up two eggs.

"Take."

"No."

"For walking."

"Eggs are famously bad walking equipment."

"For carrying message."

Ah.

Payment.

Not requested.

Not copper.

Two eggs.

I looked at them.

Then at her.

"Are these mine or Cala's?"

"Yours."

"That distinction has saved households."

I accepted the eggs.

Now I had two crutches, one shoulder bag, and two eggs.

The universe disliked stable systems.

I opened the leather bag.

Charcoal and narrow scrap occupied almost no room.

The eggs would roll.

Bad.

Mera gave me a twist of straw without comment.

I nested the eggs in it and placed them carefully in the bag.

The stay-bead notch held the strap where it had been set since I bought the bag.

I felt no magical event.

I did feel two fragile objects become my responsibility.

This was worse.

"If I arrive with one egg, do I owe you half a message?"

Mera said, "Go."

Carrow's women had formed a conspiracy around that word.

Back at the cooper's lane, Olan had finished the latch.

He was sitting on an upturned block with the basket between his knees.

He had not opened the binding.

Good.

"Mera approves one bit if simple. If more, leave it. She pays when she collects."

Olan nodded.

Then he began.

I could have left.

I almost did.

Then he pulled the first brown wrap free and the rim did not open.

That answered the condition immediately.

Simple, apparently.

I stayed because the repair would take minutes and because I wanted to see how the carry tie sat inside the handle foot.

That was curiosity, not ownership.

There was a difference.

Olan did not explain until I asked something worth answering.

He unwound three turns of old binding.

The green thread lay beneath, flattened against the basket rim.

One section had gone gray where the handle foot had worked loose.

"Gray means weak?" I asked.

"Sometimes dirty."

Of course.

He rubbed it between finger and thumb.

A faint green color returned under the grime.

"This is dirty."

"Useful system."

"Wash hands."

"Revolutionary."

He ignored me.

He reset the handle foot into the basket wall, not in the exact old holes. One reed over.

"Why move it?"

"Old holes widened."

Simple.

He threaded the existing green loop around the reset foot.

No new magical material.

Then he used fresh brown binding from his pouch to wrap the handle foot tightly into the rim.

Around.

Through.

Around again.

He wet the end with his tongue.

"High craft."

"Quiet."

He tucked it.

Then he pressed two fingers against the pale green knot.

A dull green pulse moved through the thread.

One.

Brief.

The basket rim tightened almost invisibly around the handle foot.

No glow remained.

Olan waited.

I noticed that.

Not because waiting after set pulses was new to me.

Because people who knew their work kept doing it.

After a few breaths he lifted the basket by both handles.

Then he looked into the cooper's shop.

"Water."

The shopkeeper pointed at a bucket.

"Half."

Olan put the half bucket into the basket.

The basket sagged.

Not much.

The repaired handle held.

He lifted it.

Set it down.

Lifted again.

"Feel?" he asked.

I blinked.

"You want me to carry it?"

"One step. Both handles."

This was not my magic.

Not my repair.

Not dangerous by the evidence in front of me.

I set both crutches against the wall within reach and stood on the right leg with the left knee clear.

Then I took the two basket handles.

The half bucket had weight.

Obviously.

But the basket did not drag hard toward one side the way I expected from the repaired foot. The pull felt broader through both handles, less concentrated in the fingers nearest the repair.

Not lighter.

Better distributed.

I set it down after one controlled lift.

"That the tie?"

Olan said, "Partly basket. Partly tie."

Good.

"Can you tell which?"

"Not without another basket and more time."

Better.

I reclaimed my crutches.

"Then it holds."

"For Mera's use, yes."

"Not stone."

"Why would she carry stone?"

"I asked that this morning."

Olan stared at me.

I left before the conversation became circular.

By the time I reached home, both eggs remained eggs.

This felt like an achievement worth hiding.

Cala was at the table.

Lyssa had pale cloth folded beside her and dark thread across one finger.

I put the two eggs down.

Cala looked at them.

"Mera."

"Payment for message carrying. Mine, apparently."

"One yours," Cala said. "One house."

I considered the legal structure.

"Accepted."

Lyssa looked up.

"Basket?"

"Olan repaired it. One bit if simple. It was simple. Mera approved before he started. She pays on collection."

"By noon?"

"Yes."

Lyssa nodded and went back to her work.

That was the whole effect on her business.

No Greg delivery route.

No partnership.

No optimization meeting.

Her cloth would go because the person she had already arranged to carry it would have her basket back.

I sat.

Cala cracked the house egg into a bowl.

My egg remained on the table.

Tavi appeared.

I covered it with one hand.

"No."

"Mine."

"You have a concerning theory of property."

"Door red."

"Denied."

He wandered away.

I looked at the narrow scrap in my bag.

There was room below the magic numbers.

I did not write the basket repair.

Not because it had been uninteresting.

It had been useful.

Old carry tie.

Attachment failure first.

Existing magical thread still serviceable.

Handle foot moved one reed over because old holes had widened.

Fresh ordinary binding.

One set pulse.

Load test.

One bit if simple.

All clean facts.

But they belonged to a repair I had watched, not a problem I needed to solve.

I could remember enough.

More importantly, I had carried the basket to Olan, carried one message to Mera, carried one approval back, and stopped.

The two eggs were the only thing the work had turned into for me.

One of those had already become household property by treaty.

I picked up the remaining egg.

Yesterday I had moved a paper square at near and failed to move it at middle and far.

Today I had moved a broken basket through three lanes without breaking the other handle.

The second achievement was less magical.

It had also paid better in breakfast.

I put the egg beside Cala's bowl.

"House can have both."

Cala said, "Why?"

"I am investing in soup."

"Egg."

"A diversified portfolio."

She shook her head and cracked it.

The three copper stayed upstairs.

The three clean sheets stayed clean.

My bag had carried charcoal, notes, two eggs, and no new profession.

By noon, Mera's basket was gone from Olan's lane.

I knew because I did not go check.

Lyssa's finished cloth disappeared from our table shortly after.

I knew because Mera came to the door, said, "Got it," took the wrapped pieces, and left with the repaired basket hanging from both hands.

The handle held.

The old green knot showed no light at all.

It did not need to.

Some magic announced itself.

Some magic made a basket slightly less unpleasant to carry.

And sometimes the useful thing was simply knowing which handle was yours.

# CHAPTER 300

## THE BLUE HORSE

After Mera left with Lyssa's cloth, Tavi discovered that the room was too small for his ambitions.

He crossed it six times in under a minute, first as himself, then apparently as something with more knees. On the seventh pass he struck the table with one palm, shouted, "Horse up," and turned around so quickly that his shoulder clipped the stool.

The stool survived.

Tavi did too.

Cala watched him from beside the basin.

Lyssa had already gone back upstairs with the brown cuff and the kind of face that said the next person to ask what she was doing would become part of the garment.

I was innocent only because I had learned.

Tavi ran past again.

Cala said, "Take him bench court."

I looked at her.

Then at Tavi.

Then back at her.

"You are giving me responsibility for a moving person."

"Yes."

"He has no handles."

"Greg."

Tavi slapped the table again.

"Horse up."

Cala pointed toward the door. "Bench court. Not market. Sit by gate."

That was the whole assignment.

I knew the court. Two quiet lanes over, enclosed on three sides by old brick and on the fourth by a low iron gate that had not closed properly in years. There were benches along the walls, a rain tree in the middle, and enough packed earth for children to become exhausted without immediately joining cart traffic.

The gate mattered.

With two crutches, I could not hold Tavi's hand and move normally at the same time. I could manage a few steps one-handed if I had to, but turning an ordinary walk into a balancing exercise while supervising a child seemed like a poor use of everyone involved.

Bench court solved most of that by geometry.

Cala had apparently reached the same conclusion before I finished thinking.

"Wall side," she told Tavi.

He stopped moving.

That was more impressive than anything I had done at the Guild yesterday.

"Wall side," he repeated.

"With Greg. Stop corners."

Tavi nodded with the solemnity of a man accepting military command.

I did not trust it for a second.

Cala handed him a small piece of bread.

He ate half before we reached the door.

The other half became my concern when he tried to put it in my shoulder bag.

"No."

"Bag."

"Bread does not travel with charcoal."

He considered this and put the bread in his mouth instead.

Problem solved by consumption.

The front door opened cleanly. Tavi stepped out first, remembered something, then moved to the wall side without being told again.

I looked back at Cala.

She had already returned to the basin.

Apparently we had passed inspection.

The first lane was narrow enough that Tavi could walk along the building edge while I took the center. I kept my pace slow. He kept his for almost twelve seconds.

Then he saw a beetle.

"Bug."

"Yes."

He crouched.

I stopped.

The beetle was ordinary, black, and uninterested in becoming educational. It crossed a crack and disappeared beneath a step.

Tavi stood.

We resumed.

At the first corner I said, "Stop."

He stopped.

This was suspicious.

I checked both directions, crossed first enough to occupy the lane, then called him through beside the wall. No carts. No running. No drama.

At the second corner he stopped before I spoke.

I stared at him.

"Who trained you?"

"Cala."

Of course.

Bench court was already loud when we arrived.

Not dangerously loud. Child loud.

Seven or eight children occupied the dirt under the rain tree. Two were drawing with chalk near the wall. One was dragging a wooden cart with nothing in it. A pair of older girls had a skipping cord stretched between them. Three boys were arguing about whether a stick was a sword, a fishing rod, or somebody's dead uncle.

None of those interested Tavi.

He saw the horse.

It was blue, roughly four feet long from nose to tail, and flying above the court.

"Horse up!"

There it was.

The kite had been painted by someone who understood horses only in the broadest moral sense. Four legs. Long head. Tail. Blue body. One eye much larger than the other. Its wings were not anatomically defensible because horses did not have wings, but the painter had apparently refused to let that stop them.

A thin main line ran down to a boy near the tree. He looked ten or eleven and had wrapped the spool cord twice around one hand despite an older woman on the bench repeatedly telling him not to.

The kite climbed another few feet.

A small brass lift bead sat just below the first cross spar. It flashed pale gold whenever the nose dipped too far, helping the cloth catch enough air to recover. Cheap children's assist, not flight magic. I had seen better versions on survey kites, messenger rigs, and camp signals in my first life. This one was old enough that its brass had gone almost brown.

Above the court, four short posts at the roof corners held a faint amber drift web. Most of the time it was invisible. When the blue horse rose near the edge, the web showed itself as a brief honey-colored line and nudged the kite inward.

I knew that kind of boundary too.

Weak deflection. Low force. Enough to keep toys out of chimneys and neighboring windows without trying to stop birds, rain, or anything heavier than the court was meant to throw into the air.

Tavi did not care how it worked.

"Horse fly."

"Technically, yes."

He looked at me.

I corrected myself.

"Horse fly."

Satisfied, he walked toward it.

I moved to the bench beside the gate.

Cala's instruction had not been decorative.

From there I could see the whole court, and more importantly, Tavi could not leave without passing within three feet of me. I rested the crutch tips against the packed earth and sat with my left knee clear, right foot planted, bag against my ribs.

The older woman on the other end of the bench glanced at me.

"Cala's?"

"Temporarily."

She nodded as if that answer made complete sense.

Carrow had stopped asking me to explain certain things.

Tavi stood beneath the horse with his head tipped back.

The boy with the spool saw him.

"Want?"

Tavi reached both hands up.

The older woman said, "Not line. Tail."

The boy rolled his eyes.

She said his name.

"Ren."

The eye roll disappeared.

"Tail," Ren said.

He called another child over to hold the spool while he lowered the kite. The blue horse came down in a sequence of ugly sideways hops, one wing folding, then opening again when the lift bead flashed.

Tavi laughed so hard he had to put both hands on his knees.

Ren caught the lower tail cord before the kite touched dirt.

It had a second loop about three feet from the cloth, plain red string with no magic in it.

"Hold here," he told Tavi.

Tavi grabbed it.

The kite pulled.

His eyes widened.

He did not let go.

"Horse strong."

"Wind strong," Ren said.

Tavi looked at the horse instead.

Ren looked at me.

I shrugged.

Some arguments were lost before they began.

The main line went back out. Ren kept both hands on the spool now, perhaps because the older woman remained within hearing range. Tavi held only the tail loop. The horse rose until the tail cord pulled lightly against his hands.

He walked backward.

"Stay dirt," I said.

He stopped before reaching the chalk drawings.

Good.

The kite dipped.

The brass bead flashed.

The nose lifted again.

Tavi shouted, "Again!"

Ren said, "Wind does it."

"Again."

Ren gave up on pedagogy.

For several minutes nothing happened except a child holding a red loop while a bad blue horse moved through the air.

It was enough.

The court had its own rhythm. Chalk scraped brick. The empty cart lost a wheel and was immediately promoted from cart to boat. One of the skipping girls accused the other of cheating at a game whose rules appeared to change every six seconds. The older woman beside me had a basket of pea pods in her lap and shelled them without watching her hands.

I knew her face vaguely from the lanes but not her name.

She knew mine.

"Greg."

I looked over.

"Yes."

She pointed a pea pod at my crutches. "Gate is good."

It took me a second.

Then I understood.

"Because I cannot chase him."

She shrugged. "Nobody should chase him. Fast."

That was fair.

"Cala said sit here."

"Cala knows."

Apparently the household had outsourced my childcare strategy to architecture and community memory.

Efficient.

The woman split another pod.

"I'm Vara."

"Good to finally know what to call the person evaluating my gate position."

"Sit better."

I looked down.

My right crutch had drifted just far enough that its upper grip projected into the walking path beside the bench.

I pulled it closer.

"Fair."

Vara returned to peas.

No lecture.

No praise.

Useful correction completed.

The blue horse touched the amber drift web.

This time the contact happened at a corner where the wind was stronger. A honey-colored curve flashed above the roofline. The kite slid along it instead of turning cleanly, tail sweeping sideways.

Ren swore.

Vara said, "Language."

Ren said, "It stuck."

"Pull down, not back."

He pulled back.

The horse flattened against the invisible boundary for half a second.

Vara did not stand.

"Down."

Ren changed the angle.

The line lowered.

The kite peeled free and dropped into the court.

The lift bead flashed twice trying to catch air.

One wing clipped the rain tree.

The main line went slack.

The spool jumped from Ren's hand.

That got my attention.

Not because the kite mattered.

Because the spool hit dirt and rolled directly toward the gate.

Tavi saw it too.

His body leaned forward.

"Stay," I said.

He froze.

The spool rolled past him.

I planted my right crutch tip on the loose line instead of the spool itself.

The cord stopped feeding.

The spool rolled another foot and tipped over.

Ren ran to it.

"Got it," he said.

"I have the line."

He picked up the spool and took tension again.

I lifted my crutch tip.

Nothing dramatic had happened.

The horse remained caught in the lower branches of the rain tree, hanging sideways with one blue leg over a twig.

Tavi pointed.

"Horse tree."

Vara said, "Now it is."

Ren looked up at the kite.

Then at the tree.

Then at Vara.

"Can you?"

"No."

"You have the pole."

I had not noticed the pole under the bench until then.

Vara looked at him.

"My peas."

Ren looked at me.

"No."

He had not asked yet.

"You also have a pole," he said, looking at one of my crutches.

"This pole is occupied."

Vara laughed.

Ren did not.

He tried pulling the kite free.

The cloth tightened around the twig.

Vara said, "Stop pulling."

This time he stopped.

A girl near the chalk wall came over carrying a forked branch longer than she was tall.

"Use this."

Ren looked at it.

"Where did you get that?"

"Tree."

"Which tree?"

She pointed at the same rain tree.

A closed information loop.

Ren took the branch. The girl guided the fork under the tail cord while he kept the main line loose. Together they lifted the cloth over the twig and let it slide free.

The blue horse dropped.

Tavi caught the tail against his chest.

Not elegantly.

But successfully.

He looked stunned.

Ren looked more stunned.

"Don't squeeze it."

Tavi immediately squeezed it.

"Tavi," I said.

He loosened his arms.

The cloth was fine.

One painted wing had a smear of tree dust across it.

Ren checked the brass bead.

Vara finally set the peas aside and stood.

She took the kite from Tavi, turned the bead between two fingers, and looked at the cross spar.

"Bead fine. Knot slipped."

I could see it once she pointed.

The small cord loop holding the bead below the spar had shifted sideways during the snag.

Ren reached for it.

Vara slapped his hand lightly away.

"You pull everything."

That sounded familiar enough to be dangerous.

She loosened the cord, centered the bead, and retied the little stop knot with two turns instead of one.

I knew the broad knot family. I also knew better than to offer commentary while somebody who had been managing neighborhood children and their flying equipment longer than I had lived in this body was fixing it.

Ren watched.

"Two?"

"Today two. Wind west."

"Yesterday one."

"Yesterday not west."

There was the local practice.

Not a universal kite law.

Not a magical principle.

Vara's answer was just enough for the problem in front of her.

She handed the kite back.

Ren relaunched it from the center of the court.

The first attempt failed because Tavi would not release the tail.

The second failed because the empty cart, now apparently a boat, crossed directly through Ren's run.

The third worked.

The blue horse climbed.

The bead flashed once, steadied, and remained dull.

Tavi clapped.

"Horse up."

Ren handed him the red tail loop again.

This time Tavi stayed in one place without my telling him.

I sat by the gate and watched.

No one needed me for the kite.

That was pleasant.

No one needed me for the children either, beyond the one Cala had specifically given me. That was more than enough.

Vara resumed shelling peas.

"He listens."

I looked at Tavi.

"Sometimes."

"To Cala."

"Ah. Then yes."

She smiled.

We sat without talking for a while.

I had spent much of my first life around magic that announced itself through scale. Wards across valleys. Flying platforms. Battle fields. Healing arrays bright enough to turn night into afternoon. Things built to make powerful people feel appropriately powerful.

The court's magic did not care about any of that.

A tired brass bead helped a cloth horse keep its nose in the wind. Four roof posts nudged bad decisions away from chimneys. Neither system would impress anyone who had ever watched a war mage work.

The children would have been furious if either stopped working.

That was probably a better measure of usefulness.

I did not write that down.

Tavi eventually released the tail loop on his own and wandered toward the chalk wall.

He chose a piece of blue chalk.

Of course.

The wall already held several drawings layered over one another: circles, houses, people, three suns, something labeled CAT that looked like a chair, and a green shape with far too many teeth.

Tavi drew one vertical line.

Then another.

Then a long horizontal one connecting them.

He looked at me.

"Horse."

I considered the evidence.

"Strong start."

He added a circle nowhere near the rest of the drawing.

"Horse."

"Improving."

Vara said, "Liar."

"Supportive supervision."

"Liar."

Tavi did not care.

He drew until his fingers were blue.

When the sun moved far enough that the gate shadow reached my right foot, I decided we had been out long enough.

Standing from the bench was easy. Right foot under, left knee clear, one hand on the bench edge, both crutches set.

I called, "Tavi. Home."

He said, "No."

There it was.

The suspicious competence had expired.

"Home."

"Horse."

"Horse belongs to Ren."

Ren, still flying it, nodded vigorously.

Tavi looked betrayed by property law.

Vara said, "Tomorrow horse."

Ren looked at her.

She raised one eyebrow.

He looked back at Tavi.

"Maybe."

Good. No appointments for children either.

Tavi dragged his feet to the gate.

Then stopped at the edge without being told.

I looked at him.

He looked at me.

"Stop corner."

"Yes."

We walked home wall side.

At the first turn, he tried to explain the entire afternoon using only the words horse, tree, up, Ren, and Greg.

I understood perhaps sixty percent.

At the second corner, he stopped early again.

By the time we reached our door, the story had changed enough that I was apparently responsible for putting the horse in the tree.

"False," I told him.

He pointed at my crutch.

"Greg pole."

"Also false. Ren proposed that. I rejected it."

Cala opened the door before we finished litigating.

She looked at Tavi first.

Then at me.

"Run?"

"Only emotionally."

"Greg."

"No. He stayed in the court. Stopped at corners."

Cala nodded.

That was the report she wanted.

Tavi pushed past her.

"Horse up! Horse tree! Greg pole!"

Lyssa called from upstairs, "What did you do?"

"Nothing!"

Tavi shouted, "Greg horse tree!"

I closed my eyes.

Cala walked away laughing.

I followed Tavi into the room and put my bag on its peg.

Three private copper remained upstairs. Three clean sheets remained clean. My body felt fine. My crutches had prevented one spool from escaping and had not become a kite-retrieval system despite Ren's best efforts.

Lyssa came down with the brown cuff folded over one arm.

She looked at Tavi's blue fingers.

Then at me.

"Nothing?"

"I supervised."

"Blue."

"Chalk."

"Horse tree."

"Wind."

"Greg pole."

"Slander."

She smiled and went to wash Tavi's hands.

I sat at the table.

Outside, somewhere two lanes over, a child yelled, and a moment later somebody else yelled back.

No bells rang. No Guild note arrived. No client knocked. Chapter three hundred, if the universe was counting, passed without noticing itself.

The only thing that had flown was a badly painted blue horse.

That seemed sufficient.

# CHAPTER 301

## THE FALSE DOOR

The theatre sent paper instead of Lenn.

I considered this an improvement until I read it.

The folded strip had arrived with a boy I did not know, who handed it to Cala, accepted half a heel of bread for reasons unrelated to me, and vanished before I could ask whether he worked for the theatre or had simply been captured by Rinna on the road.

The note said:

`AFTER MIDDAY.`

`TWO BITS.`

`TEREN NEEDS A REPEATABLE BODY.`

`RINNA.`

I read the third line twice.

Cala was sorting dried beans at the table.

"I have been reduced to a repeatable body."

"Good."

"You did not hear the insult."

"Two bits."

"You heard the money."

"Yes."

Tavi was on the floor with a wooden spoon and a square of scrap cloth he had apparently promoted to horse.

He looked up.

"Greg pole."

"The historical record is hostile."

Cala put another bad bean into a small side bowl.

"Go after food."

That part of Rinna's note had been implied by survival.

Lyssa had already left that morning. The brown cuff was gone from the table with her, which told me only that she had taken the brown cuff somewhere. I did not convert absence into a customer theory.

I ate.

The three private copper remained upstairs. I left them there. The shoulder bag carried charcoal, the narrow scrap with the magic numbers, and nothing that would become embarrassing if the theatre asked me to run.

The walk was ordinary.

My hands felt good. Right shoe flat. Residual limb comfortable. Crutches quiet. The front door closed softly behind me and the rain shelf above the gallery remained a shelf instead of a narrative demand.

I crossed the repaired ward step without looking at it long enough to turn it into a reunion.

That was becoming a skill.

The theatre yard felt almost offensively familiar after the gap.

A narrow scenery cart blocked half the passage. Someone had left two rolled carpets against the wall. A woman carrying costume sleeves told a man with a hammer that he had stolen her chair. The man said the chair had been under a ladder. She said that did not make it his. He said he had improved it by sitting in it.

Nobody asked my opinion.

Home.

Rinna was at the side table with a stack of slips under one hand and three bits in a row beside them.

She looked at me, then at the clock board on the wall.

"On time."

"I was promised two bits."

"After."

"I respect your commitment to suspense."

"Stage. Teren."

"What am I repeating?"

"Your body."

"Still rude."

Rinna had already gone back to the slips.

Conversation concluded.

The stage was open to work light, curtain raised, house empty.

Teren stood in the middle of it with Pavin, Nessa, and Jori.

That combination made more sense than I wanted it to.

Pavin had pages rolled in one hand. Nessa had a length of black cloth over one shoulder and chalk tucked behind one ear. Jori was kneeling beside a freestanding doorframe that had no door in it.

Behind the frame hung a pale gray cloth stretched from floor to a narrow crossbar.

It was only six feet behind the frame.

From the stage, that was obvious.

The cloth had faint silver lines running through it in long perspective angles, all narrowing toward a point slightly left of center. Two low hooded lamps sat behind side screens. A third lamp, smaller, rested on a box farther back.

I knew the broad trick.

Depth cloth.

Cheap stages, roadside houses, temporary halls, sometimes even military briefings when someone wanted a command tent to look larger than it was. A treated surface took guided light unevenly, carrying some lines forward and letting others sink. Paint and geometry did the rest.

Good versions could make six feet feel like thirty from the right audience angle.

Bad versions made six feet look like six feet with expensive confidence.

I did not know which version this was.

Teren saw me looking.

"Good."

"Rinna called me repeatable."

"You are."

"This theatre has become crueler while I was gone."

Pavin unrolled his pages.

"You were gone?"

"Apparently not emotionally."

Nessa pointed to a chalk mark near the false doorway.

"Bag off before you cross."

I looked at her.

"Why?"

"Strap catches the black edge."

That was enough.

I put the shoulder bag on a chair against the side wall.

Jori stood and stepped back from the frame.

"We're testing the hall."

"It currently appears to end in cloth."

"From here."

"Good distinction."

Teren handed me four pages.

"Pavin is Serrin. You're servant and Corven. Same scene as before, cut shorter."

The Blue Window.

I remembered enough to dislike Corven on principle and enough not to trust memory with lines.

I scanned the pages.

The scene was not one we had run in the small room. Serrin crossed from a real room into a hallway while Corven continued speaking from behind him. In the old staging, the hallway was apparently just an exit behind a flat.

Now it had become a false corridor visible through an open frame.

"Why change it?"

Teren said, "Because the old exit makes Serrin disappear before the last line lands."

Pavin said, "And because Rinna found the cloth."

Nessa said, "Rinna found a price on the cloth."

Jori said, "Same thing to Rinna."

Teren ignored all three.

"You read opposite Pavin. On the last section, you cross through the frame, turn left behind the veil, then come back. Same path each time."

"Am I the servant or Corven when I cross?"

"Body."

I looked at him.

"Rinna infected you."

"Read first."

We read standing near the table.

Pavin had improved since the last time I had been used as his scene furniture.

Or I had learned enough to notice different things.

He did not rush the lie about the letter anymore. He let Corven interrupt him once, then took the next line too quickly, as if Serrin regretted allowing space.

I read Corven in my own voice.

Not flat.

Not a performance.

Just enough pressure to give Pavin something to push against.

Teren stopped us twice.

The first time because I leaned into Corven's accusation.

"Less."

"I am barely doing anything."

"Do less of the barely."

Pavin looked down at his page.

I suspected him of smiling.

The second stop belonged to Pavin.

"Don't make the pause pretty," Teren said.

Pavin nodded once.

No defense.

We started again.

That run reached the door.

Nessa moved a chair two inches before we crossed.

I glanced at it.

"Why?"

"Your right crutch needs the lane."

She did not say anything else.

The adjustment had already happened.

Accumulated workplace memory was becoming rude in a different direction. People were starting to fix geometry before I complained about it.

I took both crutches, started the cross during Pavin's line, and arrived at the chalk mark near the frame on time.

Teren said, "Through. Normal pace."

I went through.

The gray cloth was behind me.

The black side masking made a narrow passage left of it. I turned, followed the path behind the veil, and emerged from the other side.

Nothing happened.

Jori said, "Again. Lamps."

Nessa closed one stage shutter.

The work light dropped around the frame.

Jori opened the two low side lamps.

The gray cloth changed.

Not dramatically from where I stood.

Silver lines warmed. Dark paint between them seemed to sink. The center point grew brighter and smaller at once, which made the cloth look less like a surface and more like something seen through a surface.

The third lamp came on.

A cool white patch appeared near the false corridor's far end.

From the stage, I could still see the trick.

Teren pointed toward the house.

"Row five."

I took the side steps because the center ones were steeper and because Jori had left a length of cable there.

Nobody explained this. I simply used the route I knew.

From row five, the doorway opened into a hall that did not exist.

I stopped.

The six feet of cloth had become maybe twenty-five feet of pale corridor, narrow and high, with a bright turn at the far end. The silver perspective lines read as wall seams. The dark paint became recess. The cool patch became another room beyond the turn.

It was not perfect.

I could see the cloth if I looked for cloth.

But I had to look.

Pavin stood in the doorway onstage.

From the house, he looked like a man with a real place behind him.

"That's good," I said.

Jori called, "It was good yesterday too."

"Then why am I here?"

Nessa shouted, "Because yesterday nobody crossed it."

There was always a second sentence.

I returned to the stage.

Teren reset us at the last page.

Pavin spoke.

I answered as Corven.

Then I took the crutches and crossed through the false doorway at normal pace.

The instant my left crutch entered the frame, the corridor bent.

I saw it even from the stage.

A narrow gray shadow doubled itself along one silver line, and the far wall appeared to kink toward me. My body followed a beat later. The effect settled after I passed.

Jori said, "There."

Nessa said, "Left lamp."

Teren said, "Again. Same."

I came back.

"Was that me?"

Jori said, "Partly."

"My crutch?"

"Long narrow object entering before body."

That was broader than me.

Nessa picked up a wooden cane from the prop table.

She held it out through the frame without stepping forward.

The corridor bent again.

Less, but enough.

"Cane too," she said.

Pavin looked at his pages.

"Serrin has a cane in act three."

Nessa said, "Not here."

"Still offensive."

Teren pointed at my chalk mark.

"Again. Do not change your cross."

"I was about to move the crutch later."

"Don't."

"Why?"

"Because then we're testing your correction instead of the door."

Hessa would have liked him.

That was upsetting.

I reset.

Same start.

Same pace.

Same crutch placement as best I could repeat it.

The corridor bent again.

Jori crouched at the left lamp and shifted its hood half a hand inward.

"Again."

I crossed.

The bend reduced but did not vanish.

Nessa took the cane and repeated the same intrusion.

Still visible.

Jori moved the lamp back to its original position.

"Wrong direction."

No shame.

No speech.

Just information.

He looked at the frame, then at the black masking along its left edge.

"Need more dead edge."

Nessa pulled the black cloth from her shoulder.

That explained why she had brought it.

She clipped a strip behind the left side of the frame, narrowing the visible corridor by perhaps two fingers from the audience angle.

From the stage it looked worse.

From row five it looked better.

That was theatre in one sentence.

We tested again.

My crutch entered.

A small double shadow appeared, but the false wall no longer kinked around it. The black edge swallowed the first part of the shadow until my body entered the light field.

Jori said, "Better."

Nessa said, "Not enough."

Teren said, "Again."

Pavin sat on the edge of the stage while I crossed four more times.

The fourth time I got clever.

I turned my left crutch slightly inward as I entered, keeping more of the shaft behind my body.

The corridor held.

I looked toward Teren.

"Better."

"Wrong."

I stopped.

"You saw it."

"Yes. You changed the cross."

"I improved the effect."

"You improved Greg crossing the effect."

He pointed at the cane in Nessa's hand.

"Door still wrong."

There it was.

I had optimized the wrong system.

Again.

I reset and crossed normally.

Nessa repeated with the cane.

Jori added a second narrow strip of dead black cloth behind the frame, lower this time, where the lamp caught objects before bodies.

The next cane test held.

Mostly.

The shadow appeared, but as an ordinary shadow inside the false hall rather than a bright fold that exposed the cloth.

Pavin went to row five.

"Again," he called.

I crossed.

He was quiet.

Then, "Looks like a crutch."

"Devastating."

"Not like a broken hallway."

Jori sat back on his heels.

"Good."

Teren said, "Now scene."

That was important.

The door could work technically and still fail theatrically if everyone treated it like a demonstration.

We reset the page.

Pavin began.

This time the scene had somewhere to go.

Serrin lied about the letter. Corven refused the lie. Pavin moved toward the doorway before the argument had ended, which made the false corridor visible behind him. He did not look back at it. He did not present it.

It was just where he intended to leave.

I read the next line and moved when Teren's blocking required it.

Crutches.

Right foot.

Frame.

The false hall stayed a hall.

I disappeared behind the veil.

Pavin said his last line into the space I had left.

For half a second, standing behind six feet of treated cloth in a passage that smelled like dust and lamp oil, I understood why the old flat exit had been wrong.

The audience needed to believe Serrin could keep walking after the scene ended.

Not because the hallway was important.

Because leaving was.

I almost turned that into a theory.

Then Nessa said, "Greg, you're blocking the reset."

I moved.

Theatre saved again.

We ran it three more times.

Pavin missed one line on the second and did not pretend otherwise.

I came in early on the third because I heard his breath and mistook it for the end of a pause.

Teren stopped me.

"Wait for the look."

Same note family as before.

Not identical scene.

Useful memory without ritual.

On the final run, nobody stopped us.

That felt less dramatic than I remembered it feeling months ago.

It also felt better.

No one needed to announce improvement. We had work that now worked.

Rinna appeared at the stage door before I had fully realized we were done.

"Finished?"

Teren said, "For today."

Rinna looked at Jori.

"Cloth?"

"Works with the extra mask."

Nessa corrected him.

"Dead edge."

"Black cloth."

"Dead edge."

Rinna looked at neither of them long enough for the argument to become billable.

She handed me two bits.

I counted them once.

Two.

"Thank you."

"Tomorrow?"

"No."

Rinna pointed toward the side passage.

"If Teren needs you again, paper. No standing rehearsal."

"My repeatable body is devastated."

"Your body can go home."

I collected the shoulder bag.

Nessa had already removed one of the black strips and was marking its end with chalk so it would return to the same place tomorrow.

Jori was measuring the gap beside the frame with two fingers and a short rule.

Pavin sat in row five watching the false hall alone.

Their work continued after mine ended.

That felt right.

I left with two bits in the inside pocket of the bag, separate from charcoal.

The city outside had not changed because a fake hallway now looked better from the fifth row.

A fish seller argued over a cracked bowl. A cart backed into a lane and made three people move. Someone above me beat dust from a rug without checking who was below.

I avoided the rug.

Old Greg had seen illusions used to hide fortifications, multiply troop movement, disguise bridges, and make people kill shadows.

This afternoon I had watched two pieces of black cloth stop a fake corridor from folding around a cane.

Same world.

Different urgency.

I liked this one more.

At home, Lyssa was back before me.

The brown cuff sat on the far side of the table. She had thread between her fingers and did not look up until I put the two bits beside my bowl.

"Theatre?"

"Theatre."

"Acting?"

"Repeatable body."

She looked at me.

"What?"

"Rinna's phrase. Teren's philosophy. I crossed a false door several times until it stopped breaking around my crutch."

Lyssa looked toward the real front door.

"That one works."

"Olan has ruined your standards."

Tavi came in from the courtyard.

"Door red."

I pointed at him.

"Different door."

"Horse up."

"Also different problem."

He climbed onto a stool and began telling Lyssa something that involved a horse, a tree, and my continued criminal use of poles.

She listened to him instead of me.

Fair.

I took the two bits upstairs and set them beside the three copper rather than mixing them into a number I did not know how to convert.

Three copper.

Two bits.

No theory required.

The false hallway had disappeared the moment Jori shut the lamps.

The pay had not.

# CHAPTER 302

## THE LOW LAMPS

Lyssa asked me to go for a walk after I had already decided not to.

This was unfair.

I had come home from the theatre, reported the useful parts, failed to become the center of Tavi's horse story, eaten, and reached the portion of the evening where a chair begins making persuasive arguments.

Lyssa finished a line of stitching at the far end of the table.

The brown cuff remained beside her.

I did not ask what stage of life it had reached.

Progress sometimes looked like silence.

She bit the thread, set the garment down, and flexed both hands.

"Walk?"

I looked toward the door.

Then at my crutches.

Then at her.

"I did that today."

"You always do that."

"Not voluntarily."

She stood.

That ended negotiations.

Cala was washing a bowl near the basin. Tavi had fallen asleep on a folded blanket with one wooden spoon still in his hand, which meant civilization had won by attrition.

Cala looked at Lyssa.

"Low lamps."

"I know."

That meant something to both of them.

It meant something to me too, once I noticed the windows across the court.

The blue-white charm lamp in the upper room opposite had been covered with a brown cloth. Two doors down, someone was lowering the glow on a wall bead until it barely lit the threshold. An ordinary oil lamp burned under a gallery without anyone caring.

I looked toward the open strip of evening sky.

A pale thing crossed it.

Then three more.

Lampwings.

Not surprising.

I had seen similar flights in other cities, usually after warm dry days when the evening air stayed soft. Different places called them different names and swore their local variety behaved differently from every other one in creation.

The insects themselves were small, maybe the length of my thumb joint, with broad pale wings that caught magical light better than flame. When enough of them passed through a bright street, the dust along their wings held a faint sheen for a while.

One alone looked like an insect.

A few hundred looked like weather.

Carrow apparently expected a few hundred.

Lyssa went upstairs for a light wrap.

I stayed seated.

This was not laziness.

It was tactical conservation of standing.

Cala glanced at me.

"You going?"

"Apparently."

"West lanes quieter."

Lyssa came back down.

She had changed nothing except the wrap over one shoulder.

"Not market," she said.

"I was not planning market."

"Good."

"I can know things before being corrected."

Cala made a sound that was not agreement.

We left.

The front door opened cleanly.

Outside, the first lampwing hit the wall beside my head.

Not hard.

It touched plaster, fluttered twice, and drifted upward again as if embarrassed.

Lyssa laughed.

"Hostile city."

"You walked into its lane."

"This is my lane."

The lampwing declined jurisdiction.

The first two streets were still bright enough to walk comfortably. Most shops had not closed, but the ones using cool charm light had covered or hooded it. A cobbler had hung a square of red cloth over his sign bead. The sign still showed through, dim and pink.

Three lampwings circled it anyway.

He waved a broom at them without leaving his stool.

"Working?" I asked.

"No."

He kept waving.

A fourth joined the other three.

"Strong evidence."

He told me to keep walking.

Lyssa did.

I followed.

The evening changed as we moved west.

Not dark.

Lower.

Warm oil lamps stayed open. Window candles remained. The sharper magical whites disappeared behind cloth, shutters, cups, bits of painted tin, whatever people owned that could turn a bright point into a dull one.

The lampwings became easier to see because the city had stopped competing with them.

They floated through the lanes in loose waves.

Pale gray when they crossed shadow.

Pearl when they caught lamplight.

Every so often one held a faint green-white trace along the wing edge for a few seconds after passing a charm.

Children chased them.

Adults told the children not to.

The children treated this as atmospheric information.

At the first corner, two girls held an empty basket between them and tried to convince the wings to enter voluntarily.

Their mother stood in a doorway behind them.

"Not in the house."

"We know."

"You say that with a basket."

One girl lowered the basket.

The other did not.

Lyssa smiled.

"Did you do that?"

I asked.

"No."

"Too dignified?"

"No basket."

That was a better answer.

The lane narrowed ahead.

A delivery cart had stopped outside a spice shop, leaving just enough room for one person at a time between the wheel and the opposite wall.

Lyssa looked at it.

Then at me.

"Other way."

I nodded.

No discussion.

The crutches made narrow passing possible if everyone cooperated, but the lampwing traffic had collected three children and a woman with a bundle near the cart. There was no reason to turn an evening walk into geometry I could avoid.

We took the side lane.

Lyssa did not slow to accommodate me.

She slowed because the lane was rougher.

There was a difference.

I knew it because I had spent enough time walking beside her to know when she was matching me and when she was simply choosing her own pace.

Tonight we were both slow.

That felt good.

A lampwing settled on the wall at shoulder height.

Lyssa stopped.

I stopped because she did.

Its wings were not actually white. Up close they were mottled cream and gray, with darker veins and a powdery line along the outer edge.

No visible glow now.

The insect folded its wings.

Lyssa leaned closer.

"Fat one."

"Professional assessment?"

"Yes."

"Guild certified?"

She ignored me.

The lampwing opened again and drifted away.

We kept walking.

I had no urge to catch it.

This also felt suspiciously competent.

At the end of the side lane a vendor had set up a shallow iron pan over a brick stove. The oil inside smelled like onions, hot dough, and the sort of decision people regretted only after eating enough to make regret difficult.

The vendor used one shielded amber lamp.

No wings bothered it.

Lyssa looked at the pan.

I looked at Lyssa.

She kept walking.

I said, "Coward."

She stopped.

"What?"

"You looked."

"So?"

"Then walked."

"I can look."

"Not convincingly."

She turned back toward the stall.

I had won something.

I was not yet sure what.

The vendor lifted a twist of fried dough from the oil with a wire scoop. Small pieces, rough and brown, dusted with salt and something red.

"One bit," he said before I asked.

Lyssa looked at me.

I had two bits upstairs.

No.

I corrected myself.

I had two bits in my pocket because I had put them there when we left.

That had been a choice I had not examined too closely.

The three copper stayed home.

Two bits came with me.

I could pretend this was caution.

Maybe the door would charge admission.

Maybe the lampwings had organized.

The truth was simpler.

I had earned small money yesterday and wanted the option to spend small money today.

This was dangerous thinking.

"One," I said.

The vendor held out his hand.

I gave him a bit.

No conversion.

No copper broken.

No household money.

One bit for a paper twist of hot fried dough.

The transaction took four seconds and did not become a business plan.

I was proud of everyone involved.

The vendor shook more red powder over the top.

Lyssa said, "Too much."

He stopped.

"Good?"

She considered the twist.

"Now."

He handed it to me.

I looked at her.

"You negotiated my seasoning."

"You would complain."

"I was prepared to suffer quietly."

"No."

The first piece was too hot.

I bit it anyway.

Pain arrived immediately and correctly.

Lyssa watched my face.

"Hot."

"I know."

"You bit it."

"I know."

She took one from the top and held it for three breaths before eating.

This was infuriating.

We moved away from the stall before becoming part of its furniture.

The west lane opened into a small square with a dry fountain in the middle.

Not broken.

Seasonal, as far as I knew.

Nobody had gathered to explain it to me.

Benches ringed two sides. A broad tree leaned over the third.

The square was darker than the market roads because almost every shopfront had lowered its charm light.

Lampwings filled the space above the fountain.

Not a solid cloud.

Dozens.

Maybe more.

Enough that their pale wings kept appearing and disappearing against the dark upper walls.

A boy sat on the fountain rim with a tiny glass cup over one hand.

His father, or an adult with the exhausted authority of one, said, "Open it."

"But it glows."

"It glows outside too."

The boy opened the cup.

A lampwing escaped.

He watched it rise.

"I was keeping it safe."

"It was not asking."

Lyssa sat on the fountain rim two body lengths away from them.

I chose the bench.

She looked over.

"Old."

"Efficient."

The bench was lower than ideal but had arms.

I backed to it, set the crutches where they would not project into the walking path, put my right foot under me, and sat with the left knee clear.

No difficulty.

Lyssa moved from the fountain to the bench beside me after perhaps ten seconds.

"Better?"

"For you?"

She took another piece of fried dough from the twist.

"Yes."

"Then yes."

We ate.

For a while that was all.

The square did not require interpretation.

A woman closed shutters on a blue-lit apothecary sign.

Someone upstairs played three notes badly on a reed pipe and then repeated them as if repetition could become improvement through force.

The lampwings crossed above us.

A few caught light from hidden signs and carried it away in faint lines along their wings.

One landed on the back of the bench.

Lyssa ate another piece.

I said, "You wanted to see them."

She nodded.

"Why?"

She looked at me.

"Because I wanted to."

That was the complete answer.

I smiled.

She narrowed her eyes.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Greg."

"I was about to ask for a system."

"No."

"I stopped."

"Good."

She ate the last large piece before I could reach it.

Punishment was swift.

I took one of the smaller ones.

"How long?"

"Tonight. Maybe tomorrow."

"The flight?"

"Mm."

"Every year?"

She shrugged.

"Most."

Good.

Enough.

I did not ask where they came from.

I did not ask whether the held sheen was mana storage, dust response, wing structure, or something else.

I had known species in other places that carried light for mating.

Others used it to confuse night birds.

One desert variety absorbed enough spell glow near caravan roads that wealthy travelers paid children to sweep them off ward lanterns.

Those facts told me nothing certain about Carrow's lampwings.

So I left them where they belonged.

Flying.

Lyssa nudged my shoulder.

"Theatre?"

I looked at her.

"You did not ask earlier."

"Tavi was talking."

"His version was false."

"His version had horse."

"A powerful evidentiary standard."

"What happened?"

I considered giving her the long version.

The note.

The depth cloth.

The lamps.

The false corridor bending around my left crutch.

The cane test.

Jori's lamp adjustment.

Nessa's dead edge.

Teren telling me I had improved Greg instead of the door.

Pavin from row five.

The final scene.

There was a version of the story that could occupy the rest of the square.

Instead I said, "They made a hallway look longer than it was. My crutch broke the illusion. We tested until they fixed the stage instead of making me cross differently."

Lyssa nodded.

"Good."

"That is all?"

"Did it work?"

"Yes."

"You got paid?"

"Two bits."

"Then good."

I held up the paper twist.

"One bit now."

She looked at the remaining fried crumbs.

"That was fast."

"Capital moved."

"Into your mouth."

"Half into yours."

"Less."

I stared at her.

"You ate the largest piece."

"One."

"The largest."

She did not care.

I leaned back.

A lampwing crossed close enough to my face that I felt the air from its wings.

Then another landed in Lyssa's hair.

Not deep.

At the outer edge of the wrap near her temple.

I reached automatically.

She lifted one finger.

"Wait."

I stopped.

The lampwing crawled from a fold of dark hair onto the edge of her wrap.

For a second it sat there, pale against the cloth.

A faint line along one wing held the last memory of a charm lamp.

Green-white.

Very dim.

Lyssa turned her head carefully to look at it.

"You have one."

"I know."

"You want it removed?"

"No."

So we sat with it.

The light faded first.

Then the wing color returned to cream and gray.

A few seconds later the insect opened both wings and lifted away.

Lyssa watched it go.

I watched her.

She caught me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Again."

"I am improving."

"Slow."

Fair.

The boy with the glass cup had moved on to trying to catch the shadow of a lampwing with his foot.

This worked better for everyone.

We finished the fried dough.

The empty paper twist became my responsibility because I had paid for it.

There was a refuse crock beside the fountain.

I stood.

Right foot under.

Bench arm.

Crutches.

Easy.

I carried the paper to the crock and came back.

Lyssa was already standing.

"You done?"

"Yes."

We took a different route home.

Not because the first was blocked.

Because Lyssa wanted to.

That reason was becoming easier to accept without interrogation.

The lanes were darker now.

People had lowered more lamps as the flight thickened.

The dimness changed my walking.

Crutch tips mattered more when the stone edge disappeared into shadow.

I shortened my pace slightly on uneven stretches and let the right foot confirm the surface before committing full weight.

Nothing dramatic.

Just nighttime.

Lyssa noticed once when I chose the smoother center strip instead of the wall edge.

She moved to my left without comment so oncoming people had the wider side.

I noticed.

I did not thank her.

Not because I was ungrateful.

Because after enough time together, thanking someone for every ordinary adjustment starts making the ordinary adjustment less ordinary.

A man coming the other way carried a tray of six blue-white glow beads under a dark cloth.

One escaped at the edge.

Lampwings found it immediately.

Three struck the cloth.

One landed on his shoulder.

He swore and pulled the cover tighter.

Lyssa said, "Should use box."

He said, "Should mind yours."

She kept walking.

I waited until we were past.

"Helpful."

"He knows."

"Then why say it?"

"He was rude last week."

There it was.

A whole relationship I had not been present for.

"Who is he?"

"Doesn't matter."

I accepted that with difficulty.

The city continued without my documentation.

At the next corner, an older couple had placed two stools outside their doorway. They sat facing the lane with no food, no work, no obvious reason except the lampwings.

The woman lifted a hand to Lyssa.

Lyssa lifted hers back.

Neither stopped.

I did not ask who they were.

This was becoming exhausting.

A little farther on, the flight thinned.

The household court had fewer bright lamps than the west square even on ordinary nights, so less collected there.

Cala had left one oil lamp burning near the basin.

No charm light.

The window opposite remained covered.

Tavi was still asleep.

The spoon had been removed from his hand.

Probably for public safety.

Lyssa took off her wrap.

A little pale dust marked one edge where the lampwing had sat.

She rubbed it between finger and thumb.

Nothing glowed.

Then she shook the cloth once and hung it over the chair.

I took the remaining bit from my pocket.

Cala looked at it.

"One?"

"One spent."

"Food?"

"Fried dough."

She nodded.

No trial.

No demand for accounting.

I went upstairs and put the bit beside the three copper.

Three copper.

One bit.

The number had changed.

Nothing had broken.

That felt stranger than it should have.

For a long time, money had existed in categories.

Rent.

Debt.

Food.

Tool.

Emergency.

Work.

A coin needed a reason strong enough to survive losing it.

Tonight I had spent one bit because Lyssa looked at fried dough and kept walking.

I almost wrote that sentence down.

Then I imagined finding it later.

I left the charcoal wrapped.

Downstairs, Lyssa said something to Cala.

Cala laughed.

I could not hear the words.

I did not go down to ask.

A lampwing crossed the window outside my room, pale for half a second in the covered light from across the court.

Then it disappeared into the dark.

The remaining bit stayed beside the copper.

The empty space where the other one had been stayed empty.

That was all.

# CHAPTER 303

## THE DRY BENCH

The problem with a good evening was that eventually you had to wash it off.

Not emotionally.

Physically.

I woke with fried oil still faintly in my hair, lampwing dust apparently capable of surviving several hours on my coat, and the unmistakable knowledge that I had walked enough streets in two days to become part of the street.

Cala noticed before I finished breakfast.

She looked at me.

Then at the basin.

Then back at me.

"No."

I put down my bread.

"No what?"

"Not in that basin."

"I have used that basin."

"Hands."

"Face."

"Also hands."

"Strong record."

She pointed toward the door.

"Bath."

I looked down at myself.

I had bathed since losing the leg.

Obviously.

Mostly at home, carefully, with water carried in, cloths, a stool, and the sort of planning that turned cleanliness into a minor military operation.

Carrow had public bathhouses.

I knew this.

I had walked past several.

Knowing a place existed and deciding to put your naked body inside it were different categories of information.

Lyssa was at the table with her hair tied up and the brown cuff nowhere visible.

I did not ask where it had gone.

I had survived almost twenty-four hours without interrogating that garment.

Growth continued.

She looked at me.

"You smell."

"Cruel."

"True."

"More cruel."

Tavi was eating something soft from a bowl and contributed, "Greg bath."

The household had reached consensus.

I finished breakfast.

"Which one?"

Cala said, "North Steps."

I knew the place.

Large tiled building tucked below the old uphill road, two courtyards, public steam, wash rooms, separate hotter pool, laundry side entrance.

Not expensive by the look of it.

Not elegant either.

Good.

"How much?"

"One bit wash. Towel extra if you need."

I had one private bit upstairs.

I stared toward the ceiling.

Cala watched me.

"House pays bath."

That changed the question.

"Why?"

"Because you live here."

Simple.

I disliked how often simple answers survived me.

She took two small bits from a household cup and set them beside my bowl.

"One entry. One cloth."

"I have cloth."

"You have a square."

"It is efficient."

"It is a square."

Lyssa said, "Take cloth."

Betrayal remained communal.

I accepted the two household bits.

My private money stayed upstairs.

Three copper.

One bit.

Untouched.

The shoulder bag stayed home too.

There was nothing in it I wanted near public water.

Charcoal especially had a gift for becoming more interesting when wet.

I took only the two bits, a dry change of shirt, my belt, and a folded length of household cloth Cala considered large enough to meet civilization's minimum standard.

My crutches came because the alternative was ambitious.

The morning was clear.

The lampwing flight had mostly vanished with daylight.

A few pale insects rested high under eaves where the shade stayed cool, but people had uncovered charm lamps again. Blue-white sign beads returned to normal brightness. Nobody gathered in the streets to commemorate the restoration of commercial visibility.

Carrow survived another seasonal inconvenience.

I kept walking.

North Steps bathhouse announced itself with steam escaping from three narrow roof vents.

The building was older than the businesses around it.

Dark stone lower walls.

Pale tile above.

Two arched doors, one marked with a painted red cup, the other with a blue line.

I knew the broad bathhouse conventions.

The blue line led to washing rooms.

The red cup marked hotter communal soak and steam.

A smaller side door had a painted bundle of shirts.

Laundry.

No mystery.

Inside, a woman behind a stone counter looked at my crutches, then at me.

"Wash?"

"Yes."

"Hot room?"

"Probably not."

"One bit wash. Cloth?"

"I brought one."

She pointed at the folded household cloth under my arm.

"That's cloth."

Validation.

Cala would be unbearable.

I set one household bit on the counter.

The woman pushed the second bit back toward me.

I looked down.

"I was given two."

"Then keep one."

"Strong business model."

She did not care.

"Dry bench?"

I paused.

"Yes."

She reached below the counter and handed me a small clay token with a square cut through its center.

Not numbered.

Just shape.

"Third room. Left wall."

That was all.

No speech about my leg.

No examination.

No question about how long it had been gone.

Just dry bench.

I liked her immediately and hoped never to learn her name.

A narrow hall led past changing alcoves.

The floor changed underfoot before the wet rooms.

Not visibly at first.

Same pale tile.

Then my crutch tip landed and held with a faint resistance I recognized.

Grip wash.

Common bathhouse and kitchen treatment.

Not sticky.

Not rough.

The tile simply refused the first fraction of sideways slide under wet rubber and bare feet.

I had known versions of it in military infirmaries, guild wash rooms, alchemist floors, and one monastery where the monks had apparently decided falling naked was an unacceptable spiritual practice.

The local treatment looked old.

Faint green seams appeared between every fourth tile when steam drifted low.

Maintenance lines, probably.

I did not stop to investigate.

I was trying to become less street.

The third wash room had six low stools around a long stone trough fed by three bronze spouts.

Two men were already there.

One older, heavy through the shoulders, washing his hair.

One thin and younger than me, aggressively scrubbing one foot as if it had committed fraud.

Neither looked up for more than a second.

The left wall held the dry bench.

I understood why the counter woman had named it separately.

Most benches were stone.

This one was wood raised on four pale ceramic feet, positioned beyond the direct splash line, with two upright slots built into one end.

Crutch slots.

Or cane slots.

Probably both.

The top was wide enough to sit and change without balancing clothing on a wet floor.

A small brass line ran under the front edge.

No glow.

No obvious charm effect.

The bench was simply dry.

Very dry.

Even with steam in the room.

I set my crutches into the upright slots.

They stood without sliding.

Useful.

I sat.

Right foot planted.

Left knee clear.

Then I discovered the next problem.

Boot.

Taking off one boot while seated was easy.

Taking off one boot while keeping clean clothes off a damp floor and not knocking over two crutches was still easy, but only after I stopped trying to do everything at once.

I placed the folded shirt on the dry bench behind me.

Belt beside it.

Boot off.

Sock off.

Trousers next.

No drama.

The younger man scrubbing his foot glanced over.

"Bench good?"

I looked at it.

"Excellent review so far."

He nodded.

"I hate stone."

"Cold?"

"Steals clothes."

I looked at the wet stone bench opposite us.

A shirt had fallen from it onto the floor.

Evidence supported his case.

The older man rinsed soap from his hair.

"Dry bench used to be two."

The younger man said, "Before crack."

"What crack?"

The older man pointed toward the empty stretch of left wall.

A lighter rectangle in the tile showed where another bench had once stood.

"Gone winter."

"Why?"

"Cracked."

That completed local history at the level I required.

I did not ask who maintained it.

I did not ask whether the ceramic feet carried the drying effect.

I did not ask what the brass line did.

I undressed.

The room remained a room.

That mattered more than I expected.

My left leg ended below the knee.

There was no hiding it once the trousers came off.

Not that I had hidden it exactly.

Clothing and crutches told most of the story.

Still, there was a difference between visible fact and exposed body.

The older man looked once.

Not stared.

Looked.

Then returned to his hair.

The younger man had already lost interest in everything except his enemy foot.

I had spent enough of my first life in communal baths, barracks wash rooms, healer rooms, river camps, and terrible roadside inns to know that nakedness became boring quickly when everyone had their own skin to manage.

This body was newer to me than that habit.

That was the strange part.

The bathhouse did not care.

Good.

I checked the skin at the end of the residual limb before washing.

No redness beyond ordinary.

No rubbed spot.

No issue from yesterday's long walking.

I had learned not to trust "feels fine" as the only inspection.

That lesson had arrived through repetition rather than disaster.

Also good.

A wooden dipper hung from the trough.

I moved one crutch from the slot, stood, and took the three steps to the nearest stool.

The grip tile held.

Right foot.

Crutches.

Turn.

Sit.

Easy enough.

The water from the first bronze spout was warm.

Second hotter.

Third cool.

Not labels.

Position and habit, apparently.

I tested with fingers first.

Warm was enough.

The older man saw me doing it.

"Middle bites."

"I noticed."

"It gets worse noon."

"Why?"

He shrugged.

"Hot."

Excellent.

Bathhouse thermodynamics remained undefeated.

I filled the dipper.

Washed.

There was no revelation.

Soap worked.

Water worked.

My body became cleaner.

I appreciated this.

The stump required more care than the rest of me.

Not delicate.

Careful.

I washed the skin, rinsed fully, dried it before putting any cloth back over it.

No magical salve.

No dramatic scar treatment.

No hidden complication.

Just skin under load that benefited from not being left damp.

The bathhouse made that easier because the dry bench existed.

I could wash at the stool, then return to a genuinely dry place without trying to hop, balance clothing, or trust wet stone.

The route between stool and bench was six steps.

I counted none of them after the second trip.

That was another kind of improvement.

The younger man finally defeated his foot.

He leaned back on his stool.

"Hot room?"

The older man said, "No."

The younger man looked offended.

"You always say no."

"Because you stay too long."

"I don't."

"You slept."

"One time."

"In steam."

"Warm."

The older man looked at me.

"See?"

I raised both hands.

"I have no jurisdiction."

The younger man pointed at my crutches.

"You going?"

"Hot room?"

"Yeah."

I considered.

The red-cup room was optional.

I had paid wash only.

Probably additional fee.

Also more wet floor, more heat, more transfers, and no actual reason beyond being there.

"No."

The older man nodded as if I had joined his political party.

The younger man said, "Cowards."

He left for steam.

The older man called after him, "Don't sleep."

No response.

I finished washing my hair.

That required leaning forward over the trough, which changed my balance enough that I kept one crutch braced under my right arm and the other within reach rather than pretending two free hands were worth bad positioning.

Water ran down my face.

Soap got in one eye.

This proved the gods remained attentive.

I swore.

The older man laughed.

"Middle spout."

"What about it?"

"Rinse."

I used the middle spout.

It was much hotter than the first.

I swore again.

He laughed harder.

"You said it bites."

"Did."

"Then why recommend it?"

"Funny."

Carrow's public institutions remained hostile.

I used the warm spout instead.

By the time I returned to the dry bench, I felt human again.

Wet human.

Progress.

The household cloth proved large enough.

I hated Cala for being correct.

I dried thoroughly before dressing.

Especially the left leg.

No rush.

The bench's front brass line warmed faintly under my forearm while I sat.

Not hot.

Just warmer than the room.

I looked at it.

Then deliberately looked away.

Dry bench.

It dried.

Mechanism could remain employed without my supervision.

The younger man returned from the hot room red-faced and triumphant.

The older man stared at him.

"You sleep?"

"No."

"Lie."

"Didn't."

He sat on the stone bench.

Immediately stood again.

"Cold."

I said, "Dry bench has received excellent reviews."

He glared at me.

I was becoming part of the bathhouse culture too quickly.

The counter woman had given me the square token.

I realized I still had it on the bench beside my belt.

I held it up.

"Do I return this?"

The older man said, "Counter."

"Thank you."

No one asked me to write a report.

I dressed.

The clean shirt felt better than it had any right to.

Boot on.

Sock dry.

Trousers.

Belt.

I checked the crutch tips before leaving the wet room.

Both clean enough.

One had a thread stuck near the edge of the rubber tip.

Blue.

I pulled it free.

Ordinary thread.

It went into the refuse pot by the door.

Not Lyssa thread.

Not magical thread.

Not evidence.

A thread.

I felt almost powerful.

At the counter I returned the square token.

The woman took it.

"You use cloth?"

"Yes."

She pointed at the second household bit still in my hand.

"Keep."

"Cala gave it for cloth."

"Your cloth."

"Ah."

The bathhouse had defended Cala's finances against her intentions.

I approved.

"How long does the dry bench stay dry?"

The question escaped before I could stop it.

The woman looked at me.

"Until it doesn't."

I closed my eyes.

"Of course."

"Then we tell Noll."

There was the maintenance boundary.

One name.

One action.

Enough.

I did not ask who Noll was.

I left.

Outside, the day had warmed.

Clean skin made the air feel cooler.

Probably because I was still slightly damp in places.

No theory required.

I had one household bit in my pocket because the bathhouse had refused to invent a towel expense.

That bit belonged to the house.

I knew this without debate.

A baker had set small seed rolls near the door.

I looked.

One bit for two, according to the chalk board.

I kept walking.

This was not restraint around my private money.

It was not my money.

Different skill.

At the next lane a barber had a chair outside.

The man in it wore half a face of white foam and looked asleep.

I touched my jaw.

Then kept walking because solving every form of grooming in one morning would have been suspiciously efficient.

The household door opened cleanly when I returned.

Cala looked up from the table.

"Better."

"Your standards are abusive."

She held out her hand.

I gave her the unspent household bit.

She looked at it.

"Cloth?"

"Brought one."

She looked at the folded damp cloth under my arm.

"Good."

I hated everyone.

Lyssa came down the stairs.

She stopped halfway.

"Bath."

"Apparently visible."

"Hair."

I touched it.

Still damp.

"That is how water works."

She came the rest of the way down.

Tavi appeared behind her with the timing of a curse.

"Greg wet."

"Thank you."

"Bath."

"Yes."

"Greg pole bath."

"No."

He smiled.

Historical contamination spread.

I hung the damp household cloth where Cala pointed and put the clean shirt's old companion into the wash pile.

My shoulder bag remained exactly where I had left it.

Three copper and one private bit remained upstairs.

No client had arrived.

No theatre paper waited.

No Guild message had appeared.

The morning had cost the household one bit and returned one.

Net bath entry: one household bit.

I did not write that down.

Cala already knew what baths cost.

Lyssa already knew.

Carrow already knew.

I was allowed to know something without preserving it for future civilization.

I sat at the table.

The skin at the end of my left leg was dry and comfortable.

My hair dripped once onto my shoulder.

Lyssa noticed.

"Not dry."

"Mostly."

She handed me a smaller cloth.

I rubbed my hair.

"Now?"

She looked.

"Better."

I leaned back.

The public bath had contained treated grip tile, warm-water routing, a dry bench, ceramic feet, a brass line, and at least one unknown man named Noll who apparently became relevant when dry benches stopped being dry.

A younger version of me, meaning the actual nineteen-year-old whose body I currently occupied, might have noticed none of that beyond whether the water was hot.

The older version might once have tried to understand all of it.

Today I had mostly washed.

That seemed healthier.

Then Cala set a basket of beans in front of me.

"Sort."

I looked at her.

"I have just completed an important civic ritual."

"Sort."

"Clean hands deserve better."

"Sort."

I sorted beans.

One pale.

One split.

Good into the large bowl.

Bad into the small.

Tavi climbed onto the stool opposite me and stole one good bean.

Cala took it from him without looking.

Lyssa returned to whatever work she had upstairs.

The brown cuff did not reappear.

Outside, someone uncovered a charm lamp even though the sun was already strong enough to make it pointless.

A single lampwing drifted under the gallery roof, found nothing interesting, and continued on.

I did not follow it.

By midday I smelled like soap instead of city.

The change was sufficient.

# CHAPTER 304

## THE QUIET TABLE

Hessa's note arrived while I was sorting beans.

This felt like criticism of both activities.

Cala had placed the basket in front of me after breakfast and left me with the same rules as yesterday.

Good in the large bowl.

Split, pale, or suspicious in the small.

Tavi had decided the small bowl was a hat.

This complicated quality control.

Then someone knocked.

Not loudly.

Two quick taps.

Cala opened the door.

A Guild runner stood outside with a folded square of paper.

Not the boy from the theatre.

Not anyone I knew.

He looked past Cala.

"Greg?"

I raised one hand.

The runner crossed the room, handed me the note, and left.

No conversation.

No mystery.

Just delivery.

I opened it.

AFTER MIDDAY.

EAT FIRST.

NO ATTEMPTS BEFORE.

HESSA.

I read it twice.

Lyssa was near the window, cutting thread.

She looked up.

"Guild?"

"Apparently my social life has become paperwork."

"Magic?"

"Probably."

Cala said, "Eat first."

I held up the note.

"Hessa has infiltrated the house."

"Good."

Tavi put the small bowl on his head.

"Guild hat."

"No."

He smiled.

I finished sorting beans.

This was important.

Not magically.

Civilizationally.

The three copper and one private bit remained upstairs.

I had no reason to bring any of them.

The shoulder bag came with me after midday because it carried the narrow scrap and charcoal, which were actually relevant to Guild work.

My body felt normal.

Clean, too.

This was still new enough to notice after North Steps.

The left leg was comfortable.

No skin issue.

Hands ordinary.

Right shoe flat.

Crutches sound.

I ate before leaving because I had developed a strong professional interest in not arguing with Hessa about food twice.

The Guild yard was busier than the last time I had come for magic.

A pair of apprentices carried a narrow copper frame across the stones.

Someone had opened three high shutters on the western building.

A clerk sat under the awning with six little glass seals laid in a row and was touching each one with a brass pick.

None of this concerned me.

I went to Hessa's room.

The table had changed.

Not the table itself.

The things on it.

The familiar pale paper square sat near the center.

Same square.

I recognized the softened corner.

Under it was a flat black slate plate I had not seen before.

Fine white lines crossed the slate into a grid, close enough that a small movement would stop being an argument about whether anyone had blinked.

A narrow brass arch rose over the plate.

At the top hung a clear bead with a blue center.

Four pale threads descended from the arch around the plate.

Not touching it.

Telltales.

The bead was dark.

I stopped at the door.

"That looks expensive."

Hessa did not look up from her notes.

"It is not yours."

"Then it looks reasonably priced."

"Sit."

I sat.

The chair remained in the same place as prior sessions.

Near mark on the board.

Middle.

Far.

The far mark existed and had become less dramatic since failing to do anything there once.

This was healthy.

Possibly.

Hessa finished writing.

Then came the questions.

Sleep.

Food.

Pain.

Independent draw.

Independent shaping.

Independent external attempt.

All ordinary.

No.

No.

No.

She checked pulse.

Hands.

Forearms.

Grip.

Shoulders.

She asked about the bathhouse only because I mentioned I had gone.

"Skin?"

"Normal."

"Residual limb?"

"Normal."

"Any redness after?"

"No."

"Any fall?"

"No."

"Hot room?"

I stared at her.

"No."

"Good."

"You sound like the older man."

"What older man?"

"Bath politics."

She ignored me.

This was probably for the best.

Then she turned to the table.

"Distance is not today's variable."

I looked at the far mark.

"Far and I have agreed to spend time apart."

"No."

"Middle?"

"No."

"Near only?"

"Yes."

I leaned back.

"That sounds dangerously like progress."

"It is measurement."

Less dangerous.

Hessa pointed to the black slate.

"Witness plate."

Then the threads.

"Draft telltales."

Then the bead.

"Still bead."

I knew the broad arrangement.
