Still beads showed up around fine balances, reagent scales, ink films, hair-thin rune work, and any workshop where a person wanted to blame air less often.

They did not make a vacuum.

They calmed small local drafts.

Some were mechanical-magic hybrids.

Some used treated glass.

Some used cheap field beads that lasted three months and then became decorative disappointment.

I had no idea which this was.

I did not need to.

"You're trying to stop the room from moving the paper."

"Reduce one source of ambiguity."

"Not stop."

"Correct."

I looked at the four pale threads.

They hung almost still already.

One moved faintly toward the window.

"Why the witness plate?"

"Better visual measure."

"Why same paper?"

"Object history."

"Why near?"

"Most clean responses."

I nodded.

Near had two clean displacements in four established attempts.

Not reliable.

Just less empty than the others.

Middle had one clean in six, plus the possible edge lift from attempt sixty-eight.

Far had one attempt.

Nothing.

Hessa touched the still bead with one finger.

It remained dark.

"Three maximum today."

"All near?"

"Yes."

"Same duration?"

"Yes."

"Same permitted effort?"

"Yes."

"Same direction?"

"Away."

"Still bead on for all three?"

"No."

I looked at her.

"Comparison."

"Yes."

That was the first interesting thing.

She pointed at the plate.

"Attempt seventy-three with the still bead off. Same room condition as much as practical, improved visual reference only."

"And seventy-four?"

"Still bead on if I continue."

"Seventy-five?"

"Same as seventy-four if I continue."

"Why not alternate off again?"

"Because I am not building a complete study in one afternoon."

I sighed.

"I keep hoping ambition will become a medical requirement."

"It has symptoms."

Cruel institution.

Hessa placed the pale paper square on the black grid.

Near position.

The white lines around it made its edges obvious.

One corner sat just short of a crossing line.

I could see exactly how annoying a tiny shift would become.

The draft telltales hung around the plate.

The leftmost moved faintly.

Window air.

Hessa adjusted nothing.

Attempt seventy-three was supposed to include the room.

Not fix it.

I settled.

Hand loose.

Shoulder down.

Breathing normal.

No leaning.

No reaching with my body.

Minimal established external-effect attempt.

Direction away.

Same duration.

Hessa said, "Expectation?"

"Low to medium."

"Evidence?"

"Near has two clean responses in four attempts."

"Anything else?"

"I like the black plate."

She looked at me.

"Not evidence."

"It improves morale."

"Begin."

I worked.

The paper did nothing.

The left telltale shifted once.

The paper did not.

My mind noticed the grid lines and became convinced that maybe the near corner had moved a hair.

Then the end cue came.

I stopped.

Hessa moved before I spoke.

Paper.

Grid.

Telltales.

Window.

Table.

Floor.

Me.

She crouched to look across the slate.

Then stood.

"No observed response."

"I thought the corner might have."

"No."

"Good."

She looked at me.

"Why good?"

"Because the plate stopped me from promoting imagination into data."

Hessa wrote that down.

"I regret speaking."

"Body?"

"Fine."

"Urge to increase?"

"Low."

She checked me.

Pulse normal.

Hands normal.

Forearm normal.

Then she touched the clear bead.

A soft blue light appeared in its center.

Not bright.

Not theatrical.

The four telltale threads changed almost at once.

The left thread stopped drifting toward the window.

The right one moved once, then settled.

Within a few breaths all four hung straight.

The room still had air.

I could breathe.

Steam did not descend from the ceiling.

Nothing dramatic happened.

The little volume above the table simply became quieter.

I knew the broad sensation from good weighing benches.

My shirt sleeve stopped lifting at the cuff.

A loose corner of Hessa's note stopped trembling.

Small changes.

Useful ones.

"Still field reaches how far?" I asked.

Hessa pointed at the brass arch.

"Enough for this plate."

"That is not a distance."

"It is the distance that matters."

"Terrible teacher."

"Sit still."

I sat still.

She lifted the paper and replaced it at the same near position.

Not exactly the same grid intersection.

Close.

She marked two tiny reference points in her notes.

Attempt seventy-four.

The telltales stayed quiet.

The blue center of the bead stayed steady.

"Expectation?"

"Same ability expectation. Higher confidence in interpreting movement if movement happens."

Hessa's pencil stopped for half a second.

"Better."

"I have trained for years to receive that word."

"Begin."

I worked.

Nothing.

For the first few breaths, nothing.

Then the paper moved.

Not much.

A small clean slide away.

One white grid line appeared at the near edge where paper had covered it before.

The far edge crossed a second line by a smaller amount.

No corner popped.

No curl.

No flutter.

The square simply changed place.

I stopped on the cue.

My heart did not.

Hessa was already checking.

She did not touch the paper.

First the telltales.

All four still.

Then the bead.

Blue steady.

Then the table edge.

Then the slate.

She crouched.

Looked from left.

Right.

Across the paper.

Then she used a thin measuring strip against the grid.

I waited.

This was the hardest part of magic.

Not doing it.

Waiting for another person to tell me what I had already seen.

Hessa stood.

"Observed small net displacement away."

I breathed out.

"Clean?"

"Clean enough to log as displacement."

"Inside the still field."

"Yes."

"With telltales quiet."

"Yes."

"So not wind."

"No."

I closed my eyes.

She waited.

I opened them again.

"Ordinary room draft less likely."

"Yes."

"Not impossible."

"Correct."

"Still bead itself could matter."

"Yes."

"Table vibration."

"Checked, not eliminated."

"Body movement."

"Controlled as practical, not eliminated."

"Some cause we are not measuring."

"Always."

I looked at the paper.

"Do you ever enjoy anything?"

"Yes."

"Hidden skill."

She wrote.

I wanted the clean movement to become more than it was.

My brain tried immediately.

This proves the effect is real.

This proves air was never responsible.

This proves near works.

This proves the still bead helps.

This proves the external effect crosses calm fields.

This proves something about distance.

None of those sentences survived Hessa standing beside me.

So I chose the one that did.

"One clean near displacement under reduced-draft conditions."

"Correct."

That was enough.

Almost.

"Can I be happy?"

"You can be anything that does not change attempt seventy-five."

There it was.

The next trap.

Success created appetite.

I knew that one.

"Body?" Hessa asked.

"Fine."

"Urge to increase?"

"Medium."

"Because?"

"Because seventy-four moved and I want seventy-five to move more."

"Will you increase?"

"No."

"Certain?"

I took one breath.

Then another.

"Yes."

Hessa checked pulse.

Grip.

Hands.

Forearm.

She made me wait longer than I liked.

The paper remained where attempt seventy-four had left it until she measured and reset it.

The blue still bead stayed on.

The telltales stayed straight.

Attempt seventy-five.

Same near position.

Same paper.

Same witness plate.

Same still field.

Same direction.

Same permitted effort.

Same duration.

I settled.

The clean result from seventy-four tried to become a technique.

Whatever I had done, do that again.

That thought was dangerous because I did not know what I had done.

There had been no special breath.

No sharper focus.

No secret old-life structure.

No useful sensation I could isolate.

The paper had moved.

That was all.

I let the previous attempt go as much as I could.

"Expectation?" Hessa asked.

"Emotionally high. Evidentially low to medium."

"Why?"

"One clean response immediately prior under the same controlled condition is encouraging, but near is still inconsistent."

"Anything else?"

"I do not know what I did differently."

"Good."

"I hate that answer."

"Begin."

I worked.

Nothing happened.

The paper stayed flat.

The grid stayed visible where it had been visible.

The telltales stayed still.

The blue bead remained steady.

No slide.

No curl.

No lift.

No result.

The end cue came.

I stopped.

Hessa checked everything again.

"No observed response."

I nodded.

The disappointment was immediate.

Then something else followed.

Not relief.

Maybe shape.

Attempt seventy-four had not become a new normal.

Attempt seventy-five had killed that fantasy quickly.

Useful.

Cruel.

Useful.

"Body?"

"Fine."

"Urge?"

"Lower."

"Why?"

"Because the protocol is over."

"Also useful."

She turned off the still bead.

The blue center faded.

Within a few breaths the left telltale began drifting faintly toward the window again.

I watched it.

That mattered more now.

Not because it explained the paper.

Because it showed me the room had been doing things even when the room looked still.

Tiny things.

Enough to complicate tiny magic.

Hessa moved the paper off the slate.

"Totals."

I waited.

"External attempts now seventy-five."

"Near?"

"Three clean displacements in seven established near attempts."

"Today's near?"

"One clean in three."

"Still on?"

"One clean in two attempts with today's still-bead condition."

"Still off?"

"No response in one attempt today."

I nodded.

"Middle unchanged."

"Yes."

"Far unchanged."

"Yes."

"Can we say the still bead improves response?"

"No."

"Because one of two moved."

"Correct."

"Can we say the still bead suppresses my effect?"

"No."

"Because one moved."

"Correct."

"Can we say the still bead is irrelevant?"

"No."

I sighed.

"Wonderful device."

"It improved observation."

That was the actual point.

I looked at the black witness plate.

"Did it?"

"Yes."

"Even though it introduced a new variable."

"Yes."

"Because we know the variable."

"We know it exists."

Of course.

"Do you plan to repeat near under still conditions?"

"Maybe."

"Middle under still?"

"Maybe."

"Far?"

Hessa looked at me.

I raised both hands.

"Grief ritual only."

"Not selected."

"Next appointment?"

"Not selected."

"Next object?"

"Not selected."

"Next condition?"

"Not selected."

I nodded.

This was becoming a language.

Not selected did not mean never.

It meant not yet turned into a promise.

I liked promises too much when they involved magic.

Hessa gathered her notes.

I reached for the shoulder bag.

Then stopped.

"One question."

She looked at me.

"Why now?"

"Why the still bead?"

"Because the far test answered your question and exposed mine."

I waited.

She continued.

"You wanted to know whether far had been tested. It now has. I want cleaner evidence about what counts as response before adding more positions."

That landed harder than the clean movement.

Not because it revealed secret magic.

Because it made the next problem smaller.

We did not need to understand my power.

We needed to get better at seeing what it actually did.

I could work with that.

"That sounds almost optimistic."

"It is not."

"Of course."

"Go home."

I stood.

Right foot under.

Crutches placed.

No issue.

The Guild yard looked ordinary on the way out.

A porter was arguing with someone over a crate strap.

The clerk with the glass seals had reduced six to four.

One apprentice had acquired a broom.

Civilization advanced unevenly.

I walked home without testing anything.

This was not difficult because Hessa had made the rule explicit.

It was also not easy because attempt seventy-four existed now.

A clean near displacement.

Under quieter air.

My mind kept returning to it.

Not to the feeling.

There had been almost no feeling.

To the evidence.

The paper had crossed the grid.

The telltales had stayed still.

The still bead had remained blue.

One small piece of uncertainty had narrowed.

Not disappeared.

Narrowed.

That was enough to make the whole city look sharper for half a lane.

I passed a fruit stall.

Did not buy anything.

Private money stayed upstairs.

Household money stayed household.

No magical purchase became necessary because paper had moved.

I was becoming insufferably responsible.

At home, Cala was slicing roots.

Lyssa sat near the window with cloth in her lap.

Not the brown cuff.

Different cloth.

I did not ask.

Tavi had returned to the spoon.

"Magic?" Lyssa asked.

"Yes."

"Move?"

"Once."

Cala looked up.

"Good?"

I thought about Hessa.

"Cleaner."

Lyssa frowned.

"Cleaner magic?"

"Cleaner answer."

That was closer.

I set the shoulder bag on the table and took out the narrow scrap.

The old lines from attempt seventy-two were still there.

I unwrapped the charcoal.

This time writing made sense.

Not because something had to count.

Because numbers were part of the work.

Below the older line I wrote:

73 NEAR / STILL OFF / NONE

74 NEAR / STILL ON / CLEAN AWAY

75 NEAR / STILL ON / NONE

NEAR 3 / 7 CLEAN

Then, smaller:

STILL FIELD IMPROVES OBSERVATION.

NOT PROOF IT IMPROVES EFFECT.

I looked at that last sentence.

It was annoyingly Hessa.

I kept it anyway.

Lyssa leaned enough to read.

"Good."

"Which part?"

"Not proof."

I looked at her.

"You have joined them."

"Who?"

"People preventing me from becoming interesting."

She smiled.

Tavi hit the table with the spoon.

"Paper move."

I looked at him.

"Who told you?"

"You."

"I said magic."

"Paper."

Lyssa pointed at me.

"You talk."

A devastating accusation.

I wrapped the charcoal again.

No new sheet used.

No new appointment.

No new job.

No money.

Magic totals changed, but the house did not.

Three copper and one bit still upstairs.

Three clean gray sheets still unused.

Front door still worked.

Rain shelf still worked.

My left leg remained comfortable.

The shoulder bag still closed.

I sat back.

The narrow scrap now held a better sentence than the one I had wanted.

Not proof.

That had once felt like failure.

In my first life, power had made certainty cheap.

Cast the spell.

Break the wall.

Kill the thing.

Heal the wound.

Watch what happened.

Enough force could make a question simple.

This life kept handing me effects too small for that luxury.

A paper square.

A grid line.

Four pale threads.

One blue bead.

One movement.

Then none.

I had to learn what the old version of me had often skipped.

How to be satisfied when the only thing that improved was the quality of the doubt.

That evening, I opened the bag again once.

Not to add anything.

Just to read the three new lines.

Seventy-three.

Seventy-four.

Seventy-five.

One clean.

I closed the bag.

The paper had moved.

The question had not.

For now, that was better.

# CHAPTER 305

## THE CLEAR LANE

The first mirrorhorn I ever saw nearly got me killed because I drew a sword.

The first mirrorhorn I saw in Carrow nearly got somebody else killed because he did the same thing.

Experience was efficient when it arrived forty years early.

I heard the shouting before I reached the lane.

Not panic yet.

The sharper thing that came just before panic, when several people had noticed a problem and none of them had agreed what kind.

A cart bell rang twice.

Someone yelled, "Back."

Someone else yelled, "Don't back."

A dog began barking with the full confidence of an animal contributing nothing.

I came around the corner and stopped.

The lane ahead had filled with animals that were not there.

Six mirrorhorns stood between the dye row and a broad stone warehouse.

I counted nine.

Then eleven.

Then six again.

The herd itself was easy enough to see if I watched the feet.

Four adults.

One younger animal nearly adult size.

One calf tucked too close to the largest female.

Everything above their knees was less reliable.

Mirrorhorns were built like lean mountain deer scaled up until carts became a relevant comparison. Long bodies. High shoulders. Narrow gray heads. Two backward-sweeping horns on the adults, dark at the base and almost clear at the tips.

The magic lived around those horns.

Light bent badly there.

Each animal carried two or three pale copies offset around its real body, transparent enough to see through and solid enough to fool the first glance. When the herd shifted, the copies lagged half a heartbeat and caught up.

Beautiful.

Terrible for traffic.

A fruit seller had abandoned half her stall.

Two carts sat nose to nose because neither driver could decide whether forward or backward was worse.

People had pressed into doorways and against walls.

Good.

Mostly.

A young man in a brown lane coat stood near the front cart with a short sword already out.

Bad.

Very bad.

The mirrorhorn female nearest him raised her head.

Her copies widened.

Three gray heads became five.

The young man stepped back.

Sword up.

I said, "Put that away."

He did not hear me.

Or chose not to.

The largest male turned slightly.

His horn tips caught sunlight.

For one instant the whole lane flashed white.

Several people shouted.

The dog barked harder.

The young man lifted the sword.

"Put it away," I said again, louder.

He looked at me this time.

Then at my crutches.

Then back at the herd.

Excellent.

Nothing improved a dangerous situation like deciding whether the cripple had earned the right to speak.

An older woman beside the first cart snapped, "Do it, Renn."

That helped.

Renn lowered the sword a little.

"These are in the city."

"Yes."

"They'll charge."

"Maybe, if you keep making the lane smaller."

That got his attention.

The woman beside the cart looked at me.

Gray braid.

Green work coat.

One hand still on the reins of a broad black mule that had decided complete stillness was a religious duty.

"Mirrorhorns?" she asked.

"Yes."

She looked at the herd again.

"Thought so."

Good.

Someone else knew the category.

I preferred worlds where I was not the only adult.

The woman said, "Never seen them this far in."

"Neither have I."

True enough.

In Carrow.

Renn pointed the sword toward the animals without extending his arm.

"What do we do?"

"First, put that away."

He looked at the older woman.

She said, "Renn."

He sheathed it.

The largest female lowered her head by an inch.

Not relaxed.

Less interested.

Better.

"Now kill the bright lamps," I said.

A shopkeeper behind me said, "What lamps?"

I pointed with my chin.

Three charm signs were still lit under the awnings despite the morning sun.

Blue-white.

Sharp.

One over a cloth shop.

One over a spice seller.

One little rotating bead above a moneychanger's shutter.

The mirrorhorn copies sharpened every time one of the animals turned toward them.

Not because mirrorhorns hated light.

Because bright fixed points gave the field more edges to throw around.

The herd saw its own false bodies too.

Too much reflected motion made nervous animals more nervous.

The old caravan woman who taught me that had used fewer words.

"Dark the damn lamps, boy."

I had been twenty-three the first time.

Older than this body.

Much younger than the memory inside it.

The cloth seller reached up and covered her charm with a wooden cup.

The spice seller twisted his sign bead dark.

The moneychanger was not present.

Renn crossed to the shutter.

"Can I touch it?"

"Turn the hood," somebody said.

Not me.

Local knowledge.

Renn found a black metal hood above the rotating bead and pulled it down.

The lane changed.

Not dramatically.

The mirrorhorns remained six animals surrounded by bad arithmetic.

But the copies softened.

The male's extra shoulders blurred.

One of the females stopped stepping sideways.

"Good," I said.

Renn looked at me.

"What now?"

"Open the middle."

He stared at the lane.

"It is open."

"No. It has two carts in it."

The two cart drivers looked offended.

I pointed.

"That one back half a length. Other one take the left mouth if you can do it without bells."

The second driver said, "Can't turn with them there."

"Then don't."

He looked ready to argue.

The gray-braided woman said, "He said don't."

I liked her.

The first cart was hers.

She backed the mule with two quiet clicks of her tongue.

No bell.

No shouting.

The mule had apparently been waiting for a plan and approved of this one.

The cart rolled backward.

Half a length.

Then another foot.

The clear strip between the herd and the lane mouth widened.

One mirrorhorn copy slid through the cart.

The real animal did not.

Useful distinction.

The dog continued barking.

I looked for it.

Small yellow thing tied outside a butcher.

Naturally.

The butcher had both hands on the leash and was trying to pull the dog farther inside.

The dog considered this cowardice.

"Take it through the back if you have one," I said.

The butcher said, "Door's blocked."

"Then cover its head."

He looked at me.

"With what?"

"You sell meat. Surprise me."

The gray-braided woman laughed once.

The butcher grabbed an apron from a peg and dropped it loosely over the dog's head.

The barking stopped.

Not because the dog became wise.

Because it became busy trying to understand cloth.

The nearest mirrorhorn's ears moved forward.

Then sideways.

The copies narrowed.

Renn saw that.

"So they're scared?"

"Uncertain."

"Difference?"

"Scared things run. Uncertain things decide."

"And if they decide wrong?"

"Then we have a worse morning."

He did not like that.

Neither did I.

Mirrorhorns were not harmless.

The adults weighed enough to break ribs without magic.

The horns were real under the light tricks.

A herd pressed in a narrow road could go from still to catastrophic before a person finished explaining that the animals were usually shy.

Neutral was not safe.

That lesson had cost people blood in my first life.

Sometimes mine.

I shifted farther into the stone doorway behind me.

Not retreating.

Positioning.

My right foot had a flat patch of stone.

Both crutch tips sat inside the threshold where they would not skid if I had to move fast.

Fast remained relative.

That mattered.

In my first life, if the herd broke, I could have cleared a shop awning before the first animal crossed the lane.

Now I needed the doorway to do some of that work for me.

Old experience.

Current geometry.

Same survival problem.

The calf moved.

It stepped from behind the large female and toward the abandoned fruit stall.

The copies followed badly.

One calf became three.

The fruit seller made a sound through her teeth.

"Leave it," I said.

She froze.

The calf sniffed a fallen yellow pear.

One of its false heads passed through the stall leg.

The real head lowered.

Renn said, "It's eating."

"Maybe."

The calf touched the pear with its nose.

Then jumped backward as if the pear had insulted its ancestors.

The crowd laughed.

Small.

Nervous.

Too loud.

The largest female snapped her head toward us.

Her copies vanished.

Every one of them.

My stomach dropped.

"There," I said.

Not loudly.

The gray-braided woman heard the change in my voice.

"What?"

"Nobody move toward her."

Renn said, "Copies are gone."

"Yes."

"Isn't that good?"

"No."

The female stood completely real.

Gray hide.

Dark legs.

Clear-tipped horns.

No doubled neck.

No false shoulders.

Nothing.

Mirrorhorns mirrored when they wanted the world to believe there were more of them.

They stopped when they had chosen to use the body they actually possessed.

The first time I had seen that happen, I had thought the magic was ending.

I had drawn my sword.

The caravan woman had hit my wrist with a stick.

Hard.

Then a male mirrorhorn had crossed thirty feet fast enough to bury a horn in the wagon where my chest had been.

I remembered the sound better than the pain.

Wood opening.

Canvas tearing.

My own breath after.

The woman saying, "Copies mean thinking. One means done thinking."

Forty years later, I finally appreciated the teaching method.

"Eyes down," I said.

Renn looked at me.

"Don't stare at her face. Give her the road."

The gray-braided woman lowered her gaze first.

Not submissive.

Practical.

Others followed because somebody had to be first.

I did too.

I watched the female's front feet instead.

Real hooves.

Real shadow.

Her right forehoof shifted.

Forward.

Stopped.

The calf moved back behind her.

The female's copies returned.

One.

Then two faint shoulders beside the real one.

I breathed again.

Renn whispered, "That was charge?"

"That was considering it."

"How do you know?"

"Because we're still talking."

He looked like he wanted a better answer.

He was not getting one while six mirrorhorns occupied the lane.

The male turned toward the open strip the carts had created.

Good.

The herd wanted somewhere to go.

That was the whole problem.

People saw dangerous animals in a city lane and thought the animals had chosen the lane as a destination.

Usually an animal wanted through.

Food.

Water.

Cover.

Herd.

Nest.

Mate.

Escape.

The road was often just in the way.

The male took one step.

Then another.

His copies flowed around him.

The first female followed.

The younger animal shifted.

The calf stayed tight to the dangerous female.

Renn moved his hand toward the cart as if to push it farther back.

"Don't," I said.

He stopped.

"Why?"

"Because they started."

That was enough.

Once a mirrorhorn herd accepted a corridor, changing the corridor was a fine way to make them reconsider.

Reconsideration was what we had spent the last several minutes avoiding.

The male walked.

Not ran.

Walked.

Through the middle of Carrow.

His shoulder came level with the first cart.

The mule closed its eyes.

I respected that strategy.

The male's nearest false body passed through the mule's nose.

The mule twitched but did not bolt.

The real mirrorhorn passed three feet away.

Its hide was not smooth.

Short gray hair lay over darker scales along the shoulder line.

I had forgotten the scales.

Or maybe this regional kind had more.

Not important now.

The horn tips held a thin inner glow, pale as morning moonlight.

No one spoke.

The second animal followed.

Then the younger one.

A child started crying somewhere behind a doorway.

Quietly.

Good child.

The fruit seller stayed absolutely still while the calf approached the fallen pear again.

This time it bit.

The pear vanished in two bites.

The seller watched her inventory leave the market.

I whispered, "Expense of doing business."

She made a strangled sound that might have been laughter.

The large female came after the calf.

Her copies brushed both sides of the lane.

The real animal stayed in the middle.

Exactly where we had left space.

She passed the fruit stall.

Passed Renn.

Passed me.

Closer than I wanted.

Her real head turned.

One dark eye found the doorway.

Found me.

For half a second every false head turned too.

Four eyes.

Six.

Ten.

I did not move.

The inner light in her horn tips brightened.

Not attack.

Attention.

I knew the difference.

Mostly.

She smelled like wet stone and crushed leaves.

Then she kept walking.

I waited until her hind feet cleared the first cart before breathing normally.

The last female followed.

No charge.

No broken stall.

No blood.

Six real animals.

At least eighteen opinions about where they had been.

They reached the far lane mouth.

The male stopped there.

Renn tensed.

I said, "Wait."

The herd bunched.

The copies thickened again.

Beyond them, a delivery wagon had turned into the cross street.

The wagon driver saw the mirrorhorns and did the smartest thing anyone had done all morning.

He stopped.

Backed.

Disappeared.

The male continued.

One by one the herd followed him out of the lane.

The calf was last.

It had somehow acquired a second pear.

The fruit seller whispered, "Thief."

I said, "You may file a complaint."

Then the last false gray tail slipped around the corner and vanished.

Nobody moved.

For three breaths.

Four.

The dog escaped the apron and barked once.

The entire lane shouted at it.

The dog sat down.

Civilization restored.

Renn looked at me.

Then toward the cross street.

"That's it?"

"Probably."

"Probably?"

"They are animals, not a signed contract."

The gray-braided woman relaxed her grip on the mule's reins.

The mule opened one eye.

She patted its neck.

"Good girl."

The mule had contributed more than most of us.

The two carts started negotiating who moved first.

The fruit seller picked up a basket.

The butcher removed the apron from his dog and called it several names I did not think belonged to the animal at birth.

Shop lamps came back on.

The lane reassembled itself.

That was how you knew a danger had been ordinary enough.

People complained before the adrenaline finished leaving.

Renn remained where he was.

He looked young now that the sword was away.

Maybe twenty.

A little older than this body.

Much younger than me in every way that mattered and several that did not.

"What are they doing here?" he asked.

"I don't know."

He blinked.

"You knew all that."

"I know mirrorhorns."

"Not why they're here."

"No."

The gray-braided woman said, "North road herd sometimes comes near the old lime fields."

Renn looked at her.

"This isn't the lime fields."

She looked around at the buildings.

"Strong observation."

Then she added, "Could be gate work. Could be noise. Ask drovers if you need the why."

There.

I knew the animals.

She knew the nearby route.

Neither of us owned the whole answer.

Renn rubbed his mouth.

Then looked at me again.

"What's your name?"

There it was.

Attention.

Small.

Ordinary.

Still attention.

"Greg."

"Greg what?"

"Greg remains sufficient."

The gray-braided woman laughed.

Renn did not.

"Lane watch keeps incident names."

"Then write yours."

He stared at me.

I stared back.

He sighed.

"Renn."

"Greg," I said again.

Renn studied me.

Then nodded.

"Where'd you learn mirrorhorns?"

That question was worse.

Not because I lacked an answer.

Because I had too many.

Northern salt roads.

Caravans.

A military supply route where frightened recruits killed a calf and turned a harmless crossing into injuries.

Decades.

None available to nineteen-year-old Greg.

"Travel stories," I said.

Not a complete lie.

Stories had traveled.

Some of them inside me.

The gray-braided woman looked at me.

She knew that answer was thin.

She was polite enough not to pull it.

Renn was not.

"That's more than stories."

I shrugged.

"Good stories."

He almost smiled.

Then someone called his name from the far end of the lane.

He turned.

A second lane watchman stood there gesturing toward the cross street.

Renn looked back at me.

"Stay?"

"No."

"We might need what direction they went."

"You can see the road."

"What if they stop again?"

"Then keep people out of the middle and find somebody who handles them for a living."

He frowned.

"You're not a handler?"

"No."

That mattered.

Old Greg had handled enough creatures to fake competence past the edge of wisdom.

Current Greg had two crutches, no rope, no animal gear, no authority, and no need to convert one useful memory into a profession.

Renn nodded reluctantly.

"Fine."

I left before fine became a job.

The gray-braided woman called after me.

"Greg."

I turned.

"Good call on the lamps."

"Good call on the cart."

She lifted one hand.

Fair trade.

I continued down the lane.

My hands were shaking slightly.

I noticed only after the first corner.

Not fear exactly.

After-fear.

The body's complaint filed once the meeting had ended.

I stopped beside a wall.

Right foot flat.

Crutches planted.

Breathed.

No pain.

No problem with the left leg.

No issue with the crutches.

Just blood still expecting six large animals to return through stone.

Old experience did not remove that.

Maybe it should not.

The first-life memory came back while I stood there.

Twenty-three.

Sword half drawn.

A mirrorhorn male becoming one animal instead of four.

The old caravan woman striking my wrist hard enough to make me swear.

"Copies mean thinking," she had said.

I had called that superstition.

Then the horn went through the wagon.

Afterward she made me help repair the canvas.

She never explained the magic.

She taught behavior.

That had been enough to keep me alive.

Years later I learned more.

Not everything.

Mirror fields changed with light, crowding, age, probably bloodline.

None of that mattered as much as the sentence I had almost ignored.

Copies mean thinking.

One means done thinking.

I had carried that for decades.

Today it had worked.

Not approximately.

Not maybe.

Not an uncertain future memory bent by forty years.

The animal had dropped its copies.

I had known what came next.

We had given it road.

It had chosen road.

I was fucking right.

The satisfaction arrived under the leftover shaking.

Warm.

Sharp.

Dangerous in its own way.

I did not need to sand it down into uncertainty.

I did not know why the herd was in Carrow.

I did not know whether the local route had changed.

I did not know whether the darker shoulder scales were regional or simply something I had forgotten.

Those uncertainties could live.

The behavior I knew.

That could live too.

I pushed away from the wall and kept walking.

At home, Cala was hanging a cloth under the gallery.

Lyssa sat at the table with a seam spread over one knee.

Tavi had found the bad-bean bowl again.

No one looked as if six mirrorhorns had recently occupied a commercial lane.

I opened the door.

Cala looked at me.

"You late."

"Animals."

She waited.

"Mirrorhorns."

Her eyebrows rose.

"Where?"

"Dye row."

Lyssa looked up.

"Inside?"

"Very."

Cala came away from the cloth.

"Six?"

I looked at her.

"How do you know six?"

"Someone came through."

Of course.

Carrow remained faster than walking.

Lyssa said, "She said eight."

"Six real."

Tavi said, "Eight."

"Do not join taxonomy."

Lyssa smiled.

Cala asked, "They hurt?"

"No."

"Cart?"

"No."

"Good."

That was the whole safety report she needed.

Lyssa looked at me more carefully.

"You did something."

I set the bag down.

"Possibly."

"You have that face."

"What face?"

"The one after Hessa says you were right about one small thing."

I was offended by the accuracy.

"Different authority."

Cala sat.

So I told them.

Not every detail.

The sword.

The bright lamps.

The open middle.

The female losing her copies.

The herd passing.

The calf stealing two pears.

Tavi immediately adopted the important part.

"Pear deer."

"Mirrorhorn."

"Pear deer."

"Incorrect."

"Two pear."

Lyssa laughed.

Cala did not know the mirrorhorn charge sign.

She knew people sometimes saw them north of the lime fields.

Same as the cart woman.

Lyssa had seen one once as a child from far enough away that somebody had lifted her onto a wall to watch.

"They were pretty," she said.

"They are."

"And bad?"

"Not bad."

"Dangerous."

"Yes."

She nodded.

No contradiction.

Cala asked, "Who was lane watch?"

"Renn."

She nodded.

Lyssa said, "He ask you?"

"Name. Where I learned it."

"What say?"

"Good stories."

She smiled.

"Bad answer."

"Excellent survival answer."

Tavi put the bowl on his head.

"Pear deer hat."

We had lost the room.

I went upstairs later and looked at the narrow scrap.

Seventy-three.

Seventy-four.

Seventy-five.

Near three in seven clean.

Still field improves observation.

Not proof it improves effect.

I did not add mirrorhorns.

I did not need to write down knowledge I had carried longer than this body had been alive.

That was new too.

Not all useful knowledge was a discovery.

Some of it was mine already.

I put the scrap back in the bag.

The three copper and one bit remained where I had left them.

No payment for the lane.

No reward.

No Guild note.

No theatre paper.

No new job.

Downstairs, Cala was telling someone at the door that yes, Greg had seen them and no, Greg was not a mirrorhorn handler.

I closed my eyes.

Attention traveled faster than caution.

Then Cala called up, "Greg."

I leaned over the rail.

"What?"

A woman I did not know stood in the doorway.

Not frightened.

Not official.

Holding a basket of onions.

She looked up at me.

"Mirrorhorns really stop copying before charge?"

There it was.

Not a job.

Worse.

A question.

I could have said maybe.

I could have protected myself with uncertainty until every useful edge went blunt.

Instead I said, "Yes. If the whole animal goes single and holds still, give it space. Do not test it."

The woman nodded.

"Good."

She left.

Cala looked up at me.

I looked down at her.

"That is how this starts."

"What?"

"People asking things."

Cala shrugged.

"They ask."

"Terrifying."

She went back to the kitchen.

By evening, nobody else came.

The lane stayed open.

No herd appeared at our door.

Renn did not send paper.

My debt remained exactly as unresolved as it had been that morning.

My magic remained thirty-six, thirty-two, seventy-five.

Nothing had changed on the page.

Something had changed anyway.

For weeks, most of my useful knowledge in Carrow had come from learning the city.

A price.

A route.

A local rule.

A repair.

A name.

Today the city had presented something I already knew better than the people nearest me.

Not because they were fools.

Because I had lived longer than I looked.

Because I had survived the mistake once.

Because some knowledge did not rot when the calendar moved backward.

At dusk, I stepped into the household court.

The charm lamp above the gallery came on.

Blue-white.

Sharp.

For half a second the light caught on the rim of a water cup and split into three pale reflections.

My body reacted before thought.

Then I laughed.

No horns.

No herd.

Just a cup.

I left the lamp on.

The road outside stayed clear.

# CHAPTER 306

## THE BAD TOOL

Three copper had been enough money to make me curious.

That was the problem.

Curiosity had survived poverty by becoming theoretical.

Once I could afford things, it developed hands.

The morning after the mirrorhorns, nobody came to ask me about mirrorhorns.

This was excellent.

Cala made porridge.

Lyssa worked near the window with pale thread and dark cloth that I did not recognize and did not need to recognize.

Tavi had converted a spoon into a horse.

Ordinary civilization resumed.

I ate.

Then I went upstairs and looked at my money.

Three copper.

One bit.

The bit stayed where it was.

One copper stayed with it.

I took two.

Not because I had an errand.

Because I had decided to look at bad tools.

This was not a responsible sentence.

The lower market had several kinds of used goods, but the salvage edge was different from the ordinary clothing, pots, chairs, bags, and household things farther in.

Salvage meant somebody had already decided the object had failed one life.

Warehouse fittings.

Cart hardware.

Lengths of treated chain.

Bent brackets.

Old charm housings with the beads removed.

Beads with the housings removed.

Rope blocks that had spent years hanging over something heavier than me.

Cracked ward plates sold as metal instead of magic.

The broad categories were familiar.

The exact marks were not.

Good.

That was what markets were supposed to be.

I had spent enough of my first life around adventurers to know the ugly truth about equipment.

The useful thing was rarely the thing labeled useful.

A perfect military buckle could be useless for climbing.

A terrible wagon brace could save a bridge.

A cooking charm could become an excellent way to ruin a lock if a person had no respect for cookware or property.

Most of those discoveries had already happened by the time Old Greg encountered them.

Some had not.

That was the interesting part.

I moved slowly along the salvage line.

Not searching for treasure.

That was how people bought polished garbage.

I looked for constraints.

A copper-framed warming plate with one corner melted.

No.

A pair of old door wards that only matched each other and neither matching door.

No.

A brass pulley with a blue load bead split neatly through the center.

Beautiful corpse.

No.

A box of six light hooks whose glow surfaces had gone cloudy.

Possible repair problem.

Still no.

I had two copper in the inside pocket of my coat.

That was enough to buy something regrettable.

I intended to preserve the opportunity.

Then I saw the holdfast.

Palm wide.

Black iron.

Three folding teeth tucked under a round base.

A thick ring through the top.

One dull amber bead sat off-center under the ring, almost hidden by old grime.

No release tongue.

I stopped.

The woman behind the tarp looked at me.

She had short gray hair and forearms marked with old rope burns.

"Don't," she said.

I looked at the holdfast.

"Strong sales technique."

"You stopped at the bad one."

"That usually means interesting."

"Usually means bad."

Fair.

The holdfast lay among ordinary cargo fittings.

Two iron strap eyes.

A bent chain guide.

Three hook plates.

A narrow brass tension block with no bead.

The holdfast was the only thing with active magic still inside it.

Probably.

"Fixed hold?" I asked.

The woman looked at me more carefully.

"You know them?"

"Old ones."

"Old is right."

She picked it up by the ring.

The three folding teeth clicked against each other underneath.

No rust at the joints.

That mattered.

The iron body had been oiled recently.

"Warehouse pullout," she said. "North storage. They replaced the wall eyes. This was in a bin with the dead fittings."

"Dead?"

"Mostly."

She tapped the amber bead with one nail.

"This bastard still works."

Better sales technique.

I leaned closer.

A shallow maker's mark sat on the underside of the ring mount.

Three short lines.

One long line crossing them.

I knew that mark.

Not from a museum.

From being wet, tired, and very angry in a ravine while a rescue line depended on two pieces of hardware I had insulted for an hour.

Forty holds.

That was what people called them later.

Not the real product name.

The name that survived.

You set one against stone, timber, or another surface solid enough to take its bite.

Twist the ring.

The teeth seated.

The bead took hold.

For a short fixed interval, the thing became extremely difficult to move relative to whatever it had accepted as its surface.

Then it released.

Whether you wanted it to or not.

That last part had killed the cargo market.

A cargo anchor that could not be released early was bad.

A cargo anchor that definitely released itself later was worse.

Carters liked their loads attached until they chose otherwise.

Warehouse crews had similar opinions about walls.

Years after this time, people stopped pretending the old fixed holds were cargo equipment.

Rescue crews used them.

Climbers used them.

Dungeon teams used them.

Anyone who occasionally needed one point in the world to stay one point for long enough to do something stupid somewhere else.

I had used them.

A lot.

Not this one.

Not this exact unit.

But the mark and the missing release tongue put it in the old family I remembered.

My pulse picked up.

This was not Hessa's paper.

No uncertainty about whether I had once seen the category.

I had trusted my life to the damn things.

The woman said, "You going to stare or ask?"

"Both, if pricing allows."

"Two copper."

I stared at her instead.

"For the bad one."

"For the working one."

Also fair.

"No early release?"

"None."

"Warning before it lets go?"

"Three blinks if the bead isn't filthy."

"How long?"

She shrugged.

"Long enough to annoy you. Short enough to ruin cargo."

"Technical."

"Depends heat. Depends what it bites. Depends how long it sat cold. Old book said forty breaths."

There it was.

Forty holds.

Not because they always held forty breaths.

Because somebody had printed forty once and people enjoyed lying with numbers.

The woman pointed behind her stall.

A thick plank was bolted upright between two posts.

Its surface held scratches from tests.

"Want to see?"

"Yes."

She carried the holdfast over.

I followed.

Not under her hands.

Not near the teeth.

Old habits survived too.

She folded the three teeth outward.

Pressed the base flat against the plank.

Then twisted the top ring a quarter turn.

The amber bead lit.

Not bright.

A deep orange point under the iron.

The teeth moved.

Not much.

They bit into the wood with three dry clicks.

The woman took her hand away.

The holdfast stayed on the vertical plank.

She pulled the ring.

Hard.

Nothing.

She put both hands on it and leaned back.

The plank creaked.

The holdfast did not move.

"Works," she said.

"Load?"

"No idea."

"Ever fail under test?"

"Not with me. I don't test stupid."

A philosophy.

"How much is stupid?"

"More than my body."

Reasonable ceiling.

She stopped pulling.

The orange bead stayed steady.

I watched.

Counted breaths automatically.

Then stopped counting.

My breathing was not a clock.

Hessa had damaged me.

The woman saw me looking at the bead.

"Watch."

Eventually the steady orange dimmed.

Then flashed.

Once.

A pause.

Twice.

Shorter pause.

Three.

The woman stepped away from the plank.

So did I.

Two breaths later, the bead went dark.

The three teeth released together.

The holdfast dropped.

The woman caught the ring before the iron hit the ground.

"That's the bad part."

"That's the useful part."

She stared at me.

I felt the old memory open fully.

A ravine in spring flood.

A broken stair cut into black rock.

Water rising below.

Two fixed holds biting into a stone wall while six people crossed a rope line one at a time.

The warning blink on the first while the last man was halfway across.

Me shouting at him to stop climbing and swing.

Him objecting.

Me expressing a professional opinion about his parents.

The hold releasing exactly when we knew it would release.

The rope dropping free after everyone was safe.

No one had needed to go back for hardware under a waterfall.

The fixed release had not been a defect that day.

It had been the reason we could leave.

Another memory.

A mine brace.

A cracked beam.

One hold used long enough to slide a proper support under it.

Another.

A wagon wheel in mud, not as an anchor but as a temporary line point while three people pulled sideways.

Bad cargo tool.

Excellent temporary certainty.

The seller shook the holdfast once.

"Useful for what?"

I came back to Carrow.

"Anything that needs to stay where it is for a little while and absolutely does not need to stay there forever."

"That's not an answer."

"Rescue line. Temporary brace. Short pull. Controlled crossing."

She considered the holdfast.

Not stupid.

Not impressed.

"And when it lets go under load?"

"Everyone should already be done."

"Should."

"That word has killed more people than magic."

She laughed once.

Then handed me the iron.

I did not take it immediately.

"Cooldown?"

"Long."

"How long?"

"Never tried twice same hour."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because I wasn't going to believe a precise answer anyway."

She narrowed her eyes.

I liked her.

I took the holdfast.

Heavier than it looked.

Maybe three pounds.

Not enough to trouble the shoulder bag by itself.

Enough that I would not want three of them.

The folded teeth sat safely under the base.

The ring was thick enough for rope or a hook.

The amber bead remained dark and slightly warm from activation.

I turned it over.

Three short marks.

One long across them.

No doubt.

The mark mattered less than the design.

Later versions had release tongues.

Better for most people.

Worse for some uses.

The old blind holds had fewer moving parts and a habit of staying committed until the timer ended.

I had once trusted one because a newer release tongue had jammed full of sand.

Future knowledge did not always mean better technology.

Sometimes it meant knowing which obsolete failure you preferred.

I gave the holdfast back.

The seller raised an eyebrow.

"No?"

"Thinking."

"Do it away from the tarp."

I stepped aside.

Two copper.

Two-thirds of my private copper.

Three copper had taken several jobs to accumulate.

One had already become the shoulder bag.

That purchase solved a daily need.

This solved nothing today.

No client required it.

No household repair required it.

No theatre job required it.

Hessa had not asked for it.

The Guild had not approved it.

It did not make my own magic stronger.

It did not pay Vale.

It did not buy food.

It was not even guaranteed to be safe under the loads I remembered from later versions.

Different unit.

Old age.

Unknown service history.

Unknown threshold.

Two copper.

I could walk away.

I should probably walk away.

Then another memory arrived.

Old Greg at forty-eight, watching a young rescue crew open a crate of modern fixed holds.

One of them complaining that the new release tongues made the body too bulky for narrow stone seams.

Me telling him the old blind ones had been better for that.

Him laughing because by then old blind holds were collector junk and nobody issued them anymore.

I remembered the shape of this one.

The plain ring.

The three teeth.

The missing release tongue.

The ugly orange bead.

I did not remember a market price.

I did not remember this seller.

I did not know whether this exact piece would survive another fifty activations or five.

But I knew the job it could do.

That was enough.

I went back.

"Two copper."

The seller held out her hand.

I gave her two coins.

She bit neither.

Different merchant philosophy.

She turned them once in her palm, then put them into a small iron box.

"Yours."

"Any return?"

"If it kills you, no."

"If it doesn't?"

"Also no."

Clear terms.

She wrapped the holdfast in a square of old sailcloth and tied it with thin cord.

Not gift wrapping.

Finger protection.

"Keep teeth folded," she said.

"Yes."

"Don't turn ring while it's sitting flat against anything you care about."

"Yes."

"Don't put your fingers under it after set."

"Yes."

She looked at me.

"You really do know them."

"Enough to be afraid correctly."

"Good."

She handed me the bundle.

I put it in the shoulder bag.

The bag settled lower.

The stay bead on the first notch gave one faint green blink and held the strap position.

The extra weight was noticeable.

Not bad.

I walked ten steps.

Turned.

No swing into either crutch.

The seller watched from the stall.

"Bag holds?"

"So far."

"Good bag."

"One copper."

"You paid one for that?"

"Yes."

She looked offended on behalf of commerce.

"Good price."

I continued before she could sell me something else.

The remaining private copper and one bit were upstairs.

That felt different now.

Three copper had been a small pile.

One copper was a decision already made.

The walk home became more expensive with every step.

Not because the holdfast grew heavier.

Because I had crossed a line.

Until now, most of my private spending had been easy to defend.

The bag carried things while I used crutches.

Fried dough had been fried dough and required no theory.

This was speculation.

Not financial speculation exactly.

Capability speculation.

I had bought a tool before I had a job for it because I believed the constraint was more valuable than the market around me believed.

That could become very smart.

It could also become a shelf ornament that cost two copper.

Both outcomes deserved respect.

At home, Cala noticed the bag first.

"Heavy."

"Yes."

Lyssa looked up from the table.

Tavi looked up because everyone else had.

I set both crutches carefully against the wall, sat, and took the wrapped holdfast from the bag.

Cala looked at the bundle.

"What?"

"Bad tool."

Tavi said, "Bad."

"Thank you."

Lyssa put down her thread.

"How bad?"

"Two copper."

Silence.

Not angry silence.

Accounting silence.

Cala said, "Yours."

"Very much mine."

"Good."

Household money remained household money.

Private money remained private money.

That did not protect me from judgment.

Lyssa pointed at the bundle.

"Show."

I untied the sailcloth.

Did not unfold the teeth.

Did not place the base flat on the table.

The seller's warning had been excellent.

I held it by the ring so they could see.

Cala frowned.

"Anchor?"

"Temporary holdfast."

"For what?"

"Originally cargo, probably. Bad at cargo."

Lyssa looked at me.

"You bought bad cargo anchor."

"Yes."

"Two copper."

"That part remains unfortunate."

Tavi reached.

I lifted it higher.

"No."

"Bad."

"Correct."

Cala pointed at the amber bead.

"Works?"

"Seller tested it."

I explained the fixed interval.

Sets hard.

No early release.

Three warning blinks.

Then it lets go whether the user approves or not.

Cala's face flattened as the explanation continued.

"Bad anchor."

"For cargo."

Lyssa said, "For what good?"

That was the question.

I could not say ravines forty years from now.

I could not say military rescue teams that did not exist yet in the form I remembered.

I could say the logic.

"Temporary brace. Pull point. A place for a rope when you know you only need it for a short time. Something you want fixed now and gone later."

Lyssa considered that.

She understood tools through cloth, fit, tension, sequence.

"Like basting."

I looked at her.

"Exactly like basting if basting could break your arm when the stitch decided time was over."

She smiled.

"Bad basting."

"Powerful basting."

Cala said, "Bad."

Tavi said, "Bad."

Democracy had spoken.

I rewrapped the holdfast.

Lyssa asked, "Need it now?"

"No."

"Know you need later?"

"No."

"Then why buy now?"

I looked at the bundle.

Because I knew what it became.

Because I remembered trusting a version of it when newer tools failed.

Because useful knowledge had finally collided with enough money to act on it.

Because three copper felt like waiting and one copper felt like choosing.

I said, "Because it does one thing very well, and tools get expensive after people agree what the one thing is for."

Lyssa watched me.

Not convinced.

Not dismissive.

"Maybe."

That was fair.

Cala said, "Where keep?"

Good question.

Not near Tavi.

Not on the table.

Not flat against a wall with the ring exposed where someone could twist it by accident.

We put it upstairs on the high shelf above my folded clothes, still wrapped, ring turned sideways, with the base resting on the sailcloth rather than directly against wood.

I added a small scrap tag from an already-used edge of paper.

DO NOT TURN RING.

Tavi could not read it.

This was less reassuring than writing usually was.

I moved the bundle farther back.

Then farther again.

No activation.

No home test.

The seller had already demonstrated that this unit set and released once.

I had no reason to punch three teeth into Cala's furniture for science.

Hessa would probably have approved of that sentence.

Possibly after insulting the purchase.

I did not take the holdfast to the Guild.

It was not part of my magic protocol.

I did not take it to Rell.

I did not ask Jori what it was worth.

I did not walk back to the salvage market looking for three more.

One expensive bad idea was enough for a day.

In the afternoon I went out without money.

This felt wise after the fact.

The city behaved normally.

No bridges collapsed in need of temporary anchors.

No carts required rescue.

No warehouse beam cracked in front of me.

No client appeared with exactly the problem I had just spent money preparing to solve.

Good.

The world did not owe me immediate justification.

I passed a crew raising a shop awning.

They used rope, a wooden cleat, and two people who knew their work.

Better than my holdfast.

I passed a delivery man bracing a handcart on a slope with an ordinary stone under the wheel.

Cheaper than my holdfast.

I passed a woman tying a load to a roof rack with three loops of plain rope.

More reusable than my holdfast.

This was healthy.

A specialized magical tool did not make every mundane solution obsolete.

That was how people ended up carrying fifty pounds of enchanted nonsense to solve problems that required string.

Old Greg had done that too.

Several times.

Current Greg was carrying nothing but his normal shoulder bag.

The bad tool stayed home.

At supper, Cala did not mention the two copper.

Neither did Lyssa.

That made it worse in a mature way.

I mentioned it myself.

"I have one copper and one bit left."

Cala said, "I know."

Of course she knew.

Lyssa said, "Rich."

"Thank you."

"Very rich."

"Cruel household."

Tavi said, "Bad."

"You have one word now."

"Door red."

He had two.

After supper I went upstairs and looked at the shelf.

The wrapped holdfast sat exactly where I had left it.

A terrible cargo anchor.

An excellent temporary certainty.

Maybe.

The seller's unit had held her plank.

That was established.

It had warned before release.

Established.

It had released on its own.

Very established.

Its safe load was unknown.

Its cooldown was unknown.

Its remaining service life was unknown.

Whether it would behave on stone the way I remembered later units behaving was unknown.

Whether I would ever need it was deeply unknown.

But the category was not.

The use was not.

This was different from buying a mystery because I hoped to solve it.

I had bought a known bad interface because I knew a job where the bad interface became useful.

That did not make the purchase correct.

Not yet.

It made the bet legible.

I took out the narrow scrap.

Below the magic notes, I wrote:

FIXED HOLDFAST.

2 COPPER.

NO EARLY RELEASE.

THREE WARNING BLINKS.

SAFE LOAD UNKNOWN.

DO NOT TEST ON HOUSE.

I considered writing FUTURE USEFUL.

I did not.

That was not evidence available to anyone but me.

Instead I wrote:

USEFUL ONLY IF RELEASE TIME IS PART OF PLAN.

Better.

I put the charcoal away.

The three clean sheets remained untouched.

The horse page remained full.

My magic remained thirty-six, thirty-two, seventy-five.

The mirrorhorns remained somewhere else.

My debt remained unresolved.

No one had paid me today.

I had paid somebody else.

Two copper.

That fact sat heavier than the iron had.

I looked at the shelf again.

For the first time since I started earning private money in Carrow, I had spent most of a small pile on something that might matter later.

One copper left meant fewer mistakes available.

That was good too.

Possibility without consequence was just browsing.

The bad tool had made the consequence real.

I blew the charcoal dust from my fingers.

Downstairs, Lyssa laughed at something Cala said.

Tavi shouted, "BAD DOOR RED," combining his education.

I put the scrap back in the bag.

The holdfast stayed on the shelf.

Not treasure.

Not an upgrade.

Not profit.

A tool with the wrong job.

For now, that was enough.

# CHAPTER 307

## THE RED POT

The holdfast remained on the high shelf all morning. For two copper, it was excellent at not helping.

I left it there.

By late afternoon, Cala had found a more immediate use for me. She stood beside the kitchen table with a clean gray scarf tied over her hair and pointed at onions, two carrots, a tied bundle of greens, a bowl of soaked beans, and the red cooking pot waiting with its lid upside down beside it.

"Dinner."

I looked at the ingredients, then at her. "Is dinner arriving?"

"You are making it."

Tavi looked up from the floor, where three wooden blocks and a spoon had become something involving a horse. "Greg cook."

"Do not encourage her."

Cala was already putting on a different blouse, which concerned me more than the vegetables. The gray scarf had narrow yellow stitching along one edge. Not house clothes. Not errand clothes. She was going somewhere.

"Where are you going?"

"Out."

"I had gathered that from the advanced scarf."

She lifted a covered bowl from the counter. "Cards."

I stared at her. Cala played cards. Apparently entire civilizations had continued in this house while I was busy surviving them.

"With who?"

"People."

"Specific."

"Yes."

Tavi nodded as if this settled everything. "Cards."

Cala pointed at him. "You eat what Greg makes."

His face changed. I had never seen a child understand betrayal so quickly.

"Now," I said, "we have a reasonable witness."

Cala ignored me. "Beans already soaked. Pot already filled. Greens last. Salt after boil. Red pot on left stone."

"I know how to make stew."

She looked at me.

I corrected myself. "I understand the theory of stew."

"Red pot on left stone. Ear to wall."

The pot had two short side handles. I looked at them. "Both ears exist."

"The cracked one."

One handle had a hairline split near the base, bound years ago with dark wire. "Why?"

Cala was already at the door. "Because."

Excellent instruction.

She opened it, stopped, and looked back toward the hearth. "Half heat. Not full."

"I know a heat stone."

"I know you know a heat stone."

That was somehow more insulting.

A knock came before I could answer. Cala opened the door wider. An older woman stood there with a flat wooden box under one arm and a green shawl looped over the other. She looked past Cala at me, then at the table.

"You left him cooking?"

"Yes."

The woman looked at Tavi.

"Greg cook," he said.

She winced.

I had not even touched an onion yet. "This household has very little faith."

The woman laughed. Cala did not. She handed Cala the shawl, took the covered bowl from her, and they left together without explanation, invitation, or concern that I might wonder who the woman was.

The door closed. Tavi and I looked at each other.

"All right. We have been abandoned."

"No."

"Good correction. We have been trusted."

He considered that. "No."

Fair.

The kitchen hearth was built low into the brick wall beside the counter. Three dark heat stones sat under iron plates, each fitted inside a clay ring with a small brass tongue at the front. Ordinary household work. I had used versions in inns, camps, guild kitchens, field tents, and one deeply regrettable mountain station where every stone had been set too hot by a cook who believed boiling was a moral quality.

Cala's left stone was old. Its black center had gone gray around the edges, and one amber line glowed brighter than the others when I pushed the brass tongue halfway in. That did not mean the stone was broken. It meant the stone was old. Household equipment was allowed to age without becoming a case.

I moved my chair closer to the table before I started. Cala had already done the important geometry for me. The pot held water but remained on the counter within easy reach of the hearth. The knife, board, vegetables, and salt were all at the table. She had not announced any accommodation. She had simply put the heavy things where I did not need to cross the room carrying them while using two crutches.

That was how the house worked now.

I sat with my right foot flat and my left knee clear under the table. Both crutches leaned against the wall within reach, angled so neither could slide. Tavi climbed onto the chair opposite me.

"You are not helping with the knife."

"I help."

"Not with the knife."

"Carrot."

"You may inspect the carrot."

He picked up the smaller carrot and held it to one eye. "Good."

"Professional opinion?"

"Good."

"Excellent. Put it down."

He did not.

I cut the onions first. This was not a mistake in principle. Humanity had survived onions. The mistake was believing I could cut them while Tavi narrated the carrot.

"Long."

"Yes."

"Orange."

"Strong work."

"Horse."

"No."

He put the carrot between two blocks. "Cart."

"That is better engineering."

The knife was dull enough to irritate me and sharp enough to punish carelessness. Normal knife. I liked normal knife. By the time I finished the vegetables, Tavi had built a stable around the remaining whole carrot and was charging the spoon admission.

I stood using the table edge and one crutch, then took the second. The pot was already full enough to be awkward, so I did not carry it across the kitchen. I shifted it six inches from the counter onto the left hearth plate with both hands while standing close, my right hip against the counter and both crutches braced behind me. Then I stepped back, resettled my grips, and checked the position.

The cracked ear faced the room.

Cala had said wall.

I turned the pot.

Tavi nodded. "Red pot."

"You knew that?"

"Red pot."

"That does not answer the question."

"Red pot."

I had once negotiated with a duke whose entire political method had been roughly similar.

I pushed the brass tongue halfway. The amber lines in the heat stone brightened, and dry stored warmth rose through the iron plate. No flame. No smoke. Nothing worth investigating. I knew the category. I did not know Cala's stove. That distinction had become useful often enough that I no longer resented it on principle.

I added the onions.

The first hiss came from the front side of the pot. I leaned close enough to feel the heat pattern against my knuckles. The plate ran harder along one quadrant, not dangerously, just unevenly.

So that was why the cracked ear went to the wall. With the pot turned that way, its deeper side sat over the hotter section.

Household science. Discovered by Cala through the advanced method of living here.

I stirred until the onions softened, then got distracted by the front door.

Lyssa came in with her hair partly loose and a small crease between her eyebrows. She carried no parcel, basket, or cloth bundle. Just herself and the posture of somebody who had spent a long day leaning over work.

She stopped when she smelled the kitchen. "Cala?"

"Cards."

Lyssa looked toward the hearth, then at me, then at Tavi.

Tavi pointed. "Greg cook."

Lyssa set one hand against the doorframe. "Oh."

I stared at her. "That sound was unnecessary."

"No."

"You haven't tasted anything."

"I can see."

"Hostile house."

She washed her hands at the basin and sat at the table without offering to take over. I appreciated that. I also resented it. Both could be true.

Then I remembered the onions.

They had gone past soft. Not black. Brown. Aggressively brown.

I stirred.

Lyssa leaned over my shoulder. "Burnt."

"Caramelized."

"Burnt."

"Those are adjacent categories."

She took one piece from the spoon, blew on it, ate it, and made a face. "Burnt."

Tavi joined from the floor. "Burnt."

"Wonderful. A panel."

Lyssa sat again while I added carrots, beans, and the onions to the pot.

"Greens later," she said.

"Cala said."

"Salt after boil."

"Cala said."

"Cracked ear to wall."

I turned slowly.

She smiled.

"You all know this?"

"Red pot."

From the floor, Tavi repeated, "Red pot."

There were conspiracies in Carrow. I had found one.

The stew began to warm. I returned to the table because standing beside a pot did not make it boil faster and did make my shoulders work for no reason. Lyssa took the carrot from Tavi's stable, broke the end off, ate it, and handed the rest back.

He stared at the missing piece.

"Tax," I told him.

Lyssa looked at me. "No."

"Strong government."

"No."

Tavi put the carrot behind his back.

Good citizen.

For a while nothing happened. That was the difficult part of cooking.

Old Greg had understood meals as objects that appeared after money, orders, or camp labor by people with more patience than me. Even when I cooked in the first life, it usually involved meat, fire, one pan, and the principle that hunger improved standards. Stew expected waiting.

I did not like systems where the correct action was nothing.

Hessa would have approved. I did not tell her.

The heat stone gave a faint click as one amber line shifted. Lyssa glanced over.

"Half."

"It is half."

"Then leave."

"I am leaving."

"You are staring at it."

"I am supervising."

She closed her eyes.

Tavi moved his blocks across the floor one at a time, extending the road under the table. I looked at the pot, then away. Lyssa's foot touched my right shoe under the table. Not a signal. Just there. Her eyes stayed closed.

I let my shoulder rest against hers.

The stew began to boil without my help.

Rude.

I stood again, lowered the brass tongue slightly, and watched the amber lines dim. Then I added the greens and a small pinch of salt. Cala had left the salt dish small on purpose. This was wise.

I stirred. The stew smelled better now. The burnt onion had become part of the argument rather than the winner.

Lyssa came over and tasted the broth from the spoon.

"More salt."

"How much?"

She pinched some between two fingers. "That much."

"This is not a unit."

She dropped it in. "Now it is."

I stirred and tasted.

Better.

I added nothing else.

Tavi wanted bread. There was bread. Of course there was. Cala had left half a round loaf under cloth near the window.

I had been assigned dinner in the same way a theatre worker might be assigned to move one chair after the entire stage had already been built.

Still counted.

We ate at the table. Tavi got the shallow bowl because he liked the shallow bowl. Lyssa took the chipped blue one. Mine was plain brown.

The first bite was hot enough to punish enthusiasm. Tavi blew on his. Lyssa did not. She had apparently accepted pain as efficient.

I watched her chew. "Well?"

She took another bite. "Food."

"That is a category, not a judgment."

"Food."

Tavi said, "Food."

I had created a coalition government.

The stew was decent. Not good enough to become family history. Not bad enough either. The onions were slightly bitter at the edges, the carrots softer than I preferred, the beans held, and the greens had not dissolved into green rope.

I took another bowl.

Lyssa did too.

Tavi did not. He ate bread instead and dipped it into the broth until the bread stopped being structurally honest.

Halfway through supper, somebody knocked. Tavi immediately said, "Cala."

It was not Cala.

A girl maybe twelve stood outside holding an empty covered bowl. I had seen her in the lane before but did not know her name.

"Cala?"

"Out."

She handed me the bowl. "For her."

I took it with one hand while bracing the door with my shoulder. "From?"

She looked confused by the question. "Us."

Useful.

"Excellent. I will preserve the chain of custody."

She had already turned away.

I shut the door. Lyssa looked at the bowl.

"Pickled beans."

"How do you know?"

"Smell."

I lifted the lid.

Pickled beans.

She did not explain who had sent them. Neither did Cala later. The bowl went on the counter.

After supper, Tavi became impossible. This was not caused by the stew. He had reached the hour when children transformed stored sunlight into bad decisions. He ran the spoon along the table edge until Lyssa took it, crawled under my chair, asked whether Cala was back six times, announced that the carrot was sleeping, and then lay on the floor beside it.

I washed bowls because this put me at a safe distance from parenting philosophy.

The basin was low enough that I could brace my right hip against the counter and work one-handed when needed. I stacked clean bowls on the cloth to my left. Lyssa dried because there were wet bowls and she knew where they went.

Tavi dragged his block road through the kitchen.

"Not under the crutches."

He moved the road three inches.

"More."

Three more.

"That works."

He accepted the engineering revision.

Lyssa handed me the plain brown bowl. "You can cook."

"Careful."

"Once."

"Thank you."

"Maybe twice."

"Now you're reckless."

She smiled and put the bowl away. The crease between her eyebrows was gone. I noticed and left it alone.

Cala returned after dark.

We heard her before the key. Laughter in the lane, not Cala alone. Two voices, then three, then somebody said something that made her laugh again. The voices continued past the house after she came in.

She closed the door with her hip and took off the gray scarf.

Tavi ran at her. "Cala!"

She caught him against one side. "Eat?"

"Greg cook."

Cala looked at me. "Alive?"

"All casualties are accounted for."

Lyssa said, "Burnt onion."

Betrayal.

Cala went to the pot. There was enough left for one bowl. She tasted it standing.

I waited.

This was worse than Hessa watching paper.

Cala took another bite. "Salt good."

Lyssa looked offended.

I sat straighter. "Thank you."

"Onion burnt."

I collapsed internally.

Tavi shouted, "BURNT."

Cala ate the whole bowl.

That was the only review that mattered.

She saw the covered bowl on the counter. "Oh."

That was all.

"Pickled beans," I said.

"I know."

"Of course."

She put them away.

I had questions.

I asked none.

Cala rinsed her bowl. I had already cleaned the rest. She noticed, said nothing, put hers on the drying cloth, and told Tavi it was bed.

He argued. She ignored the argument and carried him upstairs under one arm while he explained that the carrot also needed bed.

Lyssa leaned against the table.

"Good night?"

"For Cala?"

"Yes."

"Apparently."

"You sound surprised."

"I did not know she played cards."

"You don't know a lot."

"Specific."

"No."

She took my hand. "Come."

We went upstairs. The holdfast was still on the high shelf. Its warning tag hung from the sailcloth, and one copper and one bit remained where I had left them.

I did not touch any of it.

Lyssa sat on the edge of the bed and pulled out the pins holding the rest of her hair. I sat beside her.

"Burnt onion," she said.

"Go to hell."

She laughed, and I kissed her.

Downstairs, Tavi shouted, "CARROT BLANKET."

Cala said, "No."

A pause.

"Small blanket."

Lyssa put her face against my shoulder and laughed hard enough to shake the bed.

I had no answer for that.

# CHAPTER 308

## THE DEBT HOUR

The note arrived while I was eating the last of yesterday's bread.

Not the last bread in the house.

The last piece I had personally claimed before Tavi noticed it.

Important distinction.

He was standing beside my chair looking at the crust when somebody knocked.

Cala opened the door, took a folded square of paper from a boy, and handed it to me.

The seal was plain.

Vale office.

I knew that before I opened it because some kinds of dread developed handwriting.

Tavi pointed at the bread.

"Mine."

"No."

He pointed at the note.

"Mine."

"Also no."

He considered the two refusals and decided the bread remained the stronger legal case.

I unfolded the paper.

Greg,

Possible debt credit, if wanted.

Vale office. Ground floor.
One hour.
Seated.
Materials at table.
Cart covered.
Task: compare returned-goods tags against warehouse receipt list.
Mark MATCH, MISMATCH, or UNREADABLE only.
Do not assign cause.
Credit: one copper.

Reply yes or no.

Antonius.

I read it twice.

Not because the terms were complicated.

Because one copper existed in two places now.

One copper upstairs, privately mine.

One copper offered against money I already owed.

Same denomination.

Different direction.

The holdfast on the high shelf had made that distinction louder.

I had spent two copper because I believed an old bad tool would become useful later.

Vale did not care what I believed.

Vale cared what I owed.

Reasonable institution.

Annoying institution.

Cala watched me.

"Work?"

"Debt."

She nodded once and went back to cutting something green.

No lecture.

No concern about whether I should have bought the holdfast.

No accounting ceremony.

The two copper were already gone. The debt was already there.

The note was today's problem.

I looked at the terms again.

Ground floor.

Seated.

One hour.

Materials ready.

Transport covered.

Exact task narrow enough to fit inside a sentence.

Mark three categories only.

Do not assign cause.

Antonius had learned how to prevent me from becoming myself.

I found charcoal.

The boy waited at the door.

I wrote on the back:

YES.
ONE HOUR.
IF MATERIALS ARE READY AS STATED.

I almost added a question about whether the returned-goods tags were attached to the goods.

Stopped.

That was not required to accept the task.

I handed the note back.

The boy left.

Tavi took my bread.

I watched him walk away with it.

"That was theft."

"Mine."

Cala said, "You were reading."

Apparently lapse of attention transferred property.

Carrow law remained flexible.

*

The cart came before second bell.

Not a carriage.

A small two-seat hire cart with a broad step and a driver who had already turned the seat cushion sideways before I reached the door.

I knew the maneuver.

I had used Vale-covered transport before.

That did not make getting into a cart graceful.

Nothing made getting into a cart graceful.

I set one crutch inside, turned, used the frame and right leg, kept the left knee clear, and lowered myself onto the near side. The driver took the second crutch only after I handed it to him.

Good.

People who waited for the handoff were better than people who grabbed.

The shoulder bag stayed with me.

Inside it: narrow scrap, wrapped charcoal, nothing heavy.

The holdfast stayed on the shelf.

It had cost two copper and could contribute by remaining safely unemployed.

The ride to Vale's office was short enough that walking would have been possible.

That was not the point.

Transport had been included in the terms.

Taking it did not make the debt credit smaller.

Also, my shoulders were allowed to spend one hour not moving me through Carrow before spending another hour doing Vale's work.

Old Greg would have called this obvious.

Current Greg still occasionally treated saving effort like cheating.

Stupid habit.

The Vale building looked exactly as respectable as debt should.

Stone front.

Clean windows.

Brass plate beside the door.

No gargoyles.

A missed opportunity.

The driver brought the crutches around. I stood, settled both grips, and went inside.

Ground floor meant ground floor.

Antonius had not lied in the note.

Excellent beginning.

The side room already had a table, two chairs, a shallow wooden tray, a ledger, and nine flat goods tags laid in a row.

Nine.

The note had not promised a count.

Good.

A narrow green counting strip sat along the far edge of the table with six little brass sliders. Ordinary office equipment. I ignored it because it was not my task.

Antonius entered carrying a second ledger.

He wore dark gray today.

No coat drama.

No visible concern that I owned less money than three days ago.

"I expected a question," he said.

"I wrote one."

"You wrote a condition."

"Better."

He put the second ledger down and sat opposite me.

I looked at the nine tags.

Thin hardwood, each about two fingers wide, each with a string hole and a stamped number near one end. Some had ink words. Some had scratches. One had a corner missing.

No goods.

"Tags only?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because if you put damaged merchandise in front of me, I would start having opinions."

"I know."

There was no triumph in his voice.

Just accounting.

Antonius turned the first ledger toward me.

One page had nine receipt entries.

He tapped the top.

"Returned goods from East Store. The tags came back in one envelope. Warehouse copied the receipt list before the goods were dispersed for inspection."

"Dispersed where?"

"Not your task."

"Excellent."

He pointed to three small boxes ruled beside each receipt line.

"M. X. U."

"Match. Mismatch. Unreadable."

"Yes."

"What counts as match?"

"Receipt number and tag number agree. Written item family agrees where both are legible."

"Quantity?"

"Not on tags."

"Condition?"

"Not on tags."

"Maker?"

"Not part of this check."

"Cause of mismatch?"

"Not part of this check."

He had prepared for me.

I felt respected and attacked.

"What if number agrees and item word is partly legible?"

"Use judgment only on whether the word can be read. Do not infer missing letters from the receipt."

There.

Actual work.

Not solve the universe.

Read what existed.

I pulled the first tag closer.

Antonius slid a short pencil across the table.

I looked at it.

"Vale supplies tools now?"

"Return it."

Cruel.

The first tag read 442.

Receipt line: 442.

Tag word: BLUE GLASS.

Receipt: BLUE GLASS SLEEVES.
