The word sleeves did not appear on the tag.

"Match?"

Antonius said nothing.

Right.

My task.

The item family agreed.

I marked M.

Second tag: 447.

Receipt: 447.

Tag: SMALL BRASS CAPS.

Receipt: BRASS CAPS, SMALL.

M.

Third tag had a dark smear over the last digit.

44-something.

Receipt said 451.

The item line on the tag was clear enough: WINDOW PINS.

Receipt: WINDOW PINS.

I stared at the number.

There was a vertical stroke.

Could be one.

Could be seven with the top lost under the smear.

Could be somebody's fingernail dragged through wet ink and created false confidence for nineteen-year-old idiots.

I marked U.

Antonius watched without commenting.

Fourth tag.

Receipt 453.

Tag 453.

Item: GREEN SEAL CLOTH.

Receipt: GREEN SEAL CLOTH.

M.

Fifth.

Receipt 455.

Tag 455.

Item line on the tag had been written twice.

The first words were crossed out.

RED BINDING.

Below:

BROWN BINDING.

Receipt said BROWN BINDING.

I nearly marked M.

Then stopped.

The task was whether the returned tag matched the receipt.

It did.

The crossed-out red line did not change the readable current label.

M.

Sixth tag.

Receipt 458.

Tag 458.

Receipt item: LAMP HOODS.

Tag item: LAMP HOOKS.

I read both again.

Hoods.

Hooks.

One letter.

Different goods.

X.

Antonius moved one brass slider on the green strip.

I looked up.

"What was that?"

"Nothing you need."

"Now I need it more."

"No."

I returned to the tags.

Seventh.

Receipt 461.

Tag 461.

Tag item: TWO-HOLE PLATES.

Receipt: TWO-HOLE PLATES.

M.

Eighth.

Receipt 463.

Tag 436.

Both said TWINE RINGS.

Easy X.

Too easy.

I marked it.

Ninth.

The broken-corner tag.

Receipt 468.

The tag number looked like 468.

Maybe.

The eight was stamped close to the broken edge.

Part of the lower loop remained.

Enough?

I turned the tag toward the window.

Antonius waited.

The item word was clear.

WICK CLIPS.

Receipt: WICK CLIPS.

I could reconstruct the eight because the receipt wanted an eight.

That was exactly what he had told me not to do.

I marked U.

Done.

Nine tags.

Five M.

Two X.

Two U.

I counted.

Then counted again because human beings had invented errors before they invented ledgers.

Five M.

Two X.

Two U.

Antonius looked at the page.

"Why sixth mismatch?"

"Hoods versus hooks."

He checked.

"Yes."

"Eighth is 463 versus 436."

"Yes."

He tapped the third.

"Unreadable."

"Last digit smeared."

"Ninth?"

"Enough of the eight is missing that I would be using the receipt to complete the tag."

Antonius nodded.

Then pointed at the fifth.

"Why match?"

"Current readable line says brown binding. Receipt says brown binding."

"The crossed line?"

"Red binding. Crossed out."

"Could indicate relabeling after return."

"It could indicate a clerk changed the label before lunch because red and brown were on adjacent shelves."

"Which happened?"

"I have no idea."

"Good."

He moved another brass slider.

I hated the strip now.

"What is that counting?"

"Finished checks."

"Mine?"

"Office."

"That is less interesting."

"Correct."

He gathered the first ledger but left the tags.

I looked toward the small sand glass on the shelf.

Less than half the sand had fallen.

"That's it?"

"That is the first pass."

Of course.

He opened the second ledger.

"Now copy your three categories into this return sheet."

"I thought the warehouse already copied the list."

"They copied receipt lines. You are adding comparison result."

"Why not mark the first ledger?"

"Because the first ledger stays intact."

Reasonable.

He turned the sheet toward me.

Nine lines.

I copied carefully.

Not fast.

My handwriting remained legible when I refused to race it.

Line six.

X.

Line eight.

X.

Line three and nine.

U.

The rest M.

I finished and checked each against my first marks.

Antonius had not spoken for several minutes.

This was one of the things I liked about him.

He could let silence be billable.

When I was done, he took the return sheet and compared it to the first ledger himself.

Not because he distrusted me personally.

Because paying for a check did not eliminate the need to check the check.

Also reasonable.

He stopped at line five.

"Brown binding."

"Yes."

He picked up the tag.

Turned it over.

On the back, faint pencil:

R TO B.

I had not looked at the back.

My stomach sank half an inch.

"Was I supposed to?"

Antonius said, "No."

I waited.

He looked at the note he had written for the task.

"Tag number and written item family. It does not say front only."

"That sounds like yes."

"It sounds like my instruction was incomplete."

That was not the answer I expected.

He turned the other tags over one by one.

Most were blank.

The eighth had a little warehouse mark.

The third had a thumbprint and nothing else.

The fifth alone had writing.

R TO B.

"What does it mean?" I asked.

"Unknown."

"Red to brown?"

"Possibly."

"Reassigned?"

"Possibly."

"Rewrapped?"

"Possibly."

"Reclassified?"

"Possibly."

I stared at him.

"You enjoy that word."

"It preserves money."

Fair.

"Does it change match?"

Antonius set the tag beside the line.

"The front tag currently matches the receipt. The back creates an unresolved note."

"So U?"

"No."

I frowned.

He tapped the three categories.

"Your task was not whether the return history is fully understood. It was whether receipt number and readable item family match."

"Then M."

"Yes."

"Back note separate."

"Yes."

He wrote a small line in the margin himself.

BACK NOTE: R TO B. MEANING NOT ESTABLISHED.

That was irritatingly familiar language.

I looked at him.

He did not look up.

"Hessa has infected commerce."

"Who?"

"Nobody."

He finished the note.

Then he pushed the tags back toward me.

"Turn all nine. Check only for additional writing. Do not reinterpret the comparison unless the additional writing changes the current readable item family or number."

I did.

Nine backs.

One warehouse stamp.

One number fragment that matched the front number but added nothing.

R TO B.

A charcoal line.

Nothing else.

No category changed.

That took six minutes.

The sand glass was still running.

Antonius gathered the tags and sheets.

"Done."

"Time remains."

"The task is done."

"I can sit competitively."

"I do not credit debt for decorative presence."

Harsh institution.

I leaned back.

My shoulders felt fine.

Hands fine.

No unusual residual-limb pressure.

The cart ride had reduced the day's crutch work enough that one seated hour barely registered physically.

Antonius opened a narrow receipt book.

"One copper credit."

He wrote the date.

My name.

TASK CREDIT: 1 COPPER.

No balance.

He signed it and tore off the small top slip.

I read it.

"Current balance?"

"In the account ledger."

"Not on receipt."

"No."

"Can I see it?"

"Yes."

I almost said yes.

Then stopped.

I knew I owed Vale.

I knew one copper had just moved against it.

The exact remaining total would matter if I was making a payment plan today.

I was not.

I folded the receipt and put it in my shoulder bag.

"Not today."

Antonius closed the book.

That was it.

No speech about responsibility.

No reminder about Bren.

No suspicious visitor.

No hidden offer.

Just one hour traded against old obligation.

I stood.

Right foot under me.

One hand on the table.

Crutches retrieved from the wall.

Antonius waited until I had both grips settled before speaking again.

"You asked for private work before."

I looked at him.

Had I?

Not today.

Apparently old Greg was not the only version of me leaving inconvenient records.

"Cash work," he clarified.

"Yes."

"Do you want it if I have something suitable?"

There it was.

My one copper upstairs became suddenly visible in my head.

So did the one bit beside it.

"Depends on the task."

"Obviously."

"And rate."

"Obviously."

"And whether it conflicts with debt labor."

"That is a real question."

Good.

I waited.

Antonius folded his hands on the table.

"If Vale offers you a cash task, it will be separate from debt credit. You may accept or refuse. I will not convert a debt-credit task into cash after the fact because you prefer the purse."

"Separate lanes."

"Yes."

"Hostile office."

"Do you want suitable cash work offered or not?"

"Yes."

"Fine."

"That is not a standing obligation."

"No."

"Not weekly."

"No."

"Not first refusal."

"No."

"Not exclusive."

Antonius looked tired now.

Good.

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"If I have a task, I will send terms. You can say no."

"Excellent."

"I regret asking."

"Also excellent."

He stood.

Meeting apparently over.

The cart was still covered because transport had been part of the one-hour task rather than charged by the minute.

I left with a debt-credit receipt and exactly the same private money I had arrived with.

This was less emotionally satisfying than being handed a coin.

That did not make it less real.

Annoying.

*

On the ride home, we passed the lower market.

Not the salvage edge.

A fruit line.

A woman had small yellow pears stacked in a shallow tray.

I considered buying one.

Then remembered I had no money on me.

Not poverty.

Planning failure.

Different problem.

The one copper and one bit were upstairs because I had not expected to need them at Vale.

I watched the pears pass.

No tragedy occurred.

At home, Cala was folding the gray scarf from yesterday.

Tavi had put the carrot back into ordinary food status. Progress.

Lyssa was not there yet.

Cala looked at me.

"Paid?"

"Debt."

"How much?"

"One copper credit."

She nodded.

"Money?"

"Still one copper and one bit upstairs."

Another nod.

No contradiction.

One number had moved in a ledger I did not carry.

Another had not moved at all.

I took the Vale receipt upstairs and put it under the existing narrow scrap rather than writing anything new.

The holdfast sat on the high shelf above it.

Two copper spent.

One copper credited against debt.

One copper and one bit still liquid.

These facts did not combine into a lesson.

They were just different directions money could go.

Lyssa came in later with a small paper twist of roasted seeds.

She ate three before I could ask where they came from.

Then she held the twist toward me.

I took some.

"Work?" she asked.

"Vale."

"Paid?"

"Debt."

She chewed.

"Still paid."

"Cala said the same thing without words."

"Cala is efficient."

I ate the seeds.

Salt.

Something bitter underneath.

Good.

"Antonius may send cash work if something suitable appears."

Lyssa looked at me.

"May."

"Correct."

She held the paper twist away when I reached again.

"Those cost money."

"I have money."

"Not with you."

Cruel accuracy.

I checked my pockets anyway.

Nothing.

She laughed and gave me three more seeds.

No invoice.

For now.

# CHAPTER 309

## THE LAST CHIME

The sky looked wrong before breakfast finished.

Not dangerous wrong.

Expensive wrong.

There was a difference.

The clouds over Carrow had flattened into one low gray sheet, bright at the edges and dark underneath. No wind reached the lane. Laundry hung without moving. The little paper twist someone had tied to the neighbor's upper rail pointed straight down.

Cala looked through the window while spreading something pale over bread.

"Charge rain."

Tavi looked up. "Rain."

"Different rain," Cala said.

He accepted this because adults were allowed to ruin words.

I knew charge weather. Broad category, anyway. I had spent enough first-life marches under badly behaved skies to know that when the air began tasting like iron and cheap charm beads started whining, a person stopped treating the weather as decorative.

Carrow's version was apparently arriving before noon.

I finished eating.

Cala looked at my shoulder bag.

"You going?"

"I was."

She looked at the sky again.

That was not a prohibition.

It was worse.

Judgment without instruction.

"I can walk before it starts."

Cala spread more pale paste on the bread. "Can."

"That sounded hostile."

"It was a word."

Lyssa had already left for work. Tavi was building something beneath the table with two cups and a wooden horse. The fixed holdfast remained upstairs where it had spent several days proving that two copper could buy an impressive amount of shelf occupation.

One copper and one bit remained beside it.

Yesterday, one other copper had gone somewhere else entirely, into Vale's ledger instead of my hand.

I did not need money today.

I did not need anything today.

That made leaving simpler.

I put on the shoulder bag, took both crutches, and stepped outside.

The first thing I noticed was not the sky.

It was the roofs.

Three houses down, a woman had lowered a black braided cord from beneath her eave and hooked its iron end into the street gutter.

Across from her, a shop boy was doing the same thing with two shorter cords.

At the corner, somebody had already shuttered a bright blue charm sign even though the shop remained open.

Carrow was preparing.

I knew the logic.

Ground active surfaces. Close exposed bright work. Give stray charge somewhere cheaper to go than through whatever a person cared about.

The exact roof cords were local.

Old Greg had seen copper chains, wet ropes, silver mesh, carved stone sinks, iron trees, and one very expensive tower system whose designer had claimed lightning understood geometry.

Lightning had disagreed.

I walked south.

No errand.

No client.

No appointment.

I had discovered that leaving home without needing to become useful was possible, and I intended not to turn that discovery into a philosophy every time I did it.

So I walked.

The air grew heavier by degrees. Hair along my forearms lifted. A bakery charm over one doorway flickered from yellow to white, then immediately went dark when the baker reached up with a hooked pole and shut its cover.

Nobody screamed.

Nobody asked what the omen meant.

Two people complained about laundry.

Civilization endured.

At the wider lane, vendors were tying canvas lower than usual. A fruit seller moved three shallow trays away from a metal drain cover. A courier with a long leather case under one arm hurried past me, then slowed at the next corner when she saw the wet-black shine beginning on the stones.

No rain had fallen yet.

That shine was charge settling.

Very thin.

Almost nothing.

Enough.

A brass bell rang somewhere ahead.

One long note.

People looked up.

Not alarm.

Confirmation.

The fruit seller said, "First."

Her neighbor said, "I heard."

That gave me one local fact.

I did not write it down.

The first drop hit my right sleeve.

Cold.

Then another struck the stone and flashed pale violet.

Ah.

Proper charge rain.

Not the worst kind.

Probably.

That word belonged to weather.

The second bell came before I had reached the next covered crossing.

Two shorter notes this time.

The lane changed immediately.

Canvas fronts came down.

A woman carrying a baby turned back toward the nearest arcade.

Two men with a handcart stopped under a stone overhang instead of trying to beat the rain.

The courier I had seen earlier ducked beneath a bakery loading arch and leaned her leather case against the dry wall.

I had perhaps thirty yards to the next deep cover.

The rain began.

Not hard.

That was what made it strange.

Thin silver lines fell almost straight through the still air. Each drop hit wood, stone, cloth, skin, and disappeared like ordinary water.

Metal was different.

The edge of a hanging sign snapped blue.

A gutter chain glowed white for half a heartbeat.

The iron rim around a street drain ticked three times.

I changed direction.

Rushing on crutches across wet stone to prove I could reach the farther shelter before magical weather became more magical was an excellent first-life decision.

I had already had one first life.

I crossed toward the bakery arch.

The courier moved her case farther aside before I reached it.

No announcement.

No awkward invitation.

She saw the width of two crutches and gave me the dry route.

I liked her immediately and knew nothing about her.

The arch covered a recessed loading space wide enough for six people if everyone accepted that personal space had been temporarily nationalized.

We became seven.

Courier woman.

Two handcart men.

The woman with the baby.

A bakery worker with flour on both forearms.

An older man sitting on an overturned crate.

Me.

The rain silvered the whole lane.

The bakery worker pulled a thick black cord from a wall hook, clipped it to a brass stud beside the arch, then dropped the weighted end into a narrow stone slot at the curb.

A faint blue line ran down the cord and vanished.

Local ground line.

He looked at all of us.

"Nothing bright out."

The courier opened her coat enough to show him a dull red bead sewn near the shoulder.

"Covered."

One handcart man held up both empty hands.

The other said, "Cart's wood."

"Wheel pins aren't."

"They're under."

The bakery worker shrugged. His building, his rule, his level of concern.

I checked myself.

No active personal charm.

Shoulder bag stay bead.

That one mattered.

The old brass bead sat in the strap at first notch, mostly covered by leather and coat. It had never shown anything except a faint green blink when settling the bag.

I knew the broad category well enough not to invent danger from presence alone.

Still, charge rain was not the time to trust "probably fine" merely because an object had behaved itself yesterday.

I shifted the bag forward and looked.

Dark.

No hum.

No warmth.

I left it alone.

The older man on the crate noticed.

"Stay bead?"

"Yes."

"Cheap ones chatter."

"Mine has better manners."

"Give it time."

Fair.

Rain tapped the stone beyond the arch.

Not loud.

The city made more noise than the weather did.

Tick.

Snap.

A blue thread crawled along the bottom edge of a shuttered sign and died at its ground cord.

Farther down, somebody shouted, "Cover that!"

A different voice shouted something rude back.

Then a metal clang.

Then nothing.

The baby woke.

Of course.

She began crying with the dedicated fury of somebody who had not agreed to atmospheric magic.

Her mother bounced her gently.

"Yes," she said. "Sky bad. Terrible sky. Personally offensive."

The baby did not accept the apology.

The courier woman smiled.

One of the handcart men said, "Third before noon?"

The bakery worker looked out at the sky. "Maybe."

The older man said, "No."

"You own clouds now?"

"I own knees."

This earned a laugh from the flour-covered worker.

The older man rubbed one knee.

"Pressure's wrong for long rain."

I had heard worse forecasting systems.

Some had belonged to royal armies.

I sat on the other end of the loading ledge. It was low, broad, and dry. Right foot flat. Left knee clear. Crutches against the wall where nobody needed to step over them.

The decision to shelter had cost me nothing except time.

That still occasionally felt suspicious.

Old Greg had spent too many years treating delay as something inflicted by enemies, injury, bad roads, or incompetence. Weather delay had usually meant calculating lost daylight, exposed camp, monster movement, supply distance.

Current Greg was three streets from home.

If the rain lasted an hour, the beans would remain beans.

My nonexistent appointment would survive.

The bakery worker disappeared inside and returned with a shallow ceramic dish full of gray ash.

He put it near the wall.

The courier said, "For what?"

"Anything starts singing."

The older man nodded.

Ordinary precaution.

A ceramic dead dish with ash or dry grit was common enough in charged workplaces. Not because ceramic was magical. Because sometimes the safest response to a small misbehaving charm was to put it somewhere nonconductive, stop touching it, and let someone competent deal with it later.

The first singing object arrived less than a minute afterward.

Not mine.

One of the handcart men swore and reached under his shirt.

The bakery worker snapped, "Don't."

The man froze.

Good worker.

Good instruction.

A thin green tone came from somewhere near his belt.

"Pocket cool bead," he said.

"Take the coat off."

"It's tied."

"Then don't touch the bead."

The man looked irritated.

That was better than panic.

He unfastened his belt instead, slowly, keeping fingers away from a thumb-sized green bead tied into the side of a leather food pouch.

The bead gave another high note.

The bakery worker used a dry wooden bread paddle.

Not his hand.

He lifted the pouch by the leather strap, lowered the whole thing into the ash dish, and stepped back.

The green tone stopped.

Nobody applauded.

Excellent.

The handcart man said, "Lunch."

The other man said, "Was."

The bakery worker said, "It might be fine."

"You're paying if it cooks my cheese."

"No."

Commercial law moved quickly under pressure.

I knew enough to have done roughly the same thing.

That did not make it my turn to explain.

The bakery worker lived with this building and its weather procedures. He had the dish ready before the bead sang.

Local expertise did not require a lecture from the dead S-class man under the arch.

I watched the rain.

A pale flicker moved through it.

Not lightning.

Too diffuse.

The whole street brightened as if somebody had lifted a white sheet behind the clouds.

Every ground cord I could see glowed faint blue at once.

The baby stopped crying.

All seven of us looked outward.

Then the light passed.

The baby resumed.

"Reasonable," I said.

Her mother laughed tiredly.

The courier looked at me.

"You from north?"

"Why?"

"Accent went north on that word."

I had no idea what my accent did anymore.

"Carrow."

"That's not what I asked."

"Then I answered a better question."

She rolled her eyes.

Good.

Strangers should not automatically enjoy me.

The rain strengthened slightly.

Still not heavy.

More sparks now.

Drops hit the brass rim of the bakery's exterior lamp and made tiny violet crowns. The lamp itself stayed dark behind its cover.

Across the lane, a painted shop board twitched.

I blinked.

The board twitched again.

Not physically.

The painted red fish on it moved its tail once.

Then twice.

The older man sighed.

"Again."

The bakery worker looked across.

"Old paint charm."

"Thought they scraped it."

"They scraped the letters."

The fish opened one painted eye.

The mother with the baby pointed.

"That is horrible."

The courier said, "Kids love it."

"I am not a kid."

The fish swam three inches through painted wood, hit the edge of the board, and flattened into a red smear.

Then it reappeared at the other side and continued.

Nobody treated this as impossible.

Charge weather woke old, weak, badly isolated, or half-dead charm work all the time in places where people had not paid enough to remove every trace.

I had seen worse.

Once, in a military storehouse, a charge front had convinced twelve training mannequins that they were under attack.

The mannequins had won.

I kept that memory to myself.

A small boy appeared at the far end of the lane, running.

Every adult under our arch stiffened.

Not because of the boy.

Because he was holding something bright.

A spinning blue hoop.

Toy charm.

Active.

His father, mother, uncle, kidnapper, or extremely motivated neighbor came after him shouting.

"Drop it!"

The boy did not.

Children were consistent across centuries.

The hoop snapped once.

A thin line of blue ran from it toward a wet iron post.

The boy finally stopped.

Wrong place to stop.

He stared at the line.

The adult behind him slowed too.

"Don't grab him," the bakery worker said quietly.

Nobody under the arch moved.

The adult in the rain heard us or reached the same conclusion.

He stopped three steps behind the boy.

"Put it down, Teren."

Not theatre Teren.

Probably.

The world was permitted more than one person per name.

The boy looked back.

The blue hoop hummed.

"Down."

He crouched and set it on the stone.

The moment his fingers left it, the blue line jumped cleanly to the iron post.

The hoop went dark.

The adult grabbed the back of the boy's shirt and hauled him away from it.

Only then did the bakery worker exhale.

The courier said, "Idiot."

The mother said, "Child."

"Same family."

"Not always."

The older man said, "Often."

The baby chose that moment to sneeze.

Argument ended.

A lane worker came from a side door carrying a long wooden fork and a clay bucket. He collected the dead hoop without touching it and carried it under cover.

No drama.

No prophecy.

No Greg.

Just a city with procedures because magical weather had happened before.

I liked that.

I also liked not being the person standing in the rain.

My right hand had gone slightly stiff around nothing. I opened and closed it.

Fine.

No crutch load while seated.

Shoulders relaxed.

The wet street was going to be the larger problem when we left.

Charge rain plus stone did not change friction in a mysterious magical way.

Rain changed friction perfectly well on its own.

One of the handcart men sat on the ground beside the ash dish.

"How long?"

The bakery worker said, "Until third."

The older man said, "If third."

The courier asked, "What if no third?"

"Then bell man died."

The mother stared at him.

He shrugged. "Or line failed."

"That was not better."

"It was more accurate."

I looked toward the roofline where the bell sound had come from.

"Third means clear?"

The bakery worker shook his head.

"Third means front passed. Last chime means lines checked."

There.

Another local distinction.

Useful.

Not universal.

I knew cities that rang one bell for storm end and expected everyone to discover bad grounding personally.

Carrow apparently preferred one extra step between sky becoming safe and infrastructure becoming trustworthy.

Civilization improved in small bureaucratic ways.

"How long between?" I asked.

"Depends."

Of course.

"What gets checked?"

He pointed upward with his chin.

"Main grounds. Street lamps. water line at east junction. If something stayed hot, they hold the last."

The older man said, "Last year they held half the afternoon."

"South lane."

"Still Carrow."

The bakery worker did not argue.

The rain began to weaken.

Not stop.

The silver lines thinned until individual drops were visible again.

The painted red fish across the street slowed, yawned with a mouth it probably had not been painted with, and froze half outside the original outline.

The courier stared.

"That looks worse."

"It is art," I said.

She looked at me.

"No."

Fair.

The handcart man's cool bead remained quiet in ash.

He crouched beside it.

The bakery worker blocked him with one floury forearm.

"Third."

"I'm hungry."

"Be hungry."

That relationship had developed quickly.

Then the bell rang.

Three short notes.

Everyone listened.

The rain had almost stopped.

The mother shifted the baby higher on her shoulder.

One handcart man reached for the cart.

The older man said, "Last."

The worker said nothing.

They waited.

I would have waited anyway.

Wet lanes.

Crutches.

No appointment.

Sometimes competence was having enough sense not to race seven strangers out of a shelter because the first available signal sounded optimistic.

A minute passed.

Then two.

A woman across the lane opened half a shutter, looked up, and closed it again.

The courier checked the leather case at her feet.

"Paper?"

I asked.

"Patterns."

"For?"

She looked at me.

"Work."

Excellent answer.

I nodded.

She smirked because she knew exactly what she had done.

The city was contagious.

The bakery worker went inside and came back with bread ends in a wooden bowl.

"Old."

Nobody cared.

He passed them around.

I took one.

Free bread was still bread.

The handcart man with the endangered cheese took two.

"One," the worker said.

"Weather damages appetite."

"One."

He put one back.

Rule of law.

The bread was hard at the edge and soft enough inside.

The baby got none because she was a baby and had stronger things to do.

The courier ate while standing.

The older man broke his piece into precise small bites.

Nobody introduced themselves.

I liked that too.

Not every shared hour needed to become a network.

A thin blue glow appeared along the curb slot where the bakery ground cord ended.

The worker crouched.

Did not touch it.

Waited.

The glow faded.

Farther down, one street lamp came on yellow.

Then another.

Then the third stayed dark.

The worker noticed.

"North side."

The older man nodded.

I could not tell what they expected from that.

I did not ask.

Two lane workers crossed the far end of the street with long poles and a coil of black cord.

One stopped at the dark lamp.

The other continued.

Work.

Normal work.

The lamp remained dark.

Nobody under the arch became responsible.

Then the bell rang once.

A small clean chime.

Not the long first warning.

Not the two-note shelter call.

One.

The bakery worker unclipped his ground cord from the brass wall stud.

"Last."

The whole shelter changed.

People moved.

Not urgently.

The courier lifted her leather case.

The mother adjusted her wrap.

The handcart men retrieved the food pouch from the ash only after the bakery worker used the wooden paddle to tip it onto dry stone.

The bead remained dark.

"Cheese?" the second man asked.

The first opened the pouch, smelled it, and looked offended.

"Warm."

"Weather."

"Your fault."

"How?"

They rolled the cart away still arguing.

The older man stood slowly and tested his knees.

"See?"

The bakery worker said, "You predicted short rain."

"I predicted my knees."

"Rain was short."

"Coincidence."

Good scientist.

I retrieved my crutches.

Both tips dry where they had rested.

Hands settled onto grips.

Right foot under me.

Stand.

No problem.

Outside the arch, the street shone.

I waited for a woman carrying two baskets to pass before entering the lane.

The first step told me what I needed.

Wet.

Not treacherous.

Still wet.

I shortened my stride.

The courier came out behind me and turned the other direction.

"Carrow," she said.

I looked back.

"What?"

"Your better answer."

Then she left.

I had no useful response.

This was probably healthy.

The route home took longer because I avoided two steep wet cuts I would have used dry.

No heroism.

No lesson.

Just route selection.

Half the charm signs stayed dark.

The ones coming back did so unevenly.

Yellow lamps first.

Then a green pharmacy mark.

Then two shop letters that lit in the wrong order and had to be shut off again by a swearing owner.

A street-cleaning bead near the drain woke briefly, rolled three feet by itself, hit a wall, and stopped.

A boy applauded.

His mother did not.

Carrow had survived charge rain.

Barely.

By the time I reached our lane, ordinary water still dripped from the roofs.

Cala's rain shelf worked.

Of course it did.

A black ground cord I had never paid attention to before hung from the outer eave into the gutter.

I stopped outside.

Looked at it.

Then went in.

Cala was at the table.

Tavi was asleep on a folded cloth near the wall for reasons I did not ask about.

The house smelled like something with onions that I had not cooked.

Cala looked at my wet sleeve.

"Last chime?"

"Yes."

She nodded.

That was all.

I took off the shoulder bag.

Its stay bead remained dark.

Upstairs, the holdfast remained wrapped.

One copper and one bit remained where I had left them.

Vale's debt receipt remained under the narrow scrap.

Nothing had changed because the sky had spent an hour being magical.

Downstairs, one of the household wall lights flickered.

Once.

Cala looked at it.

I looked at it.

It stayed lit.

Neither of us moved.

After a moment, Cala said, "If twice, cover."

Local rule.

I sat down.

The light did not flicker again.

Good enough.

# CHAPTER 310

## THE UNCUT LINE

Two mornings after the charge rain, Lyssa told me to move.

I looked at the table.

Then at my cup.

Then at her.

“I am using this.”

“I can see that.”

“That usually strengthens my claim.”

“It does not today.”

She already had the long side of the table cleared. My cup, charcoal, and folded narrow scrap had been pushed into a neat square at the far corner without disturbing any of it. A folded gray cloth covered the middle. Pins sat in a shallow dish beside her left hand. Chalk, thread, scissors, and a little wooden gauge were arranged along the right.

This was not a request developing.

It had developed before I woke up.

“Customer?” I asked.

Lyssa nodded.

“When?”

A knock sounded downstairs.

She looked toward the door.

“Apparently now.”

I moved.

There were faster ways to surrender territory, but most required two intact legs or less dignity. I got both crutches under me, planted my right foot, kept the left knee clear of the chair, and stood. Lyssa took the chair before I had finished turning.

“Efficient,” I said.

“You were slow.”

“I was majestic.”

“You were in the way.”

That was harder to argue with.

Cala was already out with Tavi. She had said something about onions, a broken basket handle, and a woman whose name I had missed because Tavi had been explaining why horses should be allowed on roofs. The apartment was otherwise quiet.

The knock came again.

Lyssa opened the door.

The woman outside was broad through the shoulders and dressed like someone who expected her clothes to lose arguments with walls. Dark trousers. Heavy shoes. Sleeves rolled above the wrist. Her hair was tied close at the back with a strip of red cloth. She carried a dark green work vest over one forearm.

Not a decorative vest.

Load vest.

I recognized the family before she stepped inside.

The old Greg had worn three or four kinds over the years. Caravan harnesses, casualty rigs, porter gear, climbing loads, two military versions that had been designed by people who apparently hated ribs. Different makers solved the same problem differently. Spread weight. Keep a load from turning the wearer. Give shoulders, back, and hips more places to share the work.

This one had two narrow glass-blue lines sewn from the upper back down toward the side panels, with flat brass beads set near the lower ends.

Common enough.

Not my work.

The woman held the vest up.

“It pulls here now.”

She touched the left side under the arm.

Lyssa did not take the vest immediately.

“Put it on.”

The woman looked at her.

“I brought it because it does not fit.”

“Yes.”

A pause.

Then the woman put it on.

I stayed by the wall.

Lyssa glanced at me once.

It was not a warning.

It was the thing before a warning, which was cheaper.

I took the hint and sat on the low bench near the window. My crutches went beside me, tips clear of the walking space.

The vest fit badly even before the load line woke.

The left side panel rode higher than the right. The arm opening bit into the woman's shirt. Across the back, the blue lines sat slack and dull.

Lyssa walked around her once.

“Who changed this?”

“Jari.”

“When?”

“Three weeks.”

“Before this shirt?”

The woman looked down at herself.

“Yes.”

Lyssa pinched the cloth under the left arm.

“Thicker.”

“It is one shirt.”

“It is cloth.”

“That is what shirts are.”

Lyssa looked at her.

The woman sighed.

“Fine.”

I liked her.

Lyssa tugged lightly at the side seam, then at the lower edge.

“Load it.”

The woman had brought that too.

A short leather strap was folded through one vest loop. She reached into the hall and dragged in a squat canvas sack with a wooden handle sewn across the top. It landed with a dense thump.

Sand, maybe.

Maybe metal.

Not my sand.

She clipped the strap into the vest.

The glass-blue lines brightened.

Not much. A thin color under the surface, like light trapped in deep water. The flat brass beads warmed from brown to gold. The vest tightened across the woman's back and settled downward.

The left side pulled hard.

There it was.

The upper edge pressed into her shirt under the arm while the right side stayed flat.

Lyssa crouched enough to sight along the lower seam.

“Off.”

The woman unhooked the sack.

The blue lines went dull again.

“I told you.”

“You told me it pulled.”

“It pulled.”

“Now I know where.”

That ended the exchange as far as Lyssa was concerned.

The woman was not finished.

“Can you move the blue?”

“No.”

The answer came before the last word had fully left the woman's mouth.

She frowned.

“You did not look.”

“I looked.”

“You looked at the cloth.”

“Yes.”

“The blue is why it pulls.”

“Maybe.”

I looked at Lyssa.

That was almost Hessa's word.

Almost.

Lyssa's version had less interest in proving anything and more interest in getting through the morning.

The woman tugged the vest off and laid it on the table.

“If the line sits lower, it stops biting.”

“Maybe.”

“You keep saying that.”

“Because I am not cutting it.”

The woman put one finger on the glass-blue seam.

“I will pay another bit.”

My attention betrayed me.

Not visibly, I hoped.

Probably visibly.

One bit was sitting upstairs beside one copper.

One bit had just appeared on the table in sentence form and been refused before it became metal.

Lyssa did not even look at me.

“No.”

The woman stared at her.

“You do charm seam.”

“I do cloth around charm seam.”

“You fixed the red carry coat.”

“Cloth.”

“The line was in it.”

“And I left it there.”

The woman pushed the vest toward her.

“Then leave this one and make it stop pulling.”

“That I can try.”

“How much?”

Lyssa named a price.

One bit.

The same extra bit the woman had just offered for moving the blue.

My brain immediately did the arithmetic even though there was no arithmetic to do.

One bit for cloth.

Another bit available for blue.

Two bits sitting inside one problem.

Lyssa was choosing one.

The woman drummed two fingers on the table.

“Today?”

“Tomorrow.”

“Tonight.”

“Tomorrow.”

“I lift at first bell.”

“Then pick it up before first bell.”

“You will be awake?”

Lyssa looked at her.

The woman smiled.

“Tomorrow.”

Good.

Not everybody needed to win a negotiation. Sometimes the person with the scissors just waited.

Lyssa had the woman put the vest back on without load. She opened the left side with her fingers, found the current seam, then marked two short chalk lines below the arm and another near the lower edge.

“Here and here. I can give you more cloth without touching the blue.”

“How much?”

Lyssa held up two fingers.

“About this.”

“That enough?”

“I do not know until I baste it.”

The woman glanced at me then.

Not because I had moved.

Because I was looking at the vest.

“You know these?”

Lyssa said, “No.”

The woman looked at her.

I looked at her.

Lyssa kept marking chalk.

“I asked him.”

“I heard you.”

“I do,” I said.

Lyssa's chalk stopped.

That was the warning.

I should have listened.

I had, in fact, worn load lines. I had trusted them with wounded people, supplies, ugly climbs, worse descents, and one dead boar that had become everybody's problem because somebody had insisted we could carry it whole.

The first life had contained too many people to preserve exclusive names.

The woman turned a little toward me.

“This type?”

“Broadly.”

Lyssa resumed chalking.

The woman touched the lower brass bead.

“So if this moves down?”

“It might shift the pull.”

“Might?”

“Different lines set differently.”

Lyssa said, “Greg.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

The woman looked between us.

Interesting for her.

Less interesting for me.

I kept going because intelligence had never prevented me from making a bad conversational choice.

“If the cloth was taken in after the line was set, the anchor may be fine. The new shirt changes the route too. Moving the line could solve one pull and make another.”

The woman nodded slowly.

Lyssa put the chalk down.

“Finished?”

I looked at her.

“No.”

“Be finished.”

That did it.

I leaned back on the bench.

The woman grinned.

“You two always like this?”

“No,” Lyssa said.

“Yes,” I said.

Lyssa picked up the vest.

“Off.”

The woman took it off.

She was still smiling.

I was not.

Lyssa laid the vest flat and smoothed the green cloth with both hands. She did not touch the blue seam except to move the cloth beneath it. Her fingers stayed on what she actually intended to alter.

That irritated me more than it should have.

Because she was right?

Not entirely.

Because she had cut me off in front of a stranger?

Closer.

Because the extra bit still existed in my head?

Unfortunately.

The woman took a small coin from her pocket and set it on the table.

One bit.

Deposit or full price, I did not know.

Lyssa covered it with the pin dish while she worked.

That irritated me too.

Not because she was hiding it.

Because she was not thinking about it.

I was.

The woman stepped back into the hall.

“Tomorrow before first.”

“Yes.”

“If it still pulls?”

“Then you take it to someone who works blue.”

“And pay again.”

“Yes.”

“You are cruel.”

“Tomorrow.”

The woman left.

Lyssa shut the door.

For several seconds, the room was quiet.

I could have made it longer.

I did not.

“You could have charged two.”

Lyssa kept her back to me.

There are sentences a person hears after speaking them.

That was one.

She lifted the pin dish, took the bit from beneath it, and set the coin in a small wooden box on the shelf behind the worktable. The box already contained other things that were none of my business.

Then she turned.

“For what?”

“The line.”

“I am not doing the line.”

“You know enough to work around it.”

“Yes.”

“You knew what I was saying.”

“I knew what you were saying in front of my customer.”

There it was.

Not money.

Not first.

I shifted on the bench.

“She asked me.”

“And I said no.”

“She did not ask you.”

“She was in my room. With my work. At my table.”

“Our table.”

Lyssa stared at me.

I made a small correction.

“Currently your table.”

“Better.”

Not resolved.

The room stayed tight.

I looked at the vest.

The blue line had gone completely dull.

“You could have let me look.”

“You were looking.”

“You know what I mean.”

“Yes.”

“And?”

“And I did not want you joining the fitting.”

I felt the answer in the part of me that still believed usefulness was a door that should open if I had the key.

“I know load gear.”

“I know.”

That stopped me for half a second.

“You know?”

“You have told enough stories.”

“I do not tell that many.”

Lyssa gave me a look.

Fine.

“I know load gear,” I repeated.

“You know wearing it.”

“I know more than wearing it.”

“Maybe.”

That word again.

Now it sounded hostile.

I stood.

Not because standing improved arguments.

It did not.

I needed to change position.

I got my right foot under me, set both crutches, and came up. The motion was clean. The room was familiar. Lyssa waited until I had settled before speaking again, which somehow made me more annoyed.

“You know enough to say maybe,” she said. “You do not know enough for my customer to come back when the blue breaks.”

“It was not going to break because I looked at it.”

“You were not only looking.”

“I gave her an answer.”

“You gave her three answers.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“It is when she starts asking what to pay you.”

“She did not.”

“She would.”

I opened my mouth.

Closed it.

Lyssa picked up the vest and turned it inside out.

The construction became clearer.

The glass-blue line did not run through the side seam at all. It passed behind a reinforcing panel, then ended in a stitched pocket holding the lower brass bead. Jari's alteration had narrowed the ordinary cloth just beside that pocket.

I could see why Lyssa wanted to open the cloth instead of touching the line.

I could also see three possible ways to move the anchor.

Possible.

That was the problem with competence. It produced possibilities faster than permission.

“She offered another bit,” I said.

Lyssa looked up sharply.

There.

The real thing.

Not my proudest discovery.

“Ah,” she said.

I hated the sound.

“What?”

“You heard the bit.”

“I heard all of it.”

“You heard that part loud.”

“I have one copper and one bit.”

“I know.”

“I spent two copper on a tool that may or may not pay back. Vale gave me debt credit, not cash. Yes. I notice money.”

Lyssa's expression changed a little.

Not softer.

Less confused.

“Then notice mine is not yours.”

“I know that.”

“Do you?”

That landed harder than the earlier one.

I leaned more weight into the crutches than I needed to.

Not pain.

Just irritation looking for somewhere physical to live.

“I did not ask for her coin.”

“You asked why I did not take it.”

“That is different.”

“Yes.”

She sat.

That left me standing over the table like an idiot with equipment.

I moved to the other chair and sat too.

Lyssa picked up a length of ordinary dark thread.

“The blue fails under a load, who comes back?”

“You.”

“Yes.”

“If the cloth fails, who comes back?”

“Me.”

“So?”

“So I know cloth.”

“You know some of the blue.”

“I know enough not to cut it.”

The simplicity annoyed me because I had wanted the problem to be more interesting.

“What if one extra bit is enough for the risk?”

Lyssa threaded the needle.

“For you?”

“For the job.”

“No.”

“You did not think.”

“I did.”

“When?”

“Before I said no.”

I almost laughed.

Not because it was funny.

Because she had managed to insult my preferred method of existence without raising her voice.

Lyssa pressed the needle through the first chalk-marked fold.

“I do not want every job,” she said.

That was the sentence I had not expected.

I looked at her.

She did not look back.

“I have work.”

“I know.”

“I have too much some days.”

“I know.”

“I have people who come back because I did what I said I would do.”

“I know.”

She pulled the thread through.

“If I start cutting lines because somebody waves one more bit, then I have line work.”

“You could charge more.”

“I do not want line work.”

I had no immediate answer.

Not a good one.

The old Greg had lived in a world where a useful capability tended to become an obligation through repetition. Could climb that wall? Congratulations, now every wall was yours. Could break that ward? Wonderful, there were six more. Could carry the injured mage through a collapsing tunnel? Apparently that qualified you for logistics.

I knew that.

I had spent years complaining about it.

Current Greg had one copper and one bit.

Money changed the shape of principles.

Lyssa made three loose basting stitches and tied them off.

“You want to test this?” she asked.

I blinked.

“Me?”

She stared.

“The vest.”

Right.

“Not the blue.”

“I understood.”

“You looked excited.”

“I am offended by that.”

“You looked excited.”

She turned the vest right-side out.

The left side now had a temporary fold let out near the arm. Ugly. Functional.

“What do you need?”

“Lift the sack onto the chair.”

That was not a smart two-crutch carry.

I looked at the squat canvas sack by the door.

Then at her.

“Slide?”

“Yes.”

Better.

I hooked one crutch around the wooden handle, dragged the sack across the floor about two feet, then switched position and nudged it against the chair leg. Lyssa took the handle and lifted it the rest of the way onto the seat.

No heroic porter work.

Civilization survived.

She clipped the vest strap to the sack and pulled the vest up against the chair back as if the chair were a person with terrible shoulders.

The blue lines brightened.

The brass beads warmed.

The left side drew in.

Less.

Not gone.

Lyssa watched the cloth.

I watched the line.

We were looking at the same garment and doing different work.

“The arm gets more room,” I said.

“Yes.”

“The lower pull is still there.”

“Yes.”

She unclipped the sack.

The light faded.

I waited.

Lyssa adjusted one pin.

Then another.

She did not ask what I thought.

I did not tell her.

This was apparently growth.

It felt mostly like being quiet.

After a moment she said, “The cloth can give more lower.”

“Will that twist the pocket?”

“Maybe.”

I looked at her.

She looked back.

We both started laughing.

Not much.

Enough to ruin the argument's clean shape.

I hated when life did that.

She went back to the seam.

I sat there another minute, then reached for my crutches.

“Where are you going?”

“Anywhere I am not being accused of entering a fitting.”

“You are still in the fitting.”

“The customer left.”

“The vest stayed.”

“Cruel.”

“That was already established.”

I stood and went upstairs.

I did not slam anything.

There was nothing useful to slam.

The fixed holdfast sat wrapped on the high shelf above my clothes.

For one deeply stupid second, I looked at it and thought about the two copper.

Then I looked away.

The thing had not changed.

Neither had the price.

I sat on the edge of the bed, took the Vale receipt from beneath the narrow scrap, and looked at the line that said one copper credit.

Still not cash.

Also still real.

I put it back.

Downstairs, Lyssa's scissors opened and closed.

Once.

Twice.

Then thread pulled through cloth.

No blue light.

No disaster.

No extra bit.

I stayed upstairs long enough for my irritation to become boring.

That helped.

When I came down again, the vest was on the table with the left side basted open farther than before. The blue strip remained exactly where it had been.

Lyssa was working on something else.

Different cloth.

Different thread.

The green vest had become one job among several again.

I stopped beside it.

“Looks straighter.”

“The cloth does.”

Of course.

I touched nothing.

Lyssa did not look up.

After a moment she said, “You still think I should cut it?”

I considered lying.

“No.”

She glanced at me.

“I think I know what I would try.”

“That was not the question.”

“I know.”

She returned to the other cloth.

I stood there for another second.

Then I took my cup from the far corner of the table.

My cup.

That claim, apparently, had survived.

I carried it to the window one careful step at a time, one hand on a crutch, the other around the cup, and sat where I was not in the way.

Lyssa kept working.

The blue line stayed uncut.

# CHAPTER 311

## THE RETURN CART

Sevren found me three days later because apparently he still knew how.

I was outside a fruit stall arguing with a pear.

The pear had a dark spot near the stem.

The seller said it was fine.

I said that depended on price.

The seller said one bit for three.

I said I only wanted one.

She said that sounded like my problem.

Then somebody behind me said, “You got worse.”

I turned.

Sevren stood in the lane with a courier bag across his chest and dried mud up both boots.

The bag was not the same one.

Or it was the same one after surviving a small war.

The flap had been replaced with darker leather. One side seam carried two rows of pale stitching. The old brass buckle remained. So did Sevren.

Mostly.

His hair was longer. His face was thinner. There was a healed cut across the bridge of his nose that had not existed the last time I remembered looking closely.

I stared.

He stared back.

The fruit seller held up the pear.

“You buying?”

“No.”

She put it down.

Sevren said, “You lost.”

“I declined.”

“You argued with fruit.”

“I negotiated.”

“You bought nothing.”

“Excellent negotiation.”

He grinned.

That was familiar enough that the lane rearranged around it.

Not literally.

A woman with a basket had to step around both of us because we were standing in the way.

She said so.

We moved.

Sevren looked at my crutches, then my shoulder bag, then me.

Not checking.

Inventory.

“You busy?”

“Currently?”

“Until dark.”

That narrowed the question.

“No.”

“Want a cart seat?”

I looked at him.

“Where?”

“Three Wells.”

I knew the name. East road, beyond the low farms, not far enough to be a journey unless the horse hated everyone.

“Why?”

“Because there is a seat.”

“That is an answer to what.”

“It is the answer I have.”

He shifted the courier bag higher.

“Outbound freight pays the cart. Return freight pays the cart. Front bench has one empty place. Carter says he does not care if you do not touch anything.”

“Do you need me to do anything?”

“No.”

“Carry anything?”

“No.”

“Deliver anything?”

“No.”

“Sign anything?”

“No.”

“Lie to anyone?”

He considered.

“Not currently.”

Better.

I looked east.

The city wall was not visible from this lane. Too many roofs. Too many signs. Too much Carrow between me and outside.

I had left the city since the leg.

Not often.

Once with Sevren, actually.

That had been earlier, when a cart seat had felt like a body problem with scenery attached.

This morning my residual limb felt ordinary. Hands good. Shoulders good. Right leg good. Crutches sound. No rain threatening. No appointment. No work.

One copper and one bit remained upstairs.

Neither had to move.

“What time back?”

“Before dark if the return load is ready.”

“If not?”

“After.”

“That is not a time.”

“It is a road.”

Still him.

I glanced toward home.

Sevren followed the look.

“You need permission?”

“No.”

“Then why did you look?”

“I need water.”

“Reasonable.”

“And bread.”

“Less exciting.”

“And I should tell Cala.”

“There it is.”

“Shut up.”

We walked back together.

Sevren matched my pace without announcing it.

He had learned that months ago.

Maybe longer.

No hovering. No reaching for a crutch. No walking half a step too slowly in a way that made everybody aware he was walking half a step too slowly.

He just shortened.

That was friendship with mileage.

At the house, Cala was cleaning greens at the table.

Tavi was under it.

Of course.

Lyssa was out.

Also normal.

Cala looked at Sevren.

“Road.”

Sevren nodded.

“Unfortunately.”

She looked at me.

“Going?”

“Yes.”

“Back?”

“Before dark if the road obeys him.”

Sevren said, “It will not.”

Cala wrapped two pieces of bread in cloth anyway.

I filled the small water bottle that fit in the shoulder bag.

Tavi emerged from under the table and stared at Sevren’s boots.

“Horse?”

Sevren looked down.

“Probably.”

Tavi pointed.

“Bad.”

“Yes.”

Good conversation.

I checked the bag.

Water.

Bread.

Narrow scrap.

Charcoal.

No fixed holdfast.

The bad tool remained upstairs where expensive decisions belonged.

I put one folded cloth inside too.

Sevren watched.

“For what?”

“Seat.”

He nodded once.

No joke.

That meant he remembered.

The first road cart I had ridden after losing the leg had taught me more about plank benches than any person deserved to know.

We left.

The freight yard had moved.

Not the whole yard.

The entrance.

A new gate had been cut through the east wall of the compound, wide enough for two carts to pass without negotiating ancestry. The old gate still existed twenty yards down and had been half blocked by stacked wheel rims.

I stopped.

Sevren kept going two steps, noticed, and came back.

“What?”

“That was not there.”

“The gate?”

“Yes.”

“Three months.”

I looked at him.

“Three months?”

“Maybe four.”

“How long have you been gone?”

“Not gone.”

“You have not been here.”

“I have been here.”

“Not where I was.”

“That is different.”

Annoyingly true.

Inside the yard, three long carts waited under a roof. Two had high canvas covers. Ours was open, with bundled reed frames tied along one side and six sealed clay jars nested in straw toward the back.

A broad woman in a brown vest stood beside the horse checking a strap.

Sevren pointed.

“Ressa. Cart.”

The woman looked at him.

“Sevren. Noise.”

Good.

Then she looked at me.

“Seat is front. Crutches under, not across.”

“Under what?”

“Bench. Strap there.”

She pointed.

A leather loop had been fixed beneath the front bench, probably for poles, tools, or anything else long enough to become a problem.

I liked Ressa immediately.

“Do you want them from me?”

“No.”

Also good.

I got up myself.

Right foot on the iron step. Left knee clear. Both hands on the cart frame. Push. Turn. Sit.

Cleaner than the old days.

Not graceful.

Grace had no contractual role.

I folded one crutch, then the other as far as their ordinary construction allowed, slid both beneath the bench, and secured the shafts with the leather loop. Tips pointed inward. Handles within reach if I leaned.

Ressa checked the strap.

Not me.

The strap.

“Fine.”

Sevren climbed up beside me.

The bench was narrow.

I took out the folded cloth and put it beneath the back of my left thigh and residual limb so the plank edge did not press where it had no business pressing.

Sevren looked at it.

“You plan now.”

“I suffered before.”

“Same thing, slower.”

Ressa climbed to the driver’s board.

The horse started before anybody made a speech.

Carrow moved past us.

Freight yard.

Potters.

A row of low sheds.

The repaired crossing from weeks ago appeared under the wheels and vanished behind us without comment.

At the east gate a clerk checked the cart board, touched a small brass witness bead beside Ressa’s name, and watched it flash green.

Common transfer mark.

Nothing mysterious.

The gate opened.

Then Carrow ended.

Not dramatically.

Wall.

Road.

Fields.

Sky.

The first thing outside the city was cabbage.

Acres of it.

I had expected something better.

Sevren leaned back against the cart side.

“You look disappointed.”

“Cabbage.”

“You knew there were farms.”

“I expected the first view to show ambition.”

“It feeds people.”

“Practical ambition.”

He closed his eyes.

The road ran between low stone walls darkened by recent rain. Water sat in the deeper wheel ruts, but the center had dried. Every few hundred yards a shallow irrigation channel crossed beneath the road. Small square control stones at the field edges showed dull blue faces when water pressure held and gray faces when gates were closed.

I knew the family.

Different regions used different markers.

Old Greg had mostly cared whether the road flooded.

Current Greg watched one farmer kick mud away from a control stone so she could see its face.

Blue.

She kept walking.

That was the whole system I needed.

A mile farther, a cart passed us going west with stacked baskets under netting.

Ressa lifted two fingers.

The other driver did the same.

Sevren slept.

I looked at him.

Actually slept.

Mouth slightly open.

Courier bag clutched under one arm.

I considered speaking.

Friendship won.

Barely.

The east road flattened after the first rise.

My body settled into the cart rhythm better than it had months ago. Right foot braced lightly against the front board. Left knee bent comfortably. Residual limb supported by folded cloth instead of hanging against the bench edge. Hands mostly idle.

Road vibration still traveled through everything.

Cart seats remained a form of punishment.

But it was ordinary punishment now.

I could look outside.

That felt new enough that I did not explain it to myself.

Three Wells was not a village.

It was a road knot.

One inn.

One stable yard.

One long low relay house with a blue roof.

Three stone wells around a broad packed yard, which suggested somebody had named the place during a period of unusual honesty.

Ressa drove straight to the relay house.

Sevren woke before the cart stopped.

I resented this.

“You were asleep.”

“No.”

“You were making sleep noises.”

“Road thinking.”

“That is snoring.”

He jumped down.

Ressa unfastened my crutch strap before I reached for it, then stopped.

“Want them?”

“Yes.”

She handed them up one at a time.

No grabbing me.

No instructing me how to use objects I owned.

I set both crutch tips on the packed yard, turned, lowered my right foot to the iron step, then ground.

Fine.

Sevren was already halfway to the relay house.

“Wait.”

He turned.

“Why?”

“I do not know where I am going.”

“Food.”

“Excellent.”

That was not where he was going.

He pointed to the inn.

“I have handoff.”

“Then why did you say food?”

“For you.”

“Ah.”

He left.

Independent life remained rude.

I went to the inn.

The front step was too high for comfort and had a wooden half-step set beside it.

Not for me.

For everybody with short legs, old knees, heavy baskets, barrels on shoulders, children, and perhaps basic architectural regret.

I used it.

Inside, the room smelled like boiled meat, wet wool, and smoke.

A woman behind the counter said, “Stew or bread.”

“Both?”

She looked at me.

“That is allowed.”

Good place.

I paid nothing because Sevren had apparently arranged a road meal with the cart.

Or the cart had.

I did not know.

I asked before eating.

“Passenger meal included?”

The woman pointed toward the relay house.

“Courier seat?”

“Yes.”

“Yes.”

Good.

No accidental debt to stew.

I took a bowl and sat near the window.

Three Wells continued without me.

A stable boy carried two empty buckets past the window.

Ressa argued with a man over the reed frames.

A woman in a gray coat came out of the relay house, checked the sky, went back inside.

Two children rolled a wooden hoop through the yard until it hit one of the wells.

No blue charm.

No magical disaster.

Just bad aim.

Sevren returned ten minutes later and dropped into the chair opposite me.

He had a wooden token in one hand.

Round.

Dark.

A thin silver line crossed it from edge to edge.

He put it on the table.

I looked.

“Return?”

“Proof of handoff.”

I knew broad relay tokens.

Carrier takes sealed packet.

Receiving station witnesses intact handoff.

Token records or marks transfer enough to settle payment disputes.

Different houses used different versions.

I did not touch it.

Sevren ate half my bread.

I objected.

“That was included.”

“For passenger.”

“I brought passenger.”

“I am passenger.”

“You are cargo with opinions.”

“Then charge freight.”

He took another bite.

I moved the rest of the bread.

He smiled.

We ate.

For a while neither of us said anything.

That was also familiar.

Sevren had never needed every silence filled.

Outside, Ressa’s argument ended because the reed-frame man walked away carrying nothing.

Possibly victory.

Possibly lunch.

I nodded toward Sevren’s bag.

“New?”

“Old.”

“New flap.”

“Yes.”

“Who did it?”

“Woman in Bell Ford.”

“Good?”

“Still attached.”

High praise.

He stirred the stew.

“You still at theatre?”

“Sometimes.”

“Guild?”

“Sometimes.”

“Vale?”

I looked at him.

“How do you know Vale?”

“You complained about Vale before.”

“That was a long time ago.”

“You complain with continuity.”

Fair.

“Still Vale.”

“Lyssa?”

“Yes.”

He nodded.

No interrogation.

Again.

I looked at his boots.

“You?”

“What?”

“Road.”

He chewed.

“More east now.”

“I noticed.”

“North board got crowded.”

“With couriers?”

“With contracts.”

That meant something to him.

I waited.

He continued because he wanted to, not because I asked correctly.

“Short city work pays badly if everybody has legs.”

I looked down.

He pointed his spoon at me.

“Not about you.”

“I know.”

“Good.”

He ate.

“East relay lost two regulars. One married into a mill family. One broke a wrist and decided paper was safer.”

“Is paper safer?”

“No.”

“Good.”

“So you took the runs.”

“Some.”

“Better pay?”

“Yes.”

“Worse beds?”

“Much.”

There it was.

The old Sevren equation.

I smiled.

He noticed.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

“You have the face.”

“What face?”

“The one where you turn somebody else’s bad mattress into a lesson.”

I was offended.

“That is Lyssa’s accusation.”

“She is correct often.”

“Disgusting.”

Sevren finished the stew.

Then he said, “I am out more.”

Not apology.

Not announcement.

Fact.

“I noticed eventually.”

“Eventually?”

“I had other things.”

“Clearly.”

He pointed at my hair.

My hair had grown since the old date haircut.

The joke no longer made sense.

That made it better somehow.

I said, “You could have come by.”

“I did twice.”

I stared.

“When?”

“Once you were at theatre. Once Cala said you were at Guild.”

That was rude.

Not Sevren.

Time.

He had come.

I had been elsewhere.

The city had not failed to deliver him to me.

I had missed him by living.

I did not say that.

It sounded like something written on a bad wall plaque.

Instead I said, “You could have left bread.”

“I ate it.”

“Of course.”

He leaned back.

“I am here tonight.”

“Then leaving?”

“Tomorrow east.”

“For how long?”

“Three days if wheels remain round.”

“And after?”

“Carrow one day. Maybe two.”

A schedule.

Not a disappearance.

Not mine.

I nodded.

Sevren picked up the dark relay token and rolled it across his knuckles once.

The silver line flashed pale.

Not activation.

Just light.

“You ever want the longer seat?”

I looked at him.

“What longer seat?”

“East loop. Full day.”

There it was.

Not work.

Not yet.

“Why?”

“Because you keep looking at roads like they insulted you.”

“That is not a reason to spend a day on one.”

“It is a little.”

“Is there work?”

“For me.”

“For me?”

“No.”

“Pay?”

“No.”

“Cost?”

“Usually passenger bit if the cart is full contract. Sometimes nothing if seat is already covered.”

I had one bit.

He saw me calculate.

Not the amount.

The category.

“Not offering now,” he said.

Good.

That mattered.

“What are you doing?”

“Asking whether I should ask if there is one.”

Also familiar.

I looked through the window.

Ressa was tightening one of the return straps.

Beyond the yard, the east road kept going.

Fields.

Low hills.

Places I knew by old memory, places I knew wrongly, places that had not existed yet, places I had never seen at all.

Current Greg had spent a great deal of time learning one city.

That did not make the road less real.

“Ask,” I said.

Sevren nodded.

No promise.

No date.

No plan.

Then he stole the last piece of my bread.

I should have expected it.

The return freight was four wrapped wicker panels and two sacks that smelled sharply of dried mint.

Ressa made Sevren check the load ties.

Not me.

I appreciated the clarity.

The ride back began warmer.

Clouds broke over the fields. Road water turned silver in the low sun. One irrigation gate had opened since morning, and a thin sheet of water ran bright between rows of cabbage.

Still cabbage.

I had become more forgiving.

Sevren stayed awake this time.

We talked about nothing important.

A courier in Bell Ford who had lost a boot in mud and delivered the packet anyway.

A horse that refused one bridge but would cross the river twenty yards downstream.

A road inn that served eggs blue because of a harmless heating stone problem and insisted the color was traditional.

I believed half of it.

That was enough.

At the first rise toward Carrow, a small brake bead beneath Ressa’s board flashed amber as she pulled the long lever.

The cart slowed smoothly.

Common assist.

No drama.

Sevren kept talking through it.

Good fantasy, apparently, was sometimes something nobody stopped a story for.

By the east gate my back was tired.

Right thigh mildly stiff.

Residual limb warm but not angry.

No skin pain I could detect.

No hand trouble.

The folded cloth had earned permanent employment.

Ressa stopped inside the freight yard and handed me my crutches after releasing the strap.

“Same seat next time?”

“Ask me after my spine forgives this one.”

She nodded.

Reasonable.

Sevren swung his courier bag over his shoulder.

“Food?”

“I just spent half a day eating your bread.”

“My bread?”

“Exactly.”

We left the yard together.

At the next junction he turned south.

I stopped.

“You are not going home?”

“One delivery.”

“Of course.”

He walked backward three steps.

“If there is a longer seat, I ask.”

“If there is a seat.”

“That is what I said.”

“No, you said if there is a longer seat. There may be a shorter seat.”

He stared at me.

Then pointed south.

“I have work.”

“Coward.”

He turned away.

I called, “Sevren.”

He looked back.

“Good to see you.”

His expression changed just enough to make the sentence embarrassing.

“Yeah,” he said.

Then, because mercy was not a durable part of his character:

“Hair is worse again.”

“Fuck you.”

He left laughing.

I went home.

Cala was at the table.

Tavi was asleep with his head on folded arms.

Lyssa was cutting dark cloth at the far end.

She looked up.

“Three Wells?”

“Yes.”

“Worth it?”

My back hurt a little.

My right thigh wanted a different chair.

There was road dust on both trouser cuffs.

Sevren had stolen my bread.

“Yes.”

Lyssa nodded and went back to cutting.

No debrief.

No question about the green vest.

No question from me.

I put the folded seat cloth near the door so I would remember it.

Then I sat down.

Outside, a cart rolled past toward somewhere else.

# CHAPTER 312

## THE MARKED THIRD

Hessa's note arrived before breakfast.

This was becoming rude.

Cala had not even cut the bread yet.

Tavi was trying to fit one wooden spoon inside another wooden spoon, which was impossible but apparently worth sustained research.

Lyssa was already gone.

The knock came twice.

Cala opened the door.

A Guild runner held out a folded square.

Not the same runner as last time.

Carrow contained enough people that repetition remained optional.

"Greg?"

I raised a hand.

He crossed the room, gave me the note, and left.

I opened it.

AFTER MIDDAY.

EAT FIRST.

NO ATTEMPTS BEFORE.

SAME RULES.

HESSA.

I looked at Cala.

She looked at the note.

"Eat."

"I have not argued yet."

"Good."

"This is prejudice."

She cut the bread.

Tavi got one spoon stuck inside the other.

Progress.

I ate.

*

The folded seat cloth from yesterday's road trip was still near the door.

I noticed it when I left.

That was all.

Sevren had not appeared with another cart.

Ressa had not sent a message.

Three Wells had remained where we left it.

Good.

My body had recovered from the road well enough.

Back normal after sleep.

Right thigh normal.

Residual limb comfortable.

Hands fine.

Crutches sound.

The shoulder bag carried the narrow scrap and charcoal.

One copper and one bit stayed upstairs.

The fixed holdfast stayed wrapped on the high shelf.

Nothing expensive came with me.

The Guild yard was quieter than the last time.

A pair of apprentices were moving stacked wooden frames from one wall to another.

A woman at the far bench had three tiny blue lamps lined up in front of her and was cursing one of them under her breath.

Nobody needed me.

I went to Hessa's room.

The black witness plate was back.

So was the brass arch.

So were the four draft telltales.

The still bead hung dark at the top.

The paper was different.

Not the old pale square.

A long narrow strip lay across the black slate.

About the width of two fingers.

Maybe the length of my forearm.

Three black crossmarks divided it into thirds.

LEFT.

CENTER.

RIGHT.

The words were written on Hessa's page.

Not on the paper.

I stopped in the doorway.

"That looks like a trap."

Hessa looked up.

"Sit."

"That confirms it."

I sat.

The chair was at the near position.

Middle and far marks remained on the floor.

Unused.

Hessa asked the usual questions.

Sleep.

Food.

Pain.

Independent draw.

Independent shaping.

Independent external attempt.

No.

No.

No.

She checked pulse.

Hands.

Forearms.

Grip.

Shoulders.

Residual limb only because the road had been yesterday.

"Skin?"

"Fine."

"Pressure?"

"None."

"Pain?"

"No."

"Cart?"

"Bad furniture."

She looked at me.

"Expected."

"I would like that placed in the official record."

"It is."

I could not tell whether she was joking.

This was one of Hessa's more dangerous skills.

She turned toward the table.

"Distance is not the variable."

"Again?"

"Yes."

"Far will become jealous."

"No."

"Middle?"

"No."

"Near only."

"Yes."

I looked at the strip.

"Same paper, different places on it."

"Yes."

There it was.

I leaned forward.

Not enough to cross the line.

Just enough to be interested.

Hessa pointed at the strip.

"One object."

"Three targets."

"Three marked regions."

"That is three targets."

"Maybe."

I stared at her.

She stared back.

I smiled.

"No."

"What?"

"You used maybe."

"I use words."

"Not that one."

"Greg."

"Fine."

I looked at the paper again.

The strip ran left to right across the witness plate.

All three marked regions sat at approximately the same forward distance from me.

Lateral change only.

Not perfect.

Nothing in a room was perfect.

Good enough to ask something.

"You're testing whether I affect the object or the part I mean."

"I am testing whether target specification changes the observed response."

"That is the same sentence after a clerk attacked it."

"It is a narrower sentence."

Of course.

I looked at the left third.

Then the center.

Then the right.

"If I say right third and the whole strip moves?"

"We record what moves."

"If only the right end moves?"

"We record what moves."

"If nothing moves?"

"We record nothing."

"Your profession has a theme."

"Yes."

The still bead remained off.

One telltale drifted faintly toward the window.

Hessa touched the arch.

"Still field on for all three."

I looked at her.

"No off comparison?"

"Not today."

"Why?"

"Because air is not today's comparison."

She turned the bead on.

Soft blue filled the clear center.

The telltales settled one by one.

The room around the table became quieter.

Not silent.

Not dead.

Just less interested in the paper.

Hessa waited until all four threads hung straight.

Then she used a thin brass rule to set the strip along two grid lines.

Left edge.

Right edge.

One long side almost exactly parallel with the nearest white grid line.

She wrote the positions down.

"What is the instruction?" I asked.

"Same external effect. Away."

"Target?"

"Left third."

"Meaning I am supposed to push the left third away."

"Do not invent push."

I closed my mouth.

She continued.

"Direct the established external attempt away. Specify the left marked region as target."

"Without increasing effort."

"Correct."

"Same duration."

"Correct."

"Same body position."

"As practical."

"Three maximum."

"Yes."

There was one question I disliked.

I asked it anyway.

"If the whole strip moves, does that mean I cannot target part of it?"

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because one result would not establish that."

I sighed.

"What if only the left third moves?"

"Paper is continuous."

"Rotates."

"Then we measure rotation."

"So if the left end goes away and the right end stays, that is interesting."

"Yes."

I waited.

Hessa added, "Interesting is not proof of your preferred explanation."

There she was.

I settled.

Hands loose.

Shoulder down.

Right foot flat.

Left knee clear.

No leaning.

No body reach.

The strip stayed flat.

Attempt seventy-six.

Hessa said, "Expectation?"

"Low to medium."

"Evidence?"

"Near has three clean responses in seven attempts."

"Relevant to sub-object targeting?"

"No direct evidence."

"Anything else?"

"I have opinions."

"Not evidence."

"They are extremely well organized."

"Begin."

I worked.

Left third.

Away.

Not harder.

Not more.

Just the same strange narrow attempt that had occasionally moved paper and mostly insulted me.

Nothing happened.

The left third remained exactly where it was.

Center remained.

Right remained.

The telltales stayed straight.

Blue bead steady.

The paper did not curl.

Did not slide.

Did not rotate.

The end cue came.

I stopped.

Hessa checked the strip without touching it.

Grid.

Edges.

Crossmarks.

Telltales.

Table.

Me.

Then the brass rule.

"No observed response."

I nodded.

"Body?"

"Fine."

"Urge to increase?"

"Low."

"Interpretation?"

"I remain bad at moving one third of paper."

Hessa wrote something.

"That is not useful."

"It is emotionally accurate."

"Try again."

"Right third?"

"Yes."

She did not move the strip.

That mattered.

Same object.

Same placement.

Same still field.

Different specified region.

At least as close as practical.

Hessa pointed at the right third.

"Attempt seventy-seven. Target right marked region. Direction away. Same duration. Same permitted effort."

I looked at the strip.

The first attempt had left me calmer than a success would have.

Failure was useful that way.

It took some appetite out of the room.

"Expectation?" Hessa asked.

"Low."

"Why lower?"

"Seventy-six did nothing."

"Is that enough?"

"No."

"Then?"

"Emotion."

"Good."

I hated when she approved the annoying answer.

"Begin."

I worked.

Right third.

Away.

For several breaths, nothing.

Then the strip turned.

Not much.

The right end shifted away from me.

The left end shifted slightly toward me.

The center barely changed position.

A rotation.

Small.

Clean enough that the long edge no longer followed the white grid line.

I stopped on the cue.

My heart sped before my thoughts did.

Hessa moved immediately.

Not touching the paper.

Telltales first.

All straight.

Still bead steady.

Table.

Floor.

My chair.

My right foot.

Crutches.

Her own sleeve.

Then the strip.

She crouched.

Looked along the grid.

Moved to the other side.

Measured both ends.

The right end had moved farther away.

The left had moved a smaller amount toward.

The center crossmark had barely translated.

I knew what that looked like.

I also knew what I wanted it to mean.

Those were not the same thing.

"Rotation," I said.

"Yes."

"Right side away."

"Yes."

"Left side toward."

"Yes."

"Target was right."

"Yes."

I looked at her.

She kept measuring.

"You can say it."

"I am measuring."

"Hessa."

She wrote two numbers.

Then a third.

"Observed small rotation. Right end displaced away relative to left."

My skin prickled.

"Clean?"

"Clean enough to log as rotation."

"Under still field."

"Yes."

"With right third specified."

"Yes."

"So target matters."

"No."

I closed my eyes.

Of course.

"Target might matter."

"Yes."

"Because the response geometry matches the target."

"It is consistent with that possibility."

"You ruin language."

"I narrow it."

"Same violence."

She ignored me.

I looked at the strip.

It had rotated only a little.

Nothing dramatic.

A person walking past the door would not have noticed.

But it was not the same kind of movement as attempt seventy-four.

That had been a small clean slide.

This had turned.

The right third had gone where I had asked more than the left.

Maybe.

There was the dangerous word.

Maybe.

Hessa checked my pulse.

"Body?"

"Fine."

"Urge to increase?"

"High."

"Why?"

"Because I want to do that again on purpose."

"Can you?"

"I have no idea."

"Good."

"Stop praising ignorance."

"No."

She checked my hands.

Grip.

Forearm.

Shoulder.

Everything remained ordinary.

Inside my head, the rotation was becoming a mechanism.

I had targeted the right.

The right moved.

Therefore I could apply force to a region.

Therefore the effect had spatial resolution.

Therefore I could learn to twist things.

Therefore I could shape pressure.

Therefore I could eventually move larger objects by choosing leverage.

Therefore I was halfway back to being dangerous.

Old Greg could build a staircase out of one paper strip faster than any carpenter.

I recognized the habit.

Did not stop it.

Just watched it happen.

Hessa reset the strip.

Same grid position.

Same long edge.

Same crossmarks.

Same orientation as before seventy-six.

The blue still bead remained steady.

"Attempt seventy-eight," she said.

"Right third again?"

"No."

I looked up.

"Whole strip."

That interrupted the staircase.

"Why?"

"Comparison."

"If whole target slides, and right target rotates..."

"We will have two observations."

Cruel.

"If whole target does nothing?"

"We will have two observations."

"One of which is nothing."

"Nothing is an observation."

"You should put that over the door."

"No."

I settled again.

The clean rotation from seventy-seven kept replaying.

I could feel the imagined motion in my mind.

Not a sensation.

A memory.

Right end away.

Left end toward.

Turn.

The temptation was to recreate it.

But seventy-eight was not right third.

Whole strip.

Away.

Same external attempt.

No extra effort.

I forced myself to stop aiming at the remembered right side.

Whole strip.

Not left.

Not center.

Not right.

The object.

That word was dangerous too.
