Hessa said, "Expectation?"

"Emotionally high."

"Evidence?"

"One clean rotation immediately prior under the same still field, with a different target specification."

"Prediction?"

I thought.

"If specification matters, whole-strip target may produce more translation and less rotation."

"May."

"Yes."

"Anything else?"

"If specification does not matter, I have no useful prediction."

"Good."

"That sounded almost kind."

"Begin."

I worked.

Whole strip.

Away.

Nothing.

I held the permitted effort.

Did not chase.

Did not sharpen.

Did not lean.

The strip remained where Hessa had placed it.

The telltales stayed straight.

The blue bead stayed steady.

No slide.

No turn.

No lift.

The end cue came.

I stopped.

Hessa checked.

Measured.

"No observed response."

I laughed once.

Not because it was funny.

Because it was exactly the kind of answer the universe preferred.

One failure.

One clean rotation.

One failure.

Perfectly shaped to prevent confidence.

"Body?" Hessa asked.

"Fine."

"Urge?"

"Lower."

"Interpretation?"

I looked at the strip.

"Attempt seventy-seven is real."

"Observed rotation is real."

"With right region targeted."

"Yes."

"Seventy-six and seventy-eight do not repeat it."

"Correct."

"That makes ordinary random room movement less likely because still field and telltales."

"Less likely."

"Not impossible."

"Correct."

"Target specification may affect response geometry."

"Possible."

"Not established."

"Correct."

I hated how satisfying that still was.

Not because we knew the answer.

Because the question had become sharper.

Before today, the external effect had mostly been discussed as distance and whether paper moved at all.

Near.

Middle.

Far.

Move.

No move.

Now there was another axis.

What exactly counted as the thing I was trying to affect?

Whole paper?

Part of paper?

A region I named in my own head?

Did the effect care?

Maybe not.

One rotation was not a law.

But the experiment had made the question legitimate.

Hessa turned off the still bead.

The blue center faded.

The left telltale began drifting faintly after several breaths.

She picked up the strip only after recording the final positions.

"Next time?" I asked.

"No."

I stared at her.

"You have developed that answer into art."

"No next protocol selected."

"That is longer."

"More accurate."

"Do you need different material?"

"Maybe."

I smiled.

Hessa looked tired immediately.

"You did it again."

"I used a word."

"That word."

"Leave."

I did not.

Not yet.

I took out the narrow scrap from my bag.

There was not much room left.

Ch304 still-field note.

Ch306 holdfast note.

Now this.

I wrote small.

76 LEFT THIRD: NONE

77 RIGHT THIRD: CLEAN SMALL ROTATION. RIGHT AWAY / LEFT SLIGHT TOWARD.

78 WHOLE STRIP: NONE.

TARGET SPECIFICATION MAY AFFECT RESPONSE SHAPE. ONE RESULT ONLY.

I showed Hessa.

She read it.

"Add `under still field`."

I did.

"Anything else?"

"Do not write `control`."

I had not.

I had been thinking it.

Annoying woman.

I added:

NOT CONTROL YET.

Hessa nodded once.

That felt more valuable than it should have.

I packed the charcoal.

The strip remained on her table.

Not mine.

The witness plate remained.

Still bead.

Telltales.

No souvenir.

No new apparatus.

No assignment.

At the door I stopped.

"Why this question?"

Hessa looked at the strip.

"Because distance has not explained enough."

That was the most generous sentence she had given me all afternoon.

I waited for more.

None came.

Of course.

I left.

*

Outside the Guild, Carrow had continued while I was trying to move one third of paper.

A man was selling hot chestnuts.

Two girls were drawing squares on the paving with red chalk.

A delivery cart had parked badly enough to block half the lane.

Nobody knew attempt seventy-seven existed.

Good.

I walked home slowly because I had no reason to hurry.

The new result tried to become useful every thirty steps.

Could I rotate a latch?

No.

Could I twist a coin?

No.

Could I affect one side of the holdfast?

Absolutely not.

Could I move part of a larger object?

Unknown.

Could I target inside an object?

Unknown.

Could I use leverage?

Unknown.

Could I turn a cup?

Unknown.

Could I make the blue line move without the cloth?

That thought arrived uninvited.

I stopped walking.

Not because it was a good idea.

Because it was a perfect example of a bad one.

Lyssa's customer.

Lyssa's work.

A charm line Greg had spent an entire chapter learning not to adopt.

And now my brain had taken one rotating paper strip and tried to volunteer for it.

I laughed.

A woman passing with a bundle of reeds looked at me.

"Fine," I said.

She had not asked.

I kept walking.

No blue line.

No holdfast.

No latch.

No cup.

Paper first.

Maybe paper for a long time.

That was not cowardice.

It was evidence.

Hessa would approve that sentence.

I chose not to tell her.

At home, Cala was slicing something green.

Tavi was asleep under a thin blanket on the floor.

The wooden spoons had separated.

Civilization restored.

Lyssa had returned and was near the window with a dark roll of cloth across her knees.

Not the green vest.

I did not ask.

She looked at my face.

"Moved?"

"One."

"Good?"

I considered.

"Interesting."

She nodded.

No technical questions.

No request to explain.

I put the bag down.

Cala pointed at the water pot.

"Drink."

Apparently Hessa had infiltrated the house permanently.

I drank.

The one copper and one bit remained upstairs.

The fixed holdfast remained wrapped.

Three clean sheets remained clean.

Nothing had become profitable.

Nothing had become solved.

But the paper had turned.

Not much.

Enough to change the question.

I sat by the window.

Lyssa kept working.

After a while she nudged my right shoe with hers.

Not an apology.

Not a lesson.

Just contact.

I nudged back.

Outside, somebody shouted about onions.

Carrow remained committed to its themes.

I looked at my hands.

Then at nothing.

For once, I did not try to move it.

# CHAPTER 313

## THE LANDING

The cupboard arrived sideways.

That was the first problem.

The second problem was that it was taller than the stairs.

Not actually taller.

Buildings would have been difficult if furniture had to be shorter than stairs.

It was taller than the useful part of the turn.

There was a difference.

I discovered this because somebody shouted, "Stop," directly outside the door.

Then somebody else shouted, "I am stopped."

Then Cala said, "Greg."

I was drinking water.

This was apparently suspicious.

"What?"

"Move."

I moved.

Not far.

The front room was already mostly clear.

Cala had pushed the low stool against the wall and lifted Tavi's wooden cup off the floor.

The door stood open.

Beyond it, the gallery had become furniture.

A tall dark cupboard leaned across the landing at an angle that suggested optimism had recently died.

Its bottom rested on a folded blanket.

Its top was somewhere above my head, pressed close to the outer wall.

Two men held it.

One below.

One above.

A woman stood between them with both hands on her hips.

Another woman was halfway up the next stair, saying things that were not helping.

Tavi stood behind Cala and whispered, "Big."

Correct.

The cupboard was dark brown, nearly black where the wood had been oiled.

Two front doors.

Brass latch.

Rounded feet.

A narrow red stay bead sat above the latch, dull and quiet.

Ordinary travel furniture.

The bead kept the doors from swinging while the cabinet was moved.

It was not strong enough to make the thing lighter.

That would have been too convenient.

The woman with her hands on her hips looked at me.

"You live here?"

"Usually."

Cala pointed at her.

"Neris."

Neris pointed at the cupboard.

"Mine."

"Congratulations."

She did not look congratulated.

One of the men grunted.

"Can we?"

"No," said the man above.

"Still no?"

"Still wall."

The lower man looked offended by architecture.

Cala stepped onto the gallery.

I followed more slowly.

Right crutch.

Left crutch.

Right foot.

The usual.

I kept enough distance from the cupboard that nobody could accidentally turn me into a brace.

The building stair rose from the courtyard, reached our gallery, then turned sharply to continue upward.

The landing was wide enough for people.

Wide enough for baskets.

Wide enough for the red pot, if anyone became criminal.

Not wide enough for Neris's cupboard in its current relationship with geometry.

The top corner was caught under the low inner beam.

The bottom had not cleared the outer rail.

If they lifted the bottom, the top hit beam.

If they lowered the top, the bottom hit rail.

A beautiful arrangement.

"Door?" I asked.

Neris frowned.

"Which?"

"Room."

She pointed up.

"Third."

Of course.

The cupboard had already reached the second-floor landing and decided that was sufficient achievement.

The woman above the turn said, "I told her take the feet off."

Neris looked up.

"You said after it was here."

"I meant before this."

"That is not what after means."

The upper man laughed once.

The cupboard shifted.

Everybody stopped laughing.

The red stay bead flashed faintly.

The doors remained shut.

Good bead.

The lower man said, "Blanket's walking."

I looked down.

The folded blanket under the lower feet had crept half a hand toward the edge.

Not dangerous yet.

Unhelpful.

Cala said, "Tavi inside."

Tavi disappeared.

No argument.

The cupboard had authority.

Cala looked at me.

"Can you watch the stair?"

"Which part?"

"People."

That I could do.

I moved to the courtyard-side edge of the landing, outside the cupboard's possible fall path, and turned enough to see the lower stair.

A boy was already coming up with a basket.

"Wait."

He looked at the cupboard.

"Why?"

I considered the question.

"Because we have temporarily become a wall."

He sat on the third step.

Reasonable.

Another woman reached the bottom of the stairs, saw the situation, and turned around without asking.

Experience.

Behind me, Neris said, "Again."

The lower man lifted.

The upper man guided.

The cupboard rose perhaps two inches.

The top corner touched beam.

"Stop."

"I am stopped."

The lower man said something about the beam's parents.

Cala leaned against the doorway.

"Who brought it last time?"

Neris said, "My brother."

"Where is he?"

"North yard."

"Useful."

"He was until today."

I could see the cabinet's back now.

Three narrow iron straps ran vertically.

Between them, near the lower third, was a pale green square no bigger than my palm.

A dry plate.

Common enough.

Keep clothes from taking damp in bad rooms.

The broad family had been around before my first life ended, though this one looked older and simpler than the ones I remembered.

Nothing about it mattered to the stair.

I did not ask.

The lower man tried to slide the blanket.

The cabinet moved a little.

The red stay bead blinked again.

The upper woman said, "Take doors."

Neris answered immediately.

"No."

"Why?"

"Because pins are sealed."

That made sense.

Some older cupboards used the dry plate and door stay on a shared fitting line. Remove a door carelessly and the line might need reseating.

Not impossible.

Not worth doing on a stair landing because somebody had become impatient.

The upper man said, "Feet?"

Neris looked at the lower feet.

"Maybe."

"Do they unscrew?"

"I don't know."

The woman above said, "They look like feet."

This was technically true and professionally useless.

I looked at the lower right foot.

Rounded wood.

No visible pin from this angle.

The blanket hid the other.

I said, "Can you tilt enough to see under without moving the top?"

The lower man looked at me.

"Maybe."

Neris said, "Don't lift yet."

Good.

She crouched beside the lower foot.

The gallery was too crowded for me to join without becoming part of the problem.

She pulled one corner of the blanket back.

The foot ended in a dark iron plate.

Four small studs.

No screw stem.

"Fixed," she said.

"Looks fixed," I agreed.

The upper woman said, "Hit it."

Four people told her no.

Community.

The boy on the stair began laughing.

"Quiet," I told him.

He laughed quieter.

Progress.

For the next few minutes, the cupboard remained exactly where it was while everyone developed theories.

Angle it more toward the courtyard.

No, rail.

Raise the top.

No, beam.

Turn it face-down.

No, landing width.

Take the rail off.

Cala said no before anyone finished that sentence.

The outer gallery rail had a blue maintenance seal at the nearest post.

I knew the broad category.

Not decorative.

Not ours.

Good enough.

Neris rubbed her forehead.

"I measured the room."

Cala said, "Not the stair."

"I know that now."

The lower man shifted his grip.

"Need rest."

That mattered.

Everything else stopped.

They lowered the cabinet until its weight settled more fully onto the blanket and one padded corner block the upper man had wedged beneath the side.

Both carriers stepped back.

Hands flexing.

Shoulders rolling.

Nobody pretended tired arms were a moral failure.

I liked professionals.

The upper carrier came down two steps and sat.

The lower one leaned against the wall.

Neris went inside our room without asking.

Cala handed her water.

Apparently permission had arrived earlier than I had.

I stayed by the stair.

The boy with the basket said, "Can I go under?"

"No."

"I fit."

"That is not the question."

"I fit a lot."

"Impressive."

He looked pleased.

I had made an error.

Another man came up from the courtyard carrying three bundled lengths of reed.

He stopped beside the boy.

"Moving?"

"Not currently."

He looked at the cupboard.

"Ah."

That was the full diagnosis.

He sat too.

We had become a waiting room.

The carriers drank.

Neris came back out.

"Could bring it down."

The lower carrier nodded.

"Easy."

"Then what?"

Nobody answered.

That was the actual problem.

Not getting it off the landing.

Getting it through the turn.

I looked at the geometry again.

Old Greg had moved siege chests through ruined towers.

Dragged altar stones through broken corridors.

Helped evacuate a library where the shelves had been more valuable than three of the librarians.

He had also destroyed a lot of doors.

Power was useful.

Power encouraged bad carpentry.

This cupboard did not need S-class force.

It needed six inches of somewhere.

I looked at the landing.

Then the room behind me.

Our doorway opened inward.

The cupboard was currently trying to turn entirely within the gallery.

That was why the outer rail and inner beam were fighting over it.

I looked at Cala.

She was already looking at me.

"Inside?" I asked.

She glanced at the cupboard.

Then at our doorway.

Then at the floor.

Neris followed.

"What?"

Cala said, "Use room."

The upper carrier stood.

He looked through our open door.

Then back at the cupboard.

"Back corner in?"

"Maybe," I said.

He ignored me and looked at Cala.

Correct choice.

Cala moved the stool farther.

"Nothing break."

"Excellent operating standard," I said.

She pointed at me.

"You too."

Unfair.

The plan was not complicated.

That did not make it easy.

Instead of forcing the cupboard to turn entirely on the landing, they could reverse the lower half into our front room through the open door.

That would give the top more angle beneath the beam.

Then rotate the cabinet around the doorway.

Then bring it back out facing the upper stair.

A room as temporary turning space.

Not magic.

Not brilliant.

Just part of the building nobody had counted because it had been labeled house instead of stair.

The lower carrier looked at the threshold.

"Blanket catches."

Cala disappeared inside.

She came back with the thin old floor mat from beside the basin.

"Over."

He laid it across the threshold.

The mat made a smoother bridge over the raised wood.

Neris looked at Cala.

"Clean later."

"Yes."

The boy on the stairs said, "I can help."

"No," said everyone.

He accepted this with less grace.

I moved farther down the gallery to keep the path clear.

My job remained people.

I preferred it.

"Lower first," the upper carrier said.

The two men took positions.

Neris stood at the side, one hand near the cabinet but not yet touching.

Cala stayed inside our room to watch the threshold and table corner.

The upper woman finally came down from the third-floor stair.

"What do I do?"

"Nothing," said Neris.

I felt represented.

They lifted.

Not high.

Just enough to take weight off the corner block.

The lower feet slid on the blanket.

The blanket reached the threshold mat.

"Slow."

They went slow.

The bottom of the cupboard crossed into our room by perhaps half a foot.

Then a foot.

The top moved away from the beam.

"Stop."

They stopped.

Neris checked the stay bead.

Still red.

Still holding.

She put her hand flat against the side.

"Now turn."

This was where it became ugly.

The lower man needed to step inside our room while the upper man guided the top across the landing.

The cabinet could not simply spin.

It had to pivot around one padded lower corner while sliding the other.

The kind of movement that made diagrams look dishonest.

I watched.

The right upper corner passed beneath the beam.

Barely.

The left lower foot caught the threshold.

"Stop."

Everyone stopped.

Cala crouched.

"Mat folded."

It had.

One edge had rolled under the iron foot plate.

The carrier could not pull it free while holding weight.

Neris looked around.

Her eyes landed on me.

"No."

I had not spoken.

"You looked useful."

"This is discrimination."

"Hook mat."

That I could do.

I checked the cupboard.

Checked the path.

Then used the lower curve of my right crutch to catch the loose mat edge from outside the load path.

Not pulling the cabinet.

Not bracing it.

Just the mat.

"Ready?"

"Yes."

I drew the mat flat.

The folded edge came free.

Cala pressed it down with her palm after the foot cleared.

"Good."

I withdrew the crutch.

My contribution to furniture moving had peaked.

The cabinet turned another few inches.

The top cleared the beam.

The lower half went deeper into our room.

For one alarming moment, the whole cupboard stood diagonally across our doorway with one carrier inside, one outside, Neris trapped on the wrong side, and Cala telling Tavi from somewhere behind the table that no, he could not touch it.

Then the upper corner passed.

The cabinet faced the upper stairs.

Everyone exhaled.

The boy below clapped.

"Do not reward them yet," I said.

The upper carrier laughed.

They moved the cupboard back out of our room.

Across the landing.

Then onto the next stair.

The problem was not over.

It had merely become someone else's floor.

Neris went first.

The upper woman followed.

Both carriers climbed.

The cupboard rose one step at a time.

Red stay bead quiet.

Dark doors closed.

At the third-floor turn, somebody shouted, "Stop."

The lower carrier shouted, "I am stopped."

I looked at Cala.

She looked at me.

We both waited.

Then Neris shouted down, "Fine."

The cupboard disappeared upward.

The stair became stairs again.

The boy with the basket stood immediately.

"Finally."

"You are welcome for our wall service."

He passed me.

The reed-bundle man followed.

Three more people appeared from nowhere.

Traffic had memory.

Within a minute, the landing was ordinary.

Except our floor mat was crooked.

The stool was against the wrong wall.

The front room smelled faintly of old wood oil.

And a long dark scrape crossed the threshold where one iron foot plate had dragged before the mat flattened.

Cala looked at it.

I looked at it.

She rubbed it once with her thumb.

Surface only.

"Fine."

"Good."

She pointed at the mat.

I moved it back with one crutch.

Tavi emerged.

"Big gone."

"Yes."

He looked up the stair.

"Again?"

"No."

This disappointed him.

*

Neris came down perhaps half an hour later.

No cupboard.

Good sign.

She carried the folded blanket and our floor mat.

The blanket was hers.

The mat was ours.

She handed Cala the mat.

"Room."

Cala nodded.

"Stair bad."

Neris nodded.

"Room good."

This was apparently the official report.

One of the carriers came behind her, rubbing his left forearm.

Not injured.

Tired.

He looked at me.

"Good hook."

I looked at my crutch.

"Years of specialized mat training."

He nodded solemnly.

"I could tell."

Neris snorted.

No coin appeared.

No one asked what I charged.

No one asked whether I moved cupboards professionally.

This was healthy.

Neris looked at Cala.

"I bring soup."

Cala said, "No need."

"I bring."

Cala shrugged.

Negotiations concluded.

Neris went upstairs again.

The carrier left for the courtyard.

I sat.

Not because I had done much.

Because standing in one place on crutches while other people moved heavy furniture was still standing in one place on crutches.

My palms had the faint compressed feeling of a longer hold.

No pain.

Right leg fine.

Residual limb fine.

I opened my hands.

Closed them.

Cala put the mat back by the basin.

Tavi began trying to move the stool.

"Stop," Cala said.

"I stopped," he said.

I laughed hard enough that my right hand slipped on the crutch grip.

Not enough to matter.

Cala looked at me.

"Your fault."

"Absolutely."

Tavi pushed the stool one inch.

"Stopped."

"Monster," I said.

He pushed it back.

*

I went out later.

Not for a job.

Not for Hessa.

Not for the cupboard.

The building had simply become loud enough that I wanted a quieter street.

The one copper and one bit stayed upstairs.

The fixed holdfast stayed wrapped.

My bag came with me because it usually did.

The narrow scrap inside had the marked-strip notes.

I did not take it out.

At the end of our lane, two delivery women were arguing over whether a stack of round baskets belonged to number eight or number eighteen.

A barber had hung white cloth from his awning to catch the afternoon light.

Someone was roasting peppers.

A little brass shop charm over a doorway clicked every time a customer entered and twice every time a customer left.

It was probably broken.

Nobody looked concerned.

I kept walking.

At a narrow crossing, a woman with a handcart waited for me to clear the curb cut.

I cleared it.

She passed.

No story.

A group of children had chalked a long crooked road across the paving and were making small clay animals follow it.

One animal had five legs.

Tavi would have respected the craft.

I sat for a while on a public bench under a patched shade cloth.

A man at the other end was asleep with his hat over his face.

Good bench etiquette.

I thought about the cupboard once.

Not the dry plate.

Not the stay bead.

The room.

Everyone had stared at the landing because the problem was on the landing.

The useful space had been somewhere else.

That thought tried to become magic.

I refused it.

Mostly.

Target specification.

Useful space.

Angles.

No.

Hessa would kill me by narrowing a sentence until it became a blade.

I let the cupboard remain a cupboard.

That was allowed.

The sleeping man snored once.

A cart rolled by with three blue glass jars suspended from a hanging rack.

Each jar swayed.

The rack did not.

Good suspension work.

I did not investigate.

Eventually I stood.

Hands fine again.

I went home.

*

Neris did bring soup.

Too much soup.

A deep clay bowl sat on our table when I returned.

Cala had already transferred half of it into the red pot.

"Payment?" I asked.

"No."

"Gift?"

"Soup."

Stronger category.

Lyssa was home.

The dark cloth from yesterday was gone.

Not explained.

Good.

She ate with one knee tucked under the bench and listened while Cala described the cupboard.

Cala's version made the entire event six sentences.

Mine would have been longer.

This was why she got invited places.

"Tall?" Lyssa asked.

Cala held one hand above her head.

Tavi stood on the bench and held both hands higher.

"Very," he said.

Lyssa looked at the threshold scrape.

"That new?"

"Surface," Cala said.

Lyssa rubbed it with her toe.

"Fine."

Consensus.

I ate soup.

It had white beans, greens, and too much pepper.

Good.

Neris had apparently decided gratitude should hurt slightly.

Tavi said, "Stop."

Nobody moved.

He looked around.

"I stopped."

Lyssa started laughing first.

Then Cala.

Then me.

Tavi had discovered power.

This would end badly.

Later, when the bowl was empty, Cala set it by the door.

Return tomorrow.

No mystery.

No network to investigate.

Just a bowl.

Upstairs, the fixed holdfast remained where I had left it.

The narrow scrap remained in the bag.

Three clean sheets remained clean.

One copper and one bit remained money instead of becoming opportunity.

I lay back for a while.

My hands were normal.

The house above us creaked once.

Then twice.

Something heavy moved across the third-floor room.

I listened.

No shout.

No crash.

No cupboard descending through the ceiling.

Success.

I thought about how much effort it had taken to move one ordinary piece of furniture up one ordinary building.

Two carriers.

Neris.

Cala.

A blanket.

A floor mat.

A doorway.

One excellent crutch hook.

No heroics.

No spell.

No breakthrough.

The cupboard was upstairs because enough small things had been useful at the same time.

That was a kind of strength I had not respected enough in my first life.

Maybe because I had spent too long being able to replace six people with force.

Maybe because force made every room look optional.

Below, Tavi shouted, "Stop."

Cala shouted back, "Sleep."

Silence.

A stronger magic.

I closed my eyes.

The cupboard stayed upstairs.

# CHAPTER 314

## THE DRY HOUR

The water stopped while I was washing one spoon.

This was rude.

Not dangerous.

Not dramatic.

Just rude.

I had soap on the spoon.

I turned the little brass tap farther.

Nothing.

Turned it back.

Nothing.

Tapped the pipe once because humans had been doing that to uncooperative objects since before language.

Still nothing.

Cala looked over from the table.

"Dry?"

"Apparently."

She did not move.

That was suspicious.

"You knew."

"Bell."

"What bell?"

"The one you ignored."

I considered defending myself.

There had been a bell.

Probably.

Carrow had too many bells.

Meal bells.

Gate bells.

Weather bells.

Guild bells.

Fire bells.

Theatre bells.

People who owned one bell and had developed opinions.

"You could have said water."

"I did."

"When?"

"Before you washed spoon."

I looked down at the spoon.

The spoon had betrayed me.

Tavi leaned over the table.

"Dry."

"Thank you."

"Dry spoon."

"It is wet with soap."

"Bad."

"Correct."

Cala pointed toward the door.

"Lower court."

"What is in the lower court?"

"Water."

That improved the conversation.

I left the spoon in the basin.

Lyssa was not home.

The front room still showed no evidence that a cupboard had passed through it yesterday except for the dark scrape across the threshold, which Cala continued to regard as beneath human concern.

The old floor mat was back beside the basin.

Neris's soup bowl was gone.

Returned already, apparently.

No ceremony.

Good.

I took the small household jug from its hook.

Cala looked at it.

Then at me.

"No."

I stopped.

"Why?"

"Big jug."

She pointed under the table.

There was a larger clay jug with two loop handles.

I looked at it.

Then at my crutches.

Then at Cala.

"No."

She nodded.

Agreement.

Excellent household management.

She reached behind the door and pulled out the little shoulder-slung water sack instead.

Not mine.

Old household thing.

Waxed cloth.

Flat when empty.

A narrow strap.

"Two fills," she said.

"That sounds suspiciously like work."

"Water."

"Yes. I recognized the substance."

Tavi said, "Dry."

I put the sack over my right shoulder, opposite my regular brown bag because I did not need both straps fighting under my arm.

Then I reconsidered.

The regular bag stayed home.

No reason to carry charcoal and old magic notes to fetch water.

The water sack hung flat against my right hip.

Fine.

Cala said, "Lower court. Blue cart."

"Blue cart."

"Not red."

"What happens if I use the red cart?"

She looked at me for a long moment.

"I don't know."

That was less comforting than an answer.

I went downstairs.

The building courtyard was already louder than usual.

Three doors stood open.

A woman on the first floor had two buckets lined against the wall.

Somebody above shouted, "Still dry?"

Somebody below shouted, "Still asking?"

Community.

The lower street was worse.

Not bad.

Just occupied.

People had come out carrying every object in Carrow capable of holding water.

Buckets.

Jugs.

Lidded pots.

A narrow-necked bottle the size of Tavi.

One man had a cooking basin tucked under his arm and the expression of someone prepared to defend the decision.

The regular public spout at the corner was dead.

Its little green pressure square sat gray above the outlet.

A chalk board had been hung beside it.

LINE FLUSH
SECOND TO FOURTH BELL
LOWER COURT CART
HOUSEHOLD DRAW ONLY

Under that, in different handwriting:

STOP TURNING THE SPOUT

I looked at the brass handle.

It had been turned so many times it was wet with fingerprints despite producing no water.

People were optimists.

The lower court sat behind the dye lane, a broad paved square mostly used by handcarts when the market street became too crowded.

Today it had become a temporary well.

The blue cart was easy to find.

Mostly because it was blue.

Good system.

A squat tank filled the cart bed.

Four iron-banded barrels sat behind it.

Two city line workers stood beside a waist-high distribution frame with three taps.

Not Guild.

Not guards.

Gray work coats with blue shoulder tabs.

A little brass pressure plate had been clipped to the frame.

Its center glowed pale blue.

Water infrastructure.

Old Greg knew the category.

Portable head tank.

Pressure limiter.

Probably a self-closing draw valve.

Probably.

The later versions I remembered were larger and meaner and occasionally exploded when military quartermasters ignored cold-weather procedures.

This one looked content to distribute drinking water.

I did not ask it about war.

There were two lines.

One short.

One long.

I joined the short one.

A woman behind me said, "Household?"

I looked at her.

She pointed at my sack.

"Household line is there."

She pointed at the long line.

Of course.

"What is this one?"

"Shops."

I moved.

The short line had been too beautiful.

The household line curved around the edge of the court.

Maybe twenty people.

Not terrible.

I took my place behind an older man carrying two narrow copper cans on a shoulder yoke.

He looked at my water sack.

"One?"

"Two fills."

He nodded.

Apparently Cala had not invented that.

Ahead of us, a line worker called, "House mark or two-hand vessel. No casks."

A woman three places forward raised a bucket.

"What is a two-hand vessel?"

"If you need two hands to carry it empty, no."

She looked at the bucket.

Then at the worker.

"I can carry it one."

"Empty?"

"Yes."

"Then fine."

Rules were alive.

The line moved.

Slowly.

Not because the taps were slow.

Because everybody had questions.

"Can I fill the kettle after the jug?"

"Same household?"

"Yes."

"Together under the mark."

"What mark?"

"Your building mark."

"I don't have it."

"Then one vessel."

"What if my sister has it?"

"Then your sister is better prepared."

The other line worker said, "Marn."

"What?"

"Be kind."

"I am distributing free water during a line flush."

"That is not the same thing."

Marn considered this.

Then looked at the woman.

"One vessel now. Bring the mark for more."

"Thank you."

"See? Kind."

The second worker rolled her eyes.

I liked them.

A child near the front dropped a tin cup.

It bounced three times.

Nobody died.

His mother picked it up and put it inside her basket.

"You are not drinking from the ground."

"I wasn't."

"Good."

The cup went into her basket.

The older man ahead of me shifted his yoke.

The copper cans were empty but not light.

"You want ahead?"

I looked at him.

"No."

"You are standing on sticks."

"Crutches."

"Still sticks."

He had a point.

"I'm fine."

He looked at my right leg.

Then the left trouser leg pinned cleanly below the knee.

Then back at my face.

"Your funeral."

"Very small funeral."

He snorted.

The line moved another three people.

My palms were fine.

Shoulders fine.

Residual limb fine.

Standing in one place was sometimes worse than moving, but not after five minutes.

I shifted my right foot.

Changed the crutch angle.

Normal.

Across the court, the shop line moved faster.

A baker's boy filled two square lidded cans.

A woman in a leather apron argued over whether a dye rinse counted as household use.

It did not.

She knew it did not.

Her argument was mostly athletic.

The worker at that tap pointed toward a second chalk board.

TRADE DRAW: SOUTH TANK
TOKEN REQUIRED

The woman read it.

Then continued arguing.

Reading was not surrender.

Behind me, somebody said, "They flushed north line last month."

Another voice answered, "That was north."

"Water is water."

"Then drink north."

This continued.

I looked up.

A thin run of blue light moved along the exposed pipe strapped beneath the court wall.

Not mana.

Not mine.

Line charge.

The light traveled left to right, paused at a junction box, then vanished.

A moment later the brass pressure plate on the cart frame brightened.

The worker named Marn glanced at it.

"Hold."

All three taps closed.

The court complained as one organism.

Marn held up both hands.

"Pressure swap."

Nobody died of thirst in the next twelve seconds.

Barely.

The blue center of the plate dimmed.

Then brightened.

The other worker opened one tap into a shallow drain tray.

Water spat.

Cloudy for half a breath.

Then clear.

She let it run longer.

Clear.

She touched a small white test bead to the stream.

The bead stayed white.

"Good."

Marn reopened the household taps.

The line moved again.

I had seen larger versions of the same practice.

Army camps.

Field hospitals.

Siege towns where bad water killed more people than arrows and everybody insisted the arrows were more heroic.

Flush the line.

Bleed the dirty first run.

Test.

Then drink.

Nothing mysterious.

The local bead was neat.

I did not need to know how neat.

A young man two places ahead filled a tall ceramic jug.

When he lifted it, the bottom gave a small wet click.

He froze.

The line froze with him.

A hairline crack darkened along one side.

Not catastrophic.

Yet.

Marn pointed.

"Down."

The young man lowered it immediately.

Good.

Water began to bead through the crack.

"Oh, come on."

"Do not lift it."

"It's my good jug."

"It is becoming your broken jug."

The second worker slid a shallow wooden tray beneath it.

"Have another vessel?"

"No."

A woman beside him held up a squat pot.

"Use mine."

He looked at her.

"You need it."

"I have two."

"That one is soup."

"It was soup."

Marn said, "Transfer here. Slowly."

The young man looked miserable.

"I just filled it."

"Yes."

"That was the point."

"Still is."

They used a broad tin dipper from the cart kit.

No magic.

No clever rescue.

Just transfer the water before the crack opened farther.

The line waited.

Nobody shouted.

Well.

One person shouted.

"How long?"

Marn shouted back, "Longer now."

That solved nothing but improved my mood.

The older man with the yoke leaned slightly toward me.

"Good jug."

"Apparently."

"Was."

"Also apparently."

He smiled.

When the transfer was done, the cracked jug sat empty in the tray.

The young man hugged the borrowed pot to his chest.

"Thank you."

The woman waved him away.

"Bring it back."

"I will."

"You said that about my knife."

"I brought the knife."

"Three months."

"It came back."

There were relationships everywhere if you stood still long enough.

That was annoying.

Also useful.

My turn came.

Marn looked at the flat sack.

"House?"

"Second-floor Cala."

She pointed at a little board beside the tap.

Rows of scratched marks.

I found ours because Cala had shown me months ago after I had mistaken it for a carpenter's tally.

Two shallow cuts crossing one deeper diagonal.

Building draw mark.

Not family crest.

Not ownership.

Just municipal shorthand for a cluster of rooms tied to one regular line account.

I touched it.

Marn nodded.

"Two fills. One now, one after."

"After what?"

"Drink it."

I stared at her.

She stared back.

Then the second worker laughed.

"Marn."

Marn smiled.

"Fill. Empty into home storage. Come back."

"That was almost funny."

"It was very funny."

The sack had a stiff wooden mouth with a short cord loop.

I set one crutch against my right forearm, kept the other planted, and held the sack mouth beneath the tap.

Marn watched.

"Need hand?"

"No."

She did not reach.

Good.

She opened the valve.

Water hit harder than expected.

The sack jumped.

I tightened my grip.

"Violent water."

"Pressure plate is showing off."

The sack rounded against my hip.

Not huge.

Maybe enough for cooking and drinking until the regular line returned.

Marn shut the tap before it overfilled.

I tied the mouth.

Tested the knot.

The weight changed everything.

Not my balance exactly.

My right shoulder.

The sack pulled downward and inward.

Bad side for free swing.

I adjusted the strap shorter.

Moved the sack farther toward my back.

Better.

The older man with the yoke watched.

"Still fine?"

"Ask me upstairs."

"Funeral moved?"

"Third bell."

He laughed.

His two cans filled next.

I left the court carefully.

No heroics.

No racing.

Wet pavement near the drain tray.

Crowded lane.

A loaded water sack that could swing into a crutch if I let it.

So I did not let it.

Shorter steps.

Right foot planted clean.

Crutches slightly wider where space allowed.

The sack bumped my hip twice before I found the rhythm.

By the time I reached our building, I had decided water was heavier than its reputation deserved.

Cala opened the door before I knocked.

She looked at the sack.

"One."

"Yes."

"Two."

"I remember."

She took it only after I had both crutches planted and had loosened the strap myself.

Also good.

She poured the water into the covered household crock near the back wall.

Not all of it.

Some into the smaller kitchen jug.

Some into Tavi's cup because apparently he had been five minutes from becoming dust.

He drank.

"Water."

"Remarkable."

Cala handed me the empty sack.

"Again."

"You have become drunk with power."

"Water."

That word continued winning arguments.

I went back.

The second trip was easier because I knew the load.

The household line was shorter.

The cracked-jug man was gone.

The borrowed soup pot was gone.

The copper-can man was filling his second pair or perhaps somebody else's.

He saw me.

"Alive."

"Disappointing."

Marn lifted the tap.

"Second?"

"Second."

"See? System."

"I resent effective systems."

"You live in a city."

"Constant suffering."

The second fill went cleanly.

On the walk home, the regular corner spout coughed.

Everybody nearby turned.

It spat air.

Then brownish water.

Then stopped.

A woman reached for the handle.

Three people said, "Don't."

She withdrew her hand.

Good community.

A blue-coated line worker farther down the street shouted, "Not open yet. Wait for clear mark."

The green pressure square above the spout stayed gray.

Simple.

Visible.

A system that assumed people would still turn the handle anyway.

Realistic.

Back upstairs, Cala emptied the second sack.

That put enough water in the covered crock for the rest of the dry hour.

Maybe more.

I hung the sack where it had been.

My right shoulder felt used.

Not hurt.

Used.

Hands fine.

Residual limb fine.

Right leg fine.

I sat.

Tavi climbed onto the stool beside me.

"Dry?"

"Temporarily."

He thought about that.

Then pointed toward the basin.

"Spoon."

The soapy spoon still waited.

I had forgotten it.

Cala had not.

Of course.

I took the little kitchen jug.

Poured perhaps half a cup over the spoon.

Washed it.

Rinsed it into the basin.

Tavi watched with grave attention.

"Water."

"Yes."

"Good."

"Usually."

Outside, a bell rang.

Cala tilted her head.

"Line."

I waited.

Another bell.

Shorter.

Then from the street below came the unmistakable sound of twenty people testing taps at once.

Water hammered through the building pipe.

The brass tap above our basin shuddered.

Cala pointed at it.

"Wait."

"I have learned fear."

She counted under her breath.

Then opened the tap only a little.

Air hissed.

Cloudy water sputtered into the basin.

She let it run.

Clear.

A small green glass inset beside the pipe brightened.

Not a test bead.

Just the house line mark returning live.

Cala closed the tap.

"Good."

That was it.

No quest.

No broken infrastructure.

No hidden conspiracy in municipal water.

The line had been flushed.

The temporary cart had done its job.

The city remained hydrated despite several people's best efforts.

I looked at the clean spoon.

One spoon.

Two trips.

A courtyard full of vessels.

Three taps.

A cracked jug.

A pressure swap.

Old Greg had once crossed a poisoned valley because an army could not find water.

This Greg had carried two sacks downstairs and back.

The second task was smaller.

It still mattered before dinner.

I put the spoon away.

Then I drank a cup of water because spite was not hydration.

# CHAPTER 315

## THE TRUE STORY

The first lie was about a chicken.

This made sense.

Chickens had been supporting human dishonesty for generations.

The man telling it sat on an overturned flour bucket outside a tea stall, one boot planted on the street and the other hooked around the bucket rim. He had a narrow face, a yellow scarf, and the solemn expression of someone reporting a military disaster.

"Three eggs," he said.

A woman beside him shook her head.

"Too many."

"Three. Every morning. Same hen."

"Already bad."

"Wait."

I had stopped because Cala had sent me for salt and because the salt seller was still arguing with a delivery boy over whether two sacks counted as late if one had technically arrived yesterday.

I had time.

The tea stall had six stools.

All occupied.

The flour bucket made seven seats if dignity was negotiable.

I stood at the edge with my crutches and listened.

The yellow-scarf man lowered his voice.

"Fourth egg came at noon."

Someone behind the tea counter gasped theatrically.

The woman beside him said, "Disqualified."

"You haven't heard the fourth egg."

"A chicken laying four eggs in one day is lazy lying."

"It was blue."

"Worse."

"And warm enough to cook the other three."

The whole group groaned.

I laughed.

That was my mistake.

The woman turned.

She was perhaps fifty, perhaps older, with gray braided through black hair and a narrow clay cup balanced on one knee.

"You laugh, you tell."

I looked behind me.

Nobody.

"That seems like a dangerous law."

"Tea law."

"I did not buy tea."

The man behind the counter lifted a cup.

"Water free. Law still applies."

Corruption.

I looked toward the salt seller.

Still arguing.

The delivery boy had now opened one sack as evidence of something.

I should have left.

Instead I said, "What are the rules?"

The woman smiled.

"Best lie wins."

"Wins what?"

"Nothing."

"Excellent prize."

"Has to sound true long enough."

Yellow Scarf pointed at her.

"That is not the rule."

"It is today."

"Yesterday was impossible lie."

"Yesterday you lost."

"Because nobody respects maritime knowledge."

The tea seller said, "You said the fish owned a boat."

"It did."

"See? Still lying."

I had lived fifty-nine years the first time.

I had crossed countries that no longer existed in the same shape I remembered.

I had fought things with too many mouths.

I had slept inside a dead titan's rib cage because the weather outside was worse.

I had once spent nine days trapped in a tower where every staircase returned to the floor below unless you walked backward while insulting the architect.

I had watched a priest negotiate with his own shadow.

I had eaten soup beside a dragon who claimed not to be a dragon and became offended when anyone agreed.

I was, objectively, overqualified.

"Fine," I said.

The woman pointed to an empty patch of wall beside the stall.

"Stand there."

"I am already standing."

"Then you are prepared."

I shifted one crutch slightly.

The group settled.

There were eight of them if I counted the tea seller.

Yellow Scarf.

Gray-Braid.

A narrow old man with a red nose.

Two women sharing a plate of something fried.

A boy maybe fifteen with ink on three fingers.

A broad man in a flour-dusted vest.

Tea Seller.

No one asked my name.

This was refreshing.

I considered inventing something.

That seemed wrong.

Not morally.

Structurally.

I had too much material.

So I chose a true story.

"Once," I said, "I spent three days being hunted by a door."

Silence.

Gray-Braid narrowed her eyes.

Yellow Scarf said, "Bad start."

"It was a very determined door."

"Worse."

I continued.

The door had belonged to a ruined watch post north of Seln.

Not this Seln.

The old one.

Or perhaps the name had changed twice since.

It had been bound to keep intruders out.

Then the wall collapsed.

The door remained.

The binding remained.

The definition of out became complicated.

I did not explain that part.

I told them the door followed our camp at night.

Not quickly.

Doors rarely had good pursuit speed.

But we would wake and find it standing twenty feet closer.

Next night, closer again.

We moved camp.

The door came.

The boy with ink fingers leaned forward.

Good.

I described how one of our scouts tied a bell to the handle.

The bell rang all night.

Not because the door moved constantly.

Because the wind existed.

This made everyone angry.

That part was also true.

"Then what?" one fried-food woman asked.

"We burned it."

Gray-Braid frowned.

"Too easy."

"Didn't work."

Better.

The binding had treated fire as an intruder.

The door shut on it.

This made no physical sense and had still happened.

I remembered Jalen staring at the smoking door and saying, with deep sincerity, that he hated architecture.

I almost said his name.

Did not.

"So what did you do?" Tea Seller asked.

"Opened it."

The group stared.

"That was the trick?" Yellow Scarf said.

"No. Opening it made things worse."

The door decided we were inside.

Then it began trying to keep everything else out.

Rain.

Birds.

A mule.

The mule won.

Barely.

The old man with the red nose started laughing before I reached the mule.

I smiled.

This was going well.

Then I told them how the door finally stopped following us when we rebuilt enough wall around it to give the binding a stable inside and outside again.

We left a freestanding rectangle of stone in the middle of a field.

Door closed.

Happy.

Problem solved.

I finished.

Nobody spoke.

Then Gray-Braid said, "True."

I blinked.

"What?"

"You told a true story."

"The game is lying."

"Exactly."

Yellow Scarf pointed at me.

"Disqualified."

"How do you know?"

Tea Seller shrugged.

"Too many bad details."

"Bad details?"

"Mule."

"There was a mule."

"See?"

The group laughed.

I felt personally attacked by accuracy.

Gray-Braid sipped her tea.

"A liar makes the door smarter."

"It was not smart."

"Exactly."

"It was a badly defined ward attached to a door."

Yellow Scarf slapped his knee.

"He keeps proving it."

I looked at the salt seller.

The delivery argument had ended.

I could leave.

I did not.

"Fine," I said. "Again."

Gray-Braid smiled.

"One each."

"That rule was not stated."

"Tea law."

"Your legal system is corrupt."

"True."

Apparently she could say that without being disqualified.

The boy with ink fingers went next.

His lie involved a clerk who accidentally stamped himself and became municipal property.

Strong opening.

He kept the details small.

The clerk had to sleep in a records room because the stamp said STORAGE.

His wife brought blankets.

The office charged her one bit for after-hours access.

I believed this completely.

That was the problem.

"True," I said when he finished.

Everyone booed me.

The boy grinned.

"Lie."

"That is absolutely something an office would do."

"Still lie."

I disliked the game.

The broad flour-dusted man told one about a baker who could smell tomorrow's rain in uncooked dough.

I knew three bakers who could do versions of that with pressure, humidity, yeast behavior, and low-grade weather sensitivity.

"True," I said.

Wrong again.

One fried-food woman told us her sister had married a man because his house shadow fell north at noon.

That sounded like a curse-selection custom from western hill country.

"True."

Wrong.

Yellow Scarf stared at me.

"Do you believe everything?"

"Apparently only lies."

Gray-Braid laughed into her cup.

The old man with the red nose finally introduced himself as Bel.

Only because Tea Seller called him Bel while accusing him of cheating.

Bel's lie was simple.

"I was handsome at nineteen."

Everyone immediately shouted, "Lie."

Bel won the round.

I objected.

"There was no evidence."

Gray-Braid said, "Look at him."

Bel bowed from his stool.

This was not a fair court.

The salt seller had begun serving people again.

I should have gone.

Instead Tea Seller pushed a cup of water toward the edge of the counter.

"Free."

I took it.

The cup was cool clay.

No charm.

No bead.

Just shade and water.

I drank.

A woman passing with a basket stopped long enough to hear Yellow Scarf begin another story.

This one involved a bridge toll collector and a ghost.

The ghost refused to pay because dead people were exempt from road maintenance.

The collector disagreed.

The argument lasted until sunrise.

Then the ghost vanished and the collector billed the cemetery.

I waited.

Everyone looked at me.

"What?"

Gray-Braid said, "True or lie?"

I considered.

"Lie."

The group cheered.

Yellow Scarf looked offended.

"True."

I stared at him.

"No."

"My uncle."

"Your uncle billed a cemetery for a ghost's toll?"

"No. My uncle was the ghost."

The group exploded.

Yellow Scarf could barely finish because he was laughing too.

"You are lying now."

"Yes."

"So the first part?"

"Also lie."

"Then I was right."

"But for the wrong lie."

I hated everyone.

It was excellent.

By the time I remembered the salt, the sun had moved half a building.

I bought the household packet with Cala's money.

The salt seller wrapped it in brown paper and tied it with thin reed fiber.

"You were over there a while."

"I was being professionally deceived."

"Tea stall?"

"Yes."

"Bel still claiming he was handsome?"

I looked at him.

"You know Bel?"

"Everybody knows Bel."

Of course.

I tucked the salt packet under my shirt because both hands belonged to crutches.

The walk home took longer than necessary.

Not because of the body.

Body fine.

Hands fine.

Residual limb fine.

Because I kept thinking about the door.

It had happened.

I remembered the field.

The wall stones.

Jalen's burned glove.

The mule refusing to go near the thing afterward.

I remembered being thirty-something and already strong enough that a wandering warded door had been an irritation rather than a threat.

I had told the story for years.

Usually to adventurers.

They believed it because they had seen worse.

Today a group of ordinary Carrow people had heard the same truth and rejected it because the details were too stupid to be invented well.

That felt fair.

My first life contained too much material that sounded badly written.

The dragon who denied being a dragon.

The staircase.

The priest's shadow.

The duke's shoe in the ceremonial basket.

A fortress whose west wall had been built six feet inside the property line because two survey charms disagreed and neither mason wanted to be legally responsible for choosing.

All true.

Terrible lies.

I reached home.

Cala took the salt.

"Long."

"Tea law."

She nodded immediately.

That was worse.

"You know them?"

"Who?"

"Tea stall. Bel. Gray braid. Yellow scarf."

"Some."

"Of course."

Tavi was on the floor arranging three wooden blocks and one spoon into what appeared to be a defensive position.

Lyssa sat near the window with dark cloth across her lap.

Not green vest.

Still not my work.

She looked up.

"What happened?"

"I lost a lying contest by telling the truth."

She stared at me.

Then nodded.

"Yes."

"You have too much confidence in this outcome."

"What truth?"

"Door hunted me for three days."

She stopped sewing.

"What?"

Finally.

I told it again.

Shorter.

Door.

Ward.

Wall gone.

Inside and outside confused.

Camp pursuit.

Fire failure.

Mule.

Rebuilt wall.

Lyssa listened with her needle held still.

When I finished, she said, "That is true."

"Why does everyone know?"

"Because you explain too much."

"The explanation is the interesting part."

"That is why."

I considered lying about being offended.

Probably would not work.

Cala said, "Bel handsome?"

"Apparently not."

She laughed.

Tavi looked up.

"Door red."

I pointed at him.

"Finally, a reliable witness."

He returned to his fortification.

Later, after the salt was in its jar and the household had moved on, I found myself trying to construct a proper lie.

Something small.

Believable.

No dragons.

No cursed architecture.

No S-class disasters.

I tried:

I once owned a blue hat.

Too boring.

I once stole a blue hat.

Better.

I once stole a blue hat from a priest.

Too much.

The priest stole it back.

Now it sounded true.

Damn.

Lyssa passed behind me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You are making the face."

"What face?"

"Thinking wrong."

"I am trying to lie better."

"Why?"

"Professional development."

She kept walking.

"Start smaller."

"That is what Gray-Braid said."

"Who?"

"I don't know."

That was true too.

The next afternoon I passed the tea stall again.

I did not stop.

Not because I was avoiding them.

I had somewhere else to be.

No appointment.

No job.

Just bread to bring home before Cala decided I had become unreliable around errands.

Bel saw me from his bucket.

"Door boy!"

I kept walking.

"I have a better one!"

"I don't believe you!"

The whole stall laughed.

I laughed too.

Then I turned the corner and bought the bread.

Nobody paid me.

Nobody hired me.

Nobody asked what rank I had been.

For twenty minutes, a dead watch-post door had been more interesting than I was.

That felt healthy.

On the way home I decided I would return someday with a proper lie.

Not tomorrow.

Not because the story needed it.

Just because Bel was going to regret teaching me the rules.

I had fifty-nine years of truth to work around.

Surely I could invent one decent chicken.

# CHAPTER 316

## THE EAST SEAT

Sevren arrived with a seat and terrible timing.

I had one bit.

The seat cost one bit.

This was how money developed personality.

He found me outside the building just after breakfast, leaning against the wall while Cala shook a rug over the gallery rail.

Sevren lifted two fingers.

"Seat."

I looked at him.

"That is not enough information."

"East loop."

Better.

"How far?"

"Bell Ford."

That was farther than Three Wells.

Not dramatically farther in the way maps liked to pretend distance was a personality trait. Farther in the practical sense.

Two days out if the road behaved.

One night there.

Two days back if the road continued behaving.

Roads rarely respected optimism.

"Passenger?"

"Passenger."

"Work?"

"No."

"Freight?"

"Not yours."

"Signing?"

"No."

"Carrying?"

"No."

"Solving mysterious road problem that nobody mentions until we are twenty miles away?"

Sevren stared at me.

"You make travel sound exhausting."

"Experience."

Cala stopped shaking the rug.

"How much?"

Sevren said, "One bit."

There it was again.

My entire liquid non-copper fortune.

One copper upstairs.

One bit upstairs.

Two copper currently transformed into a wrapped bad tool on a high shelf.

Vale debt still existed.

Debt credit was not cash.

No client waited.

No guaranteed work waited.

The sensible answer was no.

I knew this immediately.

Then Sevren said, "Leaves tomorrow before first bell."

Damn.

"Why is there a seat?"

"Rear bench. Small freight this run. Driver already takes two riders to Three Wells. One gets off there. Seat empty after."

"Driver?"

"Not Ressa."

Good.

Not because I disliked Ressa.

Because the world containing more than one carter was reassuring.

"Who?"

"Daro Venn."

Name meant nothing to me.

"Trust him?"

"Enough to sleep."

That was a meaningful courier endorsement.

Cala hung the rug over the rail.

"Go."

I looked at her.

"You are supposed to protect me from financial decisions."

"No."

"This household has failed."

Sevren smiled.

"One bit includes road meals at Three Wells and Bell Ford relay. Not Carrow food. Bring water."

"Sleeping?"

"Relay floor first night if beds full. Bell Ford bunk included if space. Floor if not."

"Luxury."

"You asked."

Lyssa came through the door carrying a folded dark cloth.

Not green vest.

The universe remained restrained.

She looked from Sevren to me.

"Road?"

"Apparently my face has become readable."

Sevren said, "Bell Ford."

Lyssa looked at me.

Then at my crutches.

Then back at me.

"You want?"

That was the problem.

I did.

Not because Bell Ford contained destiny.

I had been to more impressive places.

First life had ruined me for impressive places.

Cities built into cliffs.

Cities floating badly.

A monastery on a glacier that moved three feet every summer and forced the monks to rebuild the stairs.

Bell Ford was a road town with a bridge.

I wanted to go because it was not Carrow.

That felt almost embarrassingly simple.

"Yes," I said.

Lyssa nodded.

"Then go."

Everyone was being unhelpful in the same direction.

I went upstairs.

The money sat where it had sat.

One copper.

One bit.

The holdfast sat wrapped above my clothes.

I looked at it.

"You are not invited."

It did nothing.

Good tool.

I took the bit.

Left the copper.

For a moment the empty space beside the copper bothered me more than the amount itself.

One coin looked lonely.

That was stupid.

Coins did not become safer in groups.

I went downstairs and put the bit in Sevren's palm.

"If I die on the road, I want this refunded."

"To who?"

"Excellent point."

He bit the bit.

"Why does everyone bite money?"

"Because you keep giving us suspicious money."

"That came from theatre."

"More suspicious."

Cala said, "Tomorrow."

Sevren nodded.

"East freight yard. First bell minus a little."

"That is not a time."

"You know what it means."

I did.

He left.

The seat became real because I had paid for it.

This produced immediate administrative consequences.

Water bottle.

Folded seat cloth from the Three Wells run.

Spare shirt.

One extra sock for the right foot.

A narrow strip of clean rag for the left residual limb if sweat or friction became annoying.

Small soap scrap.

Bread for the first morning.

Brown shoulder bag.

Charcoal.

Narrow scrap.

I considered the clean household sheets.

No.

Three remained three.

I considered the holdfast again.

Still no.

I did not know its safe load.

I did not know its surface limits.

I did not know its remaining life.

Taking it on a road trip because roads sometimes had problems would be exactly the kind of thinking that turned a speculative purchase into a personality disorder.

It stayed home.

Lyssa watched me pack.

"Too much?" I asked.

"No."

"You answered too quickly."

"You have one shirt."

"Spare shirt."

"Two shirts. Very dangerous."

"I knew it."

She took the shoulder bag from me and checked the strap seam.

Not the stay bead.

The seam.

"Fine."

"Professional opinion?"

"Bag opinion."

"Important boundary."

She looked at me.

The Ch310 argument lived there for half a second.

Not pain.

Not resolution.

Just history.

Then she handed the bag back.

"Bring it back."

"The bag?"

"You."

"Less replaceable?"

"More annoying."

Fair.

That evening I ate more than usual.

Road travel punished people who treated food like decoration.

I checked both crutch tips.

Sound.

Checked hand grips.

Sound.

Right shoe.

Sound.

Residual limb skin.

Fine.

No heroic preparation.

No speech.

I slept.

Before first bell, Carrow was gray and cold enough to make every doorway look judgmental.

Cala handed me bread.

Lyssa was awake but not pretending to enjoy it.

Tavi was not awake.

Civilization had limits.

I left.

The east freight yard had already begun making noise.

Wheels.

Harness buckles.

People swearing softly because louder swearing wasted breath.

Sevren stood beside a longer cart than Ressa's Three Wells run.

Four wheels.

Canvas-covered freight bed.

Rear passenger bench bolted across the back under a shallow canvas lip.

Two draft animals.

Not horses.

Broad-backed dun road striders with thick split hooves and short dark ears.

Common freight animals.

First life had taught me they were patient until they were not.

One looked at me.

I looked at it.

We agreed not to develop a relationship.

Daro Venn was narrow, bald, and built like someone had stretched a normal man upward without adding more material.

He pointed at my crutches.

"Under rear bench. Leather loop. Tips inward."

Good.

"Seat cloth?"

I held it up.

"Use it. Rear plank is honest."

"Meaning?"

"Hard."

I liked him.

The other two riders were already aboard.

A woman with a basket covered in netting.

A young man holding a wrapped bundle across both knees.

No introductions.

Fine.

I boarded from the rear step.

Right foot up.

One hand on frame.

Crutches passed to Sevren only after I was stable.

He slid them beneath the bench and secured the leather loop.

I put the folded cloth beneath the back of my left thigh where the plank edge would otherwise become a problem after an hour.

Bag against my right side.

Water tied to the frame.

Daro checked everything.

Not me.

Everything.

Then he climbed forward.

The cart moved.

Carrow rolled backward around us.

I had done this before.

That changed it.

The gate did not feel like an event.

The witness bead flashed green beside Daro's board mark.

The road opened.

Fields.

Ditches.

Morning fog sitting low over irrigation channels.

The woman with the netted basket got off at Three Wells.

The young man stayed.

Sevren completed one relay handoff, received a narrow brass-edged token, and returned before I finished my second piece of bread.

"Still coming?" he asked.

"I paid."

"Could run away."

"On crutches?"

"Slow dramatic escape."

"Daro would catch me."

From the front Daro said, "Wouldn't."

"See?"

"He'd leave you."

"Different threat."

We rolled east.

Past Three Wells the road narrowed.

Not dangerously.

Just enough that passing carts negotiated with wheels instead of law.

The irrigation stones became less frequent.

Low field walls appeared.

Then orchards.

Small silver tags hung from some branches.

Wind tags.

Pest tags.

Ripening marks.

Could have been several things.

I recognized families, not exact use.

Did not ask.

The young man with the bundle finally said, "First Bell Ford?"

I looked at him.

"Yes."

"Bridge sings."

"Most bridges complain."

"This one sings."

Sevren said, "Don't tell him."

The young man frowned.

"Why?"

"He likes finding things."

"I do not."

Sevren looked at me.

"You absolutely do."

I considered defending myself.

Did not.

The road climbed.

By midday my right thigh was working harder than it had in Carrow.

Not pain.

Cart vibration.

Bracing.

I changed position when Daro stopped to water the striders.

Got down.

Walked twenty careful yards.

Stretched the right hip.

Checked the left side.

Warm.

Fine.

No skin issue.

Back aboard.

Road again.

At dusk we reached a relay house smaller than Three Wells.

No town around it.

Just stable, kitchen, long sleeping room, and a stone post carrying a pale amber road light.

The light brightened as the sky darkened.

Ordinary.

Useful.

Nobody worshiped it.

Daro said, "Floor tonight."

I looked at the long room.

Twelve bed platforms.

All occupied.

Floor indeed.

Sevren found two folded reed mats from a stack.

"Luxury."

"You promised."

"I promised floor."

"And you have delivered."

Dinner was bean stew, hard bread, and something pickled that fought back.

Included.

I confirmed.

Sevren looked offended.

"You asked at Three Wells too."

"That is why I still have one copper."

He stopped.

"One?"

"One."

"What did you buy?"

"Bad tool."

"Of course."

"It may become a good tool later."

"That sounds worse."

I told him enough about the holdfast to be annoying without pretending I knew more than I did.

Fixed interval.

No manual release.

Three warning blinks.

Two copper.

Sevren stared at me over his bowl.

"You spent two copper on a thing everyone stopped wanting because it lets go by itself."

"Yes."

"And then spent your last bit to sit on Daro's hard cart."

"When you say it together, it sounds irresponsible."

"It was irresponsible separately too."

I ate stew.

"Good road, though."

Sevren looked toward the dark window.

"Yeah."

That was enough.

The sleeping room filled with breathing, snoring, boots being removed, and one man insisting his blanket had been stolen before discovering it beneath him.

I lay on the reed mat with my bag under my head.

Crutches against the wall within reach.

Left knee clear.

Right leg stretched.

Body tired.

Not angry.

Outside, the road light held steady.

Tomorrow we would reach Bell Ford.

No prophecy waited there.

No client.

No guild assignment.

No guaranteed monster.

Just a bridge, a road town, Sevren's work, and a seat I had chosen to buy.

For the first time since waking in this life, I had spent every bit I owned on going somewhere I did not need to go.

That should have frightened me more than it did.

Instead I listened to the relay house settle around us and thought about the young man's warning.

The bridge sings.

Fine.

Tomorrow I would hear whether it had anything worth saying.

# CHAPTER 317

## THE SINGING BRIDGE

The bridge did not sing until somebody lied to it.

That was not what happened.

It was what Sevren told me at breakfast.

I had learned from the tea stall that my lie detection was unreliable.

So I considered it carefully.

"What kind of lie?"

Sevren tore hard bread in half.

"Any."

"That seems technically expensive."

"Bridge knows."

"Does it know omission?"

"Yes."

"Sarcasm?"

"Especially."

"What if I sincerely believe something false?"

He chewed.

"Throws you in river."

I looked at Daro.

Daro drank tea.

"Don't encourage him."

"Which one?"

"Either."

Good morning.

The relay house fed us porridge before sunrise.

Included.

Still included.

I had no bits left to discover hidden breakfast law.

One copper remained in Carrow.

The absence of money in my pocket was oddly clean.

Not comfortable.

Clean.

Nothing to decide about buying because there was nothing to spend.

This was probably not a sustainable financial philosophy.

We loaded before the road light faded.

My body had tolerated the floor better than expected.

Back stiff for the first few steps.

Right hip tight.

Residual limb fine.

Hands fine.

Skin fine.

I walked the stiffness out while Sevren helped Daro check lashings.

Not my freight.

I stayed out of the work lane.

The young man with the wrapped bundle was named Corren because Daro finally asked whether Corren intended to carry the thing all the way to Bell Ford without ever setting it down.

Corren said yes.

Daro said, "Then stop leaning it on my wheel."

Names arrived through irritation.

Reliable system.

Corren's bundle remained his business.

We rolled east.

The country changed slowly.

Orchards thinned.

The road rose onto darker stone.

Small streams cut under it through square culverts.

Several culverts carried pale blue line marks on their upstream faces.

Flood gauges.

Pressure marks.

Water wards.

Some combination.

I knew enough to know I did not need to know.

At midmorning we heard the bridge.

Not singing.

Humming.

Low.

Far ahead.

A note held beneath the sound of wheels.

I sat straighter.

Sevren smiled without looking at me.

"Lie detector."

"I hate you."

The note did not change.

"See? True."

Corren laughed.

Daro said, "It is wind."

"Only wind?" I asked.

"Wind and bridge."

That was better.

Bell Ford appeared around a bend.

Town first.

Bridge second.

The town occupied both banks of a broad shallow river, though most buildings sat on the west side where the road widened into a market strip.

Stone foundations.

Wood upper floors.

Blue-gray roofs.

A squat relay tower near the river.

Two mills downstream.

The bridge was older than the road around it.

I could tell from the stone.

Three broad arches.

Dark piers.

Newer parapet repairs in lighter blocks.

Along each side ran a row of narrow bronze tongues mounted beneath the handrail.

Wind caught them.

Not randomly.

The bridge hummed in layers.

Low from the stone cavities.

Higher from bronze.

A third faint tone came and went beneath cart wheels.

I knew the family.

Resonance bridges.

Not rare.

Not magical instruments for entertainment, though people inevitably used them that way.

Load feedback.

Structural warning.

Sometimes flood warning.

Sometimes ice.

Sometimes all three badly combined by committees.

The exact Bell Ford system was local.

Daro slowed before the bridge.

A woman in a blue-gray coat stood beside a waist-high post at the entrance.

Not guard armor.

Road worker.

She held up one hand.

Daro stopped.

"Freight?"

He gave weight class and destination.

She looked at the cart.

Then touched two fingers to the post.

A pale line climbed it.

The bridge's low note changed.

Not louder.

Clearer.

She listened.

So did Daro.

So did everyone else who knew enough to stop talking.

I shut up.

The note settled.

The worker nodded.

"Center track. Slow."

Daro clicked to the striders.

We moved.

The first wheel touched bridge stone.

The hum changed under us.

I felt it through the bench before I understood it with my ears.

A vibration in the wood.

Then the bronze tongues answered.

Not melody.

Information shaped enough like music that people had named it singing.

Corren leaned toward me.

"If it screams, jump."

Daro said, "If it screams, stay on the cart."

Corren sat back.

"He ruins everything."

I watched the road worker walk beside us for the first arch.

Her eyes stayed on the cart wheels and the bronze line, not on our faces.

The second arch gave a slightly rougher note.

She glanced down at the parapet base.

No alarm.

Third arch smoother.

Then we were across.

The worker stopped walking.

Daro lifted two fingers.

She returned the gesture.

Done.

No toll at that station.

No lie test.

No river throwing.

I looked at Sevren.

"You owe me breakfast."

"Included."

"Tomorrow's breakfast."

"Maybe included."

Bell Ford east side was smaller.

Stable yard.

Relay house.

A row of warehouses.

Two inns.

One smithy whose sign was a hammer with three badly painted sparks.

Daro pulled into the relay yard.

Sevren became busy.

Not theatrically.

Work busy.

He took his brass-edged token inside.

Came out with a clerk.

They checked three seals on the freight canvas.

Daro opened one side.

The cargo turned out to be bundled reed panels, sacks of dried beans, and six long wooden cases.

I did not ask what was in the cases.

Growth.

Corren finally carried his wrapped bundle into the relay office.

It was apparently a clock face.

I learned this because the wrapping slipped and revealed painted numbers.

He swore.

The clerk swore.

The clock face survived.

No Greg required.

Sevren returned after nearly an hour.

"Done."

"Your face says not done."

"Return token after noon."

"So not done."

"Courier done. Waiting not done."

This distinction mattered to him.

We had the rest of the morning.

I had no money.

This made Bell Ford inexpensive.

We walked.

Sevren knew the town enough to avoid pretending he knew it deeply.

"Bread there. Expensive."

"Useful."

"Cheap stew there. Bad."

"More useful."

"Bridge office there. Don't bother them."

"You know me too well."

"Yes."

We crossed a smaller footbridge downstream.

No resonance system.

Just wood and iron braces.

The main bridge sang above us whenever a heavy cart crossed.

Different loads made different notes.

I began noticing patterns.

Empty wagon.

High and quick.

Loaded grain cart.

Lower, longer.

A four-animal timber wagon produced a deep vibration that made people near the river glance up.

Not fear.

Attention.

The blue-gray road worker at the west entrance raised a flag.

The timber wagon stopped before entering.

Another worker crossed from the east.

They spoke.

One went beneath the near parapet through a narrow maintenance stair.

I watched from the footbridge.

Sevren watched me watching.

"No bridge office."

"I am standing."

"Keep it that way."

The worker returned.

Flag lowered.

Timber wagon crossed slowly.

Bridge note stayed deep but clean.

That was all.

No failure.

No mystery.

Inspection happened because the system gave them a reason to inspect.

Then traffic continued.

I liked that more than I wanted to admit.

First life had contained too many bridges that were either perfectly ignored or dramatically collapsing.

Most real bridges spent their lives between those states.

Maintained.

Listened to.

Argued about.

Paid for badly.

We ate at the relay kitchen because my seat included one Bell Ford midday meal.

I confirmed.

Sevren groaned.

Stew.

Not bad.

Not memorable.

Ideal travel food.

A courier at the next table recognized Sevren.

"East again?"

"For now."

"Maro still taking south?"

"Last I heard."

"He owes me a strap."

"Tell Maro."

"I am telling you."

"That is why you don't have your strap."

Independent lives continued around me.

I knew none of these people.

Good.

The courier looked at my crutches once.

Then at Sevren.

"Passenger?"

"Passenger."

Done.

No rank.

No story.

No explanation.

After noon Sevren collected a dark blue return token shaped like a flattened ring.

Different from the Three Wells proof token.

I noticed.

Did not ask.

He caught me noticing.

"You are suffering."

"Deeply."

"Ask."

"No."

"Why?"

"Personal growth."

He laughed.

Daro's return freight would not leave until morning.

We had one night in Bell Ford.

The passenger bunk was included if space existed.

Space existed.

Barely.

Six bunks in a narrow upstairs room above the stable office.

I got the lower one because the keeper looked at my crutches, looked at the ladder to the upper bunks, and possessed common sense.

No speech.

No pity.

"Lower."

"Thank you."

Done.

I left my bag and walked alone before dark.

Not far.

The bridge drew me back.

Of course it did.

I stood on the west bank below the first arch.

Water moved over pale stones.

The bridge hummed above.

A child on the bank threw pebbles into the river.

His father repaired a fishing net.

Two women washed long strips of cloth downstream where the current slowed.

The resonance note shifted as carts crossed.

No one stopped to admire it.

They lived here.

That mattered.

I had once crossed a singing bridge during a siege while three enemy mages tried to break its center span.

Different bridge.

Different country.

The resonance wards screamed so loudly blood came from one soldier's ears.

We held the crossing.

Later I learned the bridge had never been designed for war.

It had been built to warn shepherds when spring flood undermined the western pier.

War had borrowed the system and called itself important.

I looked at Bell Ford's bridge.

No siege.

No screaming.

A timber wagon made workers check a pier.

A grain cart crossed.

A woman pushed a handcart of cabbages.

The bridge sang for all of them.

That felt closer to what the old builders probably intended.

Sevren found me there near sunset.

"Knew it."

"What?"

"Bridge."

"I am not investigating."

"You're under it."

"Admiring."

"Worse."

He sat on a flat stone.

I remained standing until my hands told me sitting was smarter.

Then I lowered myself beside him, right leg doing the work, left knee clear.

Crutches laid beside me.

The river smelled cold.

"Worth one bit?" Sevren asked.

I thought about it.

The hard cart.

The relay floor.

Bell Ford stew.

The humming bridge.

One copper waiting at home.

"Yes."

"Good."

"Do not become smug."

"Too late."

We sat until the light went blue.

Then the bridge lamps woke.

Small amber points along the parapet.

Not all at once.

One.

Then the next.

Then the next.

A simple chained lighting run.

Common.

Pretty anyway.

The bronze tongues caught evening wind.

For a moment the bridge actually did sound like music.

Not because anyone lied.

Not because magic demanded attention.

Because wind crossed metal over moving water and people had been clever enough to build a structure that could listen to itself.

Sevren stood.

"Come on."

I reached for my crutches.

"Tomorrow home?"

"Tomorrow west. Three Wells night if Daro pushes. Carrow day after."

"Good."

"You miss Cala?"

"I miss a bed."

"Lyssa?"

"I miss a bed."

"Tavi?"

"I miss silence."

Sevren nodded.

"Home."

That was annoyingly accurate.

We climbed back toward the relay yard.

Behind us, another cart entered the bridge.

The note deepened.

Held.

Then settled clean.

Nobody cheered.

Nobody needed to.

The bridge was doing its job.

# CHAPTER 318

## THE EMPTY PEG

The problem with the return trip was that somebody else had not returned.

Not us.

We were exactly where we were supposed to be.

This was apparently insufficient.

Daro had the cart loaded before sunrise.

Return freight was less impressive than outbound freight.

Three sacks of something that smelled medicinal.

Two narrow crates.

A bundle of bent metal hoops tied together with rope.

One covered basket that occasionally clicked.

I did not ask about the clicking basket.

This required effort.

Sevren noticed.

"Sick?"

"Growing."

"Looks painful."

"Deeply."

Bell Ford's bridge hummed under the first cart before dawn.

We crossed west after the road worker cleared Daro.

The bridge note was higher with the lighter load.

I noticed.

Did not investigate.

By full morning Bell Ford was behind us.

The road rolled through orchards and low stone fields.

My body had accumulated the trip.

Not damage.

Accumulation.

Right thigh tighter.

Back aware of the bench.

Palms normal because cart sitting was not crutch walking.

Residual limb warm under the trouser leg but comfortable.

The folded seat cloth did its job.

I changed position before discomfort became a debate.

Old Greg had been very good at ignoring his body until it became strategically inconvenient.

Current Greg had fewer spare parts and better habits.

Progress.

Daro stopped twice before midday.

Water for the striders.

Wheel check.

Harness check.

People check only in the broad sense that none of us had fallen off.

At the second stop, Sevren walked fifty yards down the road and back.

I joined him.

Not because he needed company.

Because my right hip wanted movement.

"Bell Ford worth it?" he asked.

"You already asked."

"Checking if bridge got worse overnight."

"Bridge remains acceptable."

"High praise."

"Town stew also acceptable."

"Careful. You'll become a travel writer."

"I have standards."

We turned back.

The clicking basket clicked.

I looked at it.

Sevren said, "Not your basket."

"I was looking."

"Look quietly."

Daro climbed back onto the front board.

"Bees."

I stopped.

"What?"

"Basket."

"Those are very large clicks."

"Not bees inside. Bee clasps."

That explained almost nothing.
