I accepted it.

Growth continued.

We reached the unnamed relay house from the outbound trip near midday.

No overnight this time.

Daro changed a wheel pin.

Sevren exchanged the dark blue Bell Ford token for a pale wooden marker.

I ate included bread and cheese.

Confirmed.

Nobody was surprised anymore.

Then we kept west.

Three Wells appeared in late afternoon.

The three stone wells caught the lowering sun.

The relay house roof looked bluer than I remembered.

Daro pulled into the yard.

"Night here."

Expected.

My body approved.

Sevren did not answer.

He was looking at the relay wall.

A row of wooden pegs ran beneath a shallow roof.

Most held something.

Tokens.

Small leather message sleeves.

Colored cords.

One peg near the center was empty.

Sevren walked to it.

Touched the little number plate below.

Then looked toward the east road.

"What?" I asked.

He did not answer immediately.

A relay clerk came out carrying a slate.

Older woman.

Short hair.

Ink stain on one cuff.

She looked at the empty peg.

"Not in."

Sevren said, "I see."

"Expected fourth bell."

I looked at the sky.

Fourth bell had passed.

Not by much.

"Who?" Sevren asked.

"Pell road. Naro run."

Names.

Not my names.

The clerk checked her slate.

"No delay mark from east post."

Sevren rubbed his jaw.

"How long?"

"Your cutoff is sixth."

"I know."

"Then why ask?"

"Hoping rule changed."

"It did not."

The clerk went back inside.

I looked at Sevren.

"Your transfer?"

"Maybe."

"Maybe?"

"If Naro has west packet, I take it to Carrow. If not, empty peg stays empty."

"And if he arrives after sixth?"

"Next west runner."

"You wait until sixth."

"Yes."

"Then leave."

"Yes."

Simple.

Except Sevren kept looking at the east road.

I recognized that too.

Rules could be simple while waiting remained annoying.

We had nearly two bells.

Daro took the striders to the stable side.

I put my bag in the relay sleeping room.

Lower platform available.

Good.

Then I came back out.

Sevren sat on a low wall facing east.

I sat beside him.

"You do this often?"

"Wait?"

"For missing runners."

"Enough."

"Usually late?"

"Sometimes."

"Usually problem?"

"Usually mud. Wheel. Animal. Clerk who thinks five minutes is not late because clocks are oppressive."

"Reasonable clerk."

"No."

We waited.

Carts entered.

People drew water.

A stable boy chased one road strider around a post while the animal moved exactly fast enough to remain annoying without becoming difficult.

A woman sold boiled roots from a pot.

I had no money.

The roots smelled excellent.

This was educational.

Sevren had money.

He bought two.

Handed me one.

I looked at it.

"Loan?"

"Friendship."

"More dangerous."

"Eat."

I ate.

Hot.

Pepper.

Good.

"I owe you?"

"No."

"Recorded."

"You are unbearable."

"Also recorded."

Fifth bell rang.

No rider.

The relay clerk came out once.

Looked east.

Looked at Sevren.

Said nothing.

Went back inside.

I asked, "If you leave at sixth and he arrives one minute later?"

"He misses me."

"You don't get blamed?"

"Not if I mark out correctly."

"And if you wait?"

"Daro leaves tomorrow after first bell. Carrow board expects my west token. If I delay without authorization, that becomes my delay."

There it was.

Not courage.

Not loyalty.

Work boundaries.

The late rider's problem did not automatically become Sevren's problem simply because Sevren could imagine helping.

I had opinions about this because I had spent one life collecting other people's problems until they became a career.

"You want to wait," I said.

"I am waiting."

"Past sixth."

Sevren looked at me.

"Maybe."

Interesting.

"Why?"

"Naro's usually clean."

"Meaning?"

"If he's late without mark, something unusual happened."

"That argues for concern."

"Yes."

"Not necessarily for you waiting."

He gave me a long look.

"You become wise on one bit road trip?"

"I spent the bit. Wisdom was included."

He snorted.

We waited.

A cart came from the east.

Not Naro.

Two women.

Empty produce racks.

Sevren watched them pass.

Fifth bell and half.

The clerk came out with the slate.

"Still nothing."

"I see."

"If no mark by sixth, you mark empty and go."

"I know."

"Saying it for your friend."

She looked at me.

I lifted both hands as much as crutches allowed.

"Passenger."

"Good."

She went inside.

Sevren said, "She likes rules."

"I like her."

"Of course."

Near sixth bell, a road boy came running from the east entrance.

Not a rider.

Maybe twelve.

He carried a narrow red strip tied around one wrist.

The relay clerk saw him and moved fast.

Not panic.

Purpose.

The boy handed her a small folded board.

She read it.

Then looked at Sevren.

"Axle at East Cut. Naro safe. Cart not. Packet held there. Replacement goes morning."

Sevren exhaled.

One breath.

That was all.

"No west packet tonight?"

"No. Mark empty."

He stood.

Went to the peg.

Took a little chalk stick from the box beneath it.

Drew one diagonal line across the number plate.

Then wrote something on the clerk's slate.

Signed.

Done.

No rescue expedition.

No sprint east.

No demand to borrow Daro's cart.

No Greg fieldcraft.

No mystery.

Naro was safe.

Axle broken.

Replacement morning.

System adjusted.

Sevren sat again.

"You wanted to wait," I said.

"Yeah."

"Would you have?"

He thought.

"Five minutes."

"Unauthorized?"

"Probably."

"Rebel."

"Dangerous life."

Sixth bell rang.

The empty peg stayed empty.

That was the title of the evening, apparently.

We ate relay dinner.

Included.

Beans again.

Carrow and the east road were engaged in a conspiracy involving beans.

Daro joined us.

Sevren told him the transfer would not come.

Daro nodded.

"Then lighter morning."

That was his entire emotional response.

I liked road people.

The sleeping room filled slowly.

A pair of traders argued over snoring rights.

Someone had hung damp socks too close to the stove.

The relay clerk came through once and removed them without discussion.

Authority.

I lay on the lower platform.

Right thigh tired.

Back tired.

Residual limb fine.

Hands fine.

Tomorrow Carrow if wheels remained round.

I thought about the empty peg.

First life would have made me curious about East Cut.

What broke the axle?

Why no delay mark?

Was the post damaged?

Bandits?

Creature?

Weather?

Sabotage?

That brain had survived by assuming every irregularity might become dangerous.

Useful habit.

Expensive habit.

Here, a road boy had arrived with a red strip and a board.

Naro safe.

Cart not.

Replacement morning.

Enough.

I did not need the rest.

Sevren's bunk was above mine.

"You awake?" he asked.

"No."

"Good."

Silence.

Then:

"Glad Naro's fine."

"Yeah."

More silence.

I smiled in the dark.

The road system had lost one axle and one evening transfer.

It had not become a quest.

Tomorrow we would go home.

# CHAPTER 319

## THE MISSED COIN

I came home poorer than I left and discovered I had missed a chance to become less poor.

This felt organized.

Carrow appeared near midday.

Not dramatically.

No skyline reveal.

No music.

One moment fields and low walls.

Then more carts.

Then workshops.

Then the east gate.

The gate witness bead flashed beside Daro's board mark.

The road striders walked through without caring that I had returned to the center of my current world.

Sevren looked at me.

"Home."

"Apparently."

"Disappointed?"

I considered.

"No."

True.

Bell Ford had been worth the bit.

Three Wells had been familiar enough to feel like a waypoint now.

The relay floor had been terrible.

The empty peg had stayed somebody else's work problem.

The bridge had sung.

I was ready for a bed that belonged to a room I knew.

Daro pulled into the east freight yard.

We unloaded ourselves before anyone touched freight.

Crutches out from the leather loop.

Both tips sound.

Right foot down.

Hand on frame.

Left knee clear.

I stood.

My body objected to standing after the last long cart stretch.

Right hip tight.

Back stiff.

Nothing sharp.

Nothing wrong.

I waited three breaths before moving.

Daro noticed.

Said nothing.

Good man.

Sevren handed me my water bottle.

"Next time?"

There it was.

Not a seat.

Not a schedule.

A question.

"Ask if one exists."

"Still?"

"Still."

He nodded.

No promise.

No handshake.

No dramatic road-brother pact.

Then he pointed at my hair.

"Worse."

"Five days of road improved it."

"That is what worries me."

I left him in the freight yard.

Not because the friendship ended.

Because he had work.

I had home.

The walk from the yard took longer than usual.

Cart body and walking body were different governments.

My right thigh had spent days bracing against vibration.

Now it wanted to walk normally and had forgotten the paperwork.

I shortened my stride.

Let the crutches carry rhythm rather than speed.

No rush.

The brown shoulder bag felt almost weightless after road travel.

No water sack.

No freight.

No holdfast.

One spare shirt now smelled like relay house.

The city smelled familiar.

Bread.

Drain water.

Hot metal.

Somebody frying onions.

Horse.

Soap.

Too many people.

Home.

I reached the building.

The front door opened cleanly.

Still repaired.

The rain shelf remained a shelf.

The water line worked.

The household sack from Ch314 was back on its hook where ordinary objects went after refusing to become plot.

Cala was at the table sorting beans.

Tavi was under the table.

Some systems did not change.

Lyssa was out.

Cala looked up.

"Long."

"Five days."

"Long."

"You knew the trip."

"Still long."

Tavi crawled out.

He stared at me.

Then at my bag.

"Horse?"

"No horse."

"Blue horse."

"Still no."

He looked betrayed.

I had apparently failed to bring back an animal from a trip that had never involved one.

Cala pushed a cup of water toward me.

I drank.

House water.

No relay inclusion question required.

Luxury.

"Lyssa?"

"Work."

Good.

"Anything happen?"

Cala shrugged.

"Normal."

This was the answer travelers claimed to want and never believed.

I believed it.

Then she pointed at the shelf near the door.

"Paper."

A folded note sat beneath the little clay weight Cala used to keep drafts from stealing messages.

My name was on the outside.

GREG.

Not Hessa's hand.

Not Sevren's.

Not theatre.

I recognized the Vale office hand from Antonius's earlier note.

Ah.

I picked it up.

The date mark was two days old.

That was not ideal.

I opened it.

The note was short.

VALE OFFICE.

GROUND FLOOR.

TWO HOURS.

SEATED.

CASH, ONE COPPER.

COMPARE SIXTEEN SHIPPING TALLIES TO SEALED RECEIPT COPIES.

MARK MATCH / MISMATCH / UNREADABLE ONLY.

NO CAUSE ASSIGNMENT.

MATERIALS READY.

TODAY, THIRD TO FIFTH BELL.

REPLY YES / NO BEFORE SECOND BELL.

A. VALE

I read it twice.

Then looked at the date again.

Two days ago.

One copper.

Cash.

Not debt credit.

Exactly the kind of work I had told Antonius to offer if it genuinely appeared.

Exactly the kind of work I had not been in Carrow to accept.

I laughed once.

Cala looked at me.

"Bad?"

"No."

That was true.

Annoying.

Not bad.

"Work?"

"Was."

She nodded.

No sympathy performance.

No accusation.

I folded the note again.

My private money state was now brutally simple.

One copper upstairs.

No bits.

One missed chance at another copper.

Two copper still sitting in the shape of a fixed holdfast I had not used.

And I had spent the last bit on Bell Ford.

The math could tell a moral story if I let it.

Bad purchase.

Bad trip.

Missed work.

Poor Greg punished for pleasure.

That story was available.

It was also stupid.

The holdfast might still be a bad purchase.

I did not know.

Bell Ford had been worth the bit.

I knew that.

The missed job had existed whether the trip was wise or not.

I had told Antonius I could refuse cash work.

Being absent was an especially efficient refusal.

Still hurt a little.

One copper was one copper.

I put the note beside my cup.

"When came?"

Cala said, "Boy. Morning. Two days."

"Did he wait?"

"No."

"Say anything?"

"Need answer before second. I said gone east."

"Then they knew."

"Yes."

Good.

No mystery about why I failed to answer.

No office believing I had ignored them.

No need to rush across Carrow and explain myself unless I wanted to confirm future terms still stood.

I wanted to.

Not today.

My body wanted bath water, food, and horizontal architecture.

I went upstairs first.

The room looked smaller than Bell Ford's bunk room and better than every relay floor.

The holdfast remained on the high shelf.

Wrapped.

Tag visible.

DO NOT TURN RING.

I looked at it.

"You cost me two copper."

It did not apologize.

"And now I have one."

Still nothing.

"Good talk."

I took out the narrow scrap from my bag.

Magic notes intact.

Road dust on the fold.

No need to write Bell Ford bridge mechanics.

No need to write the empty peg.

No need to write that one copper had passed me while I was gone.

The note itself existed.

I put the Vale cash-work note under the earlier debt-credit receipt.

Not as debt.

Not as obligation.

Just record.

Then I changed shirts.

The road shirt went into the wash pile.

I checked the left residual limb properly.

Warm.

A little more red where the trouser fabric had rubbed near the upper edge during the last cart day.

Not broken skin.

Not painful.

No swelling.

Wash.

Dry.

Rest.

I had learned enough not to negotiate with friction after five days of road.

Right foot fine.

Right shoe fine.

Hands fine.

Crutches fine.

I went downstairs.

Cala had put food out.

Beans.

Of course.

I stared.

"No."

"What?"

"Road fed me beans."

"Beans good."

"That is not the issue."

Tavi said, "Beans."

The household united against me.

I ate.

Lyssa came home near evening.

She opened the door.

Saw me.

Stopped.

Then continued inside.

"Back."

"Apparently."

She set down her work bag.

"Worth it?"

Same question as after Three Wells.

This time I had more answer.

"Yes."

She nodded.

Then noticed the Vale note beside the clay weight.

"Work?"

"Missed."

"Road?"

"Road."

"Bad?"

I thought.

"Annoying."

"Same?"

"Not same."

She waited.

I explained.

Two hours.

One copper cash.

Offer expired two days ago.

Cala had told messenger I was east.

No blame known.

Lyssa sat.

"You wish stayed?"

That was the question.

Not whether I wanted the copper.

I absolutely wanted the copper.

"No."

The answer came quickly enough to surprise me.

Lyssa looked at me.

"Then okay."

"I can want both."

"Yes."

"I want both."

"Yes."

This was infuriatingly simple.

I had spent years in my first life pretending choices stopped being choices if I was strong enough.

Take the contract and make the festival.

Save the town and reach the dinner.

Earn the money and keep the friendship.

Fight all night and travel all day.

S-class power made many physical limits negotiable.

It did not create more hours.

I had forgotten that often.

Current Greg had fewer ways to lie to himself.

One bit bought Bell Ford.

Five days away meant five days not here.

A one-copper job had passed through one of those days.

Both facts could be true without one proving the other wrong.

Lyssa reached across the table and touched the edge of my road-worn bag.

"Dirty."

"That is road authenticity."

"Dirty."

"Professional opinion?"

"Bag opinion."

Callback law remained strong.

I smiled.

"Tomorrow I will clean it."

"Tomorrow you go Vale?"

"Probably."

"Ask?"

"Tell them I was gone. Confirm future cash offers still welcome."

"Good."

"Not ask for replacement job."

She nodded.

That distinction mattered.

I was not owed a new coin because I had missed one.

The next morning my right hip had loosened.

Back normal enough.

Residual-limb redness faded after sleep.

No injury.

I walked to the Vale office after breakfast.

Not early enough to look desperate.

Not late enough to make the trip pointless.

Antonius was at the ground-floor desk with three ledgers open.

He looked up.

"East."

Not a question.

"Cala told your messenger."

"She did."

"I saw the note."

"Work is done."

"I assumed."

"Mera clerk took it."

Mera clerk could be a person named Mera or a clerk from somewhere called Mera or a category I did not understand.

I did not care enough to ask.

Growth.

"I came to confirm something," I said.

Antonius waited.

"If another suitable cash task genuinely appears, I still want the terms sent. Missing this one because I was away does not change that."

"Fine."

"No standing availability."

"I know."

"No assumption I accept."

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"I know."

Fair.

I nodded.

He looked back at the ledger.

Conversation over.

No replacement job appeared from beneath the desk.

No pity copper.

No consolation task.

No lecture about reliability.

I left with exactly as much money as I had entered.

One copper at home.

Zero bits.

The walk back felt lighter anyway.

Not because the money problem had improved.

It had not.

Because the terms still existed.

Cash work might appear.

I might be there.

I might not.

I could accept.

I could refuse.

And sometimes I could spend the last bit I had to hear a bridge sing, then come home and discover the world had not paused politely while I was gone.

That seemed expensive.

It also seemed like living.

# CHAPTER 320

## THE PORTRAIT

The woman drawing me had forgotten my left foot.

This was accurate.

I still objected.

"You're staring," she said.

"You've omitted something."

Her charcoal stopped.

She looked at the board on her knees.

Then at me.

Then down.

"Oh."

"Exactly."

"I thought the cloth hid it."

"The cloth hides the end of the trouser leg."

"Yes."

"There is normally a foot after that."

She looked at my crutches.

Then at the space below my left knee.

Then back at the board.

"Not in your case."

"Strong recovery."

The corner of her mouth moved.

I had not agreed to be drawn.

That part should be established.

I had gone out to clean my bag.

Not in the street.

I had enough dignity left to avoid publicly washing leather.

The brown shoulder bag had survived Bell Ford, relay floors, road dust, one damp bench, and Lyssa's professional diagnosis of dirty.

The household rag removed the worst of it.

Cala had produced a little tin of leather grease from somewhere in the cupboard.

I did not ask how old it was.

Old household tins were a separate branch of archaeology.

I worked a small amount into the leather upstairs, wiped off the excess, and discovered the bag now smelled strongly enough to make the room unpleasant.

So I hung it near the open window.

Then I remembered Tavi.

I moved it higher.

Then I remembered Tavi could climb.

I moved it to the outer sill where the strap could be looped twice around the shutter hook.

This created a bag that was clean, protected, and impossible to use until the smell stopped trying to occupy the building.

I went out without it.

That felt wrong.

The narrow scrap stayed home.

Charcoal stayed home.

One copper stayed upstairs.

No bits existed to feel lonely.

I carried only my crutches and the household errand Cala had given me.

Return a covered bowl to the woman two buildings down.

Not the bean bowl from before.

Different bowl.

Different woman.

Carrow had achieved enough density that pottery now required qualifiers.

The return took four minutes.

The woman took the bowl, said, "Good," and closed the door.

No soup.

No message.

No secret compartment.

A successful bowl transaction.

I should have gone home.

Instead I kept walking because five days on a cart had made walking around my own streets feel oddly luxurious.

My right hip had stopped complaining.

Back normal.

Left side normal.

The city was warm without being vicious.

Market traffic had thinned after the midday rush.

I reached the little paved triangle where three streets met near the old cooper's row.

There were two benches.

One occupied by an old man asleep with his hat over his face.

The other occupied by paper.

Not metaphorically.

Sheets had been spread across half the bench beneath flat stones.

Heads.

Hands.

Shoes.

A dog.

Three different noses.

A kettle.

A woman leaning over a counter.

Quick charcoal studies, most no larger than my hand.

A young woman sat on a low folding stool in front of them with a board across her knees.

Dark hair tied badly at the back.

Gray shirt.

Green skirt.

Black charcoal on three fingers and one cheek.

She was drawing a boy holding a basket.

The boy was not holding still.

This appeared to be part of the exercise.

"Stop moving," she said.

"I'm not."

"You have moved six times."

"I breathed."

"You moved before breathing."

The boy considered this accusation.

Then moved again.

I stopped because one of the pages showed a crutch.

Not mine.

Different shape.

Underarm frame rather than forearm support.

The drawing was only the lower half of a person crossing the square.

One foot.

Two crutch tips.

A long coat caught behind one brace.

The weight distribution was good.

Not beautiful.

True.

The artist glanced at me.

Then at my crutches.

Then back at the boy.

I continued looking.

The boy said, "Done?"

"No."

"You said quick."

"Quick is relative."

"To what?"

"People who pay."

He frowned.

"You're not paying me."

"Exactly."

The old man on the other bench laughed without removing the hat from his face.

The boy finally escaped three minutes later.

He took his basket and left without seeing the drawing.

The artist turned the board around.

It showed his shoulder, basket, and the expression of a person being personally oppressed by standing still.

Good.

She set it on the bench.

Then looked at me.

"Sit?"

"No."

"Excellent. Stand."

I laughed.

"No."

"Also useful."

She reached for another sheet.

"Did I agree?"

"No."

"Then don't hold still."

That was new.

"What?"

"If you hold still, I will draw you. If you leave, I will draw somebody else."

The old man said from under his hat, "She drew me sleeping."

"You were very cooperative," the woman said.

"I was unconscious."

"Best kind."

I should have left.

Instead I stood there long enough to become responsible for my own consequences.

Her charcoal moved.

Fast.

Not face first.

Crutch.

Right hand.

Shoulder.

Line of torso.

Right leg.

Left trouser leg.

I watched.

Then she forgot the foot.

Which returned us to the beginning.

After my complaint, she rubbed the lower line with her thumb.

Not erasing.

Softening.

Then she redrew the hanging trouser shape more clearly.

The left knee existed.

Below it, cloth folded and pinned back.

No invented foot.

No dramatic empty space either.

Just the body I had.

"Better?" she asked.

"Yes."

"You're difficult."

"You tried to give me a hidden foot."

"I did not give you one."

"You implied one."

"That is a very specific grievance."

"I have experience."

The old man laughed again.

The artist looked at my face now.

That was less comfortable.

People looked at faces constantly.

Being watched was ordinary.

Being measured by looking was different.

Hessa looked at movement.

Lyssa looked at fit.

Theatre people looked at whether I was about to miss an entrance, block a sightline, or fall into furniture.

This woman looked at shape.

I shifted my right foot.

She changed the line.

"Don't pose."

"I'm standing."

"You changed your shoulder."

"My shoulder became aware of itself."

"Tell it to stop."

"Unreliable employee."

Charcoal scratched.

A woman carrying onions slowed.

"Soldier?"

I looked at her.

"No."

She pointed with her chin toward the drawing.

"Looks like one."

The artist said, "It has no sword."

"Soldiers sit too."

"I'm not sitting."

The onion woman looked at me.

Then at the crutches.

Then apparently decided this conversation required more effort than onions deserved.

She left.

I looked at the artist.

"Do I look like a soldier?"

"No."

"That answer was fast."

"You look like a man standing in a square arguing about whether his missing foot was sufficiently represented."

"Good."

"Very martial."

She worked.

I had been a warrior.

That was the stupid part.

Not a soldier, exactly.

Different systems.

Different life.

Enough fighting that the distinction became academic to anyone being stabbed.

If she had drawn first-life me, there would have been armor.

Weapons.

More scars.

Both legs.

A face old enough to have learned when not to argue with charcoal artists.

Current me had a gray shirt, worn trousers, two crutches, one right shoe, and hair Sevren had recently judged worse.

The artist narrowed her eyes at the hair.

I became suspicious.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"That was a hair look."

"Your hair has direction."

"It has several."

"Yes."

"Road."

"Did the road attack it?"

"Repeatedly."

She smiled and kept drawing.

A little girl arrived with a woman who might have been her mother.

The girl went straight to the pages.

"Dog."

"Yes," the artist said.

"Whose?"

"Dog's."

The girl accepted this.

She picked up a page.

The artist immediately said, "Edges."

The girl held it by the edges.

Apparently not her first visit.

The mother looked at me.

Then at the drawing in progress.

"You're doing standing today?"

"I'm doing whoever stops."

"Bad business model."

"No business."

That explained the lack of price board.

The mother set a wrapped piece of bread beside the artist's stool.

"Eat."

"Later."

"Now."

"Later."

The mother looked at me.

I raised both hands as far as crutches allowed.

"Not my jurisdiction."

"Smart."

She left.

The artist stared at the bread.

Then at the charcoal.

Then put the charcoal down.

"Apparently break."

"Strong management."

"She doesn't manage me."

The old man said, "She brought the bread."

"Traitor."

She ate.

I sat on the free end of the bench because standing through somebody else's lunch was absurd.

The pages remained between us.

Up close, the drawings were less polished.

Some lines doubled.

Hands failed.

One face had been rubbed almost through the paper.

A shoe study had six attempts around it.

The good ones had survived selection bias.

That made me like them more.

"Learning?" I asked.

She swallowed.

"Always."

"Profession?"

"Sometimes signs. Sometimes shop cards. Sometimes copied borders. Portraits when people pay enough to discover they hate their own noses."

"Do they?"

"Usually."

"Why draw people free?"

"Hands."

I looked at the pages.

"Many heads."

"Heads are attached to hands if you wait."

Fair.

She took another bite.

"Also people move."

"Known problem."

"Paid portraits lie. Free people forget to."

I looked at the boy with the basket.

He had definitely forgotten.

"What's your name?"

"Senna."

"Greg."

"I know."

That got my attention.

She saw it.

"Not like that."

"Excellent clarification."

"Tea stall."

Of course.

"Bel?"

"Bel talks."

"Bel lies."

"Same mouth."

I considered the social consequences of losing a lying contest with a true story.

"How much did she tell you?"

"That a crutch man told the worst lie she'd heard all week and insisted it was true."

"I did not insist."

"Better."

"Did she mention the door?"

Senna looked at me.

"There was a door?"

I closed my eyes.

Good.

Networked reputation.

Accurate in the important ways.

"No."

"Now I want the door."

"You are not getting the door."

"Then I know exactly enough."

She finished the bread.

The old man sat up at last.

He had a white mustache and the face from one of the pages.

The drawing had improved him by making him look awake.

He pointed at my crutches.

"She'll make those crooked."

Senna said, "I drew yours straight."

"I don't have crutches."

"Exactly."

He considered.

Then put the hat back over his face.

Senna resumed.

This time I stayed seated.

She started a second drawing without asking.

I objected on principle.

"You already have one."

"Standing."

"I remain the same person."

"Debatable."

"Philosophy costs extra."

"No business."

The second drawing began with my hands.

Right hand resting over the crutch grip.

Left loose.

Then knees.

Right foot.

Crutch tips angled away.

My body when seated had a different geometry.

Obvious.

Still strange to see somebody else notice it without commentary.

She drew the residual limb only as the trouser shape showed it.

No emphasis.

No hiding.

Then face.

I looked away toward the street.

"Better," she said.

"Rude."

"Your face stops helping when you forget it exists."

"I was helping?"

"Badly."

A cart passed.

Two women argued over a bundle of reeds.

A man carrying three empty bird cages stopped to retie the top one.

Nobody cared that I was being drawn.

That helped.

Senna worked for maybe ten minutes.

Maybe fifteen.

I did not count.

When she turned the board, I recognized the crutches first.

Then the shoulders.

Then my right shoe.

Then me.

Not heroic.

Not damaged.

Not handsome enough to be suspicious.

Tired around the eyes.

Hair genuinely criminal.

One side of my mouth slightly higher because I had apparently been trying not to make a joke.

I stared.

"That is unfortunate."

"Your nose?"

"My hair."

"I told you."

"Sevren told me."

"Who?"

"Road friend."

"Then road friend has eyes."

I looked again.

There was something deeply offensive about being recognizable from the outside.

First-life portraits had been formal things.

Rank.

Armor.

Guild marks.

Weapons positioned to tell the viewer what kind of dangerous idiot had paid for the wall.

Later, after S-class, people had made images I never sat for.

Those were worse.

Broader shoulders.

Cleaner scars.

Jaw apparently forged from civic responsibility.

One had given me a sword I had never owned.

Another had removed a scar because the patron thought it made me look cruel.

Current Greg had no patron.

No rank worth painting.

No reason for a public image.

Senna had still caught the way I sat.

I pointed.

"Right shoulder is lower."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Because it is."

I checked myself.

Crutch habits.

Road stiffness gone, but not every asymmetry belonged to injury.

"Do I always?"

"I've known you twenty minutes."

Excellent.

No diagnosis.

No revelation.

Just a line.

The little girl returned.

Alone this time.

She looked at the drawing.

Then at me.

Then at the drawing.

"That's him."

Senna said, "Good."

The girl pointed at my left side.

"Where leg?"

"Not there," I said.

She looked.

"Why?"

There it was.

Children were efficient.

"Lost it."

"Where?"

I thought about giving her the truthful answer.

Which version?

First-life reincarnation complications were irrelevant.

Current-life injury had an answer.

The answer was not a square conversation.

"Not somewhere I can go get it."

She considered.

"Okay."

Then she pointed at the old man's portrait.

"He dead?"

The old man removed his hat.

"No."

She jumped.

Senna laughed hard enough to smudge the edge of my page.

The girl ran three steps, realized she had dignity, and walked the rest.

The old man said, "Worth waking up."

Senna wiped her thumb on a cloth.

"Now you're smudged."

"Adds mystery."

"It adds charcoal."

She set the drawing aside.

I expected her to hand it to me.

She did not.

Good.

I also wanted it.

Bad.

"Do you keep them?" I asked.

"Most."

"Why?"

"Practice."

"Right."

She looked at me.

"You want it."

"No."

"You looked at it three times after I stopped showing you."

"Evidence inflation."

"What?"

"Nothing."

She picked up the page.

"One copper."

I stared at her.

She stared back.

The old man began laughing before either of us spoke.

"Absolutely not."

"Then I keep it."

"You said no business."

"Portraits when people pay enough to hate their noses."

"That is extortion."

"You approached my bench."

"I approached paper."

"You stayed."

"You drafted me without consent."

"You could leave."

"You are using my own arguments against me."

"I met you twenty minutes ago. There aren't many yet."

I looked at the page.

One copper.

My entire liquid fortune.

For charcoal Greg.

No.

Not even close.

The fact that I wanted it did not improve the arithmetic.

"Keep it."

She nodded once.

No bargaining.

No reduced price.

No pity gift.

She slid it under a flat stone with the others.

That was somehow worse.

I could still see half my right shoe.

"Cruel."

"Poor."

"Also true."

She picked up the charcoal again.

"Tomorrow it may be two copper."

"Tomorrow I may steal it."

"Then I draw wanted notices."

"With the hair?"

"Especially the hair."

I stood.

Carefully.

No road stiffness now.

Crutches planted.

Right foot under me.

Balance set.

Senna watched the movement.

Not clinically.

Not politely looking away.

Just drawing again.

"You're doing another."

"Leaving."

"I object."

"Leave faster."

I did.

At the corner I looked back.

She was drawing the old man again.

He had fallen asleep.

My page stayed under the stone.

I went home.

The bag smell had improved.

Lyssa was at the table trimming thread ends from something dark that was not the green vest.

I did not ask.

She looked at me.

"Hair."

"What about it?"

"Bad."

"This is becoming coordinated."

Cala looked over from the stove.

"Cut."

"No."

"Cut."

"I have received enough public criticism."

Lyssa smiled.

"Public?"

I told them about Senna.

Not every detail.

Artist.

Square.

Free drawing.

One copper if I wanted the seated portrait.

I had refused.

Lyssa stopped trimming.

"One copper?"

"Exactly."

"All money."

"Exactly."

"Good."

"I know."

"Show?"

"I did not buy it."

"Tomorrow."

"No."

She looked at me.

I knew that look.

"Do not go buy my face with your money."

"Why?"

"Because it is my bad financial decision to make."

"Then make."

"No."

"Then I can."

"No."

Cala said, "Who Senna?"

I described her.

Gray shirt.

Green skirt.

Charcoal cheek.

Paper all over the bench.

Cala nodded.

"Sign girl."

Of course Cala knew.

"She does signs?"

"Fish shop. Blue letters."

Senna had said signs.

Still irritating when the city confirmed people without me.

Lyssa said, "Good signs?"

Cala shrugged.

"Blue."

A rigorous review.

I cleaned the rest of the grease from the bag strap.

Lyssa finished her thread ends.

Nobody bought the portrait.

The next morning I passed the triangle on purpose.

This was not the same as returning for the portrait.

Intent mattered.

Senna was there.

Different shirt.

Same skirt.

No charcoal on cheek yet.

The old man was absent.

The bench held new pages.

My seated portrait was gone.

I stopped.

Senna looked up.

"Morning."

"Morning."

I looked at the papers.

Not there.

Good.

Fine.

Completely reasonable.

"Sold?" I asked.

"No."

"Then where?"

She pointed behind her.

A cord had been strung between two posts.

Six studies hung from clips.

Boy with basket.

Sleeping old man.

Woman with onions.

Dog.

Me.

A hand holding a cup.

My portrait moved slightly in the breeze.

Public.

I stared.

Senna said, "Practice display."

"You have weaponized not buying it."

"No."

"People can see it."

"That is generally why drawings are displayed."

A man walking past glanced at the cord.

Then at me.

Then back at the drawing.

Recognition took one second.

He smiled.

"That's you."

I looked at Senna.

She was already drawing his hand.

"Apparently," I said.

He kept walking.

No soldier.

No hero.

No famous Greg.

Just a man who had seen a face on a cord and then found the same face standing beside it.

I looked at the portrait again.

The charcoal version of me looked like he had nowhere urgent to be.

That might have been the strangest part.

No job.

No test.

No debt hour.

No road seat.

No stage entrance.

No thing to solve before somebody died.

Just sitting in a square because I had stopped walking.

I almost explained that to myself.

Then I remembered I hated when stories did that.

So I stopped.

Senna looked up.

"You posing again?"

"No."

"Good."

I left before my face could become inventory.

At home, the bag no longer smelled like a tannery had died in it.

I put the narrow scrap and wrapped charcoal back inside.

The one copper remained upstairs.

The portrait remained in the square.

For once, both felt like they were exactly where they belonged.
